# 2:286: 42 questions

## 2:286/q01 What limit does God place on obligation?  [لَا يُكَلِّفُ ٱللَّهُ نَفْسًا إِلَّا وُسْعَهَا · interpretation]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q01/p1 God requires only what a person can perform. — FARRA: وسعها is a noun meaning capacity; its vocalic pattern parallels the alternatives in وجد and جهد. MUSNAD-AHMAD reporting أبو هريرة: The report glosses the capacity verse as assigning the soul the good it earns and charging it with the evil it acquires. TUSTARI: A soul's “capacity” means its strength or ability. TAB-FULL: God burdens a soul only with what lies within its capacity, imposing no constriction or excessive hardship in religious duties. TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari distinguishes forgetfulness caused by neglect from forgetfulness caused by incapacity or weak memory. TAB-FULL reporting الضحاك: Al-Dahhak says it asks not to be burdened with deeds beyond their capacity. ZAJJAJ: God imposes no obligation, such as fasting, prayer, or charity, beyond a person's capacity. IBNABIHATIM reporting سفيان: Sufyān applies the capacity limit to spending: only what one can afford. IBNABIHATIM reporting سفيان الثوري: Sufyān al-Thawrī interprets the capacity limit as performance of obligatory duties. THALABI reporting بعضهم: One interpretation holds that God accounts for settled convictions and intentions, while passing thoughts without resolve are exempt under the capacity verse; the oath verse supplies a parallel. JISHUMI: Ṭāqa means strength or capacity. JISHUMI: God has not imposed commands and prohibitions beyond people’s capacity, removing their excuse. JISHUMI reporting أبو مسلم: Alternatively, it begins a new divine statement to the Messenger and believers, clarifying that earlier commands and prohibitions did not exceed capacity. BAGHAWI-FULL: The petition asks not to be charged with deeds beyond their capacity. TABRISI reporting سفيان بن عيينة: Sufyān ibn ʿUyayna calls it a good interpretation that God does not impose a person's full limit of capacity, which would exhaust their effort. TABRISI: A third explanation treats this as devotional petition, though God does not impose on anyone a burden beyond their capacity. RAZI-FULL: If the believers speak the clause, it follows their “we hear and obey”: since God mercifully requires only what is within capacity, they must respond with obedience as servants. RAZI-FULL: Al-Rāzī ends this dispute by asking God to show mercy to their incapacity and limited understanding and forgive their errors, affirming that they seek only truth. RAZI-FULL reporting بعض الناس: Some accept a distinction: kasb can be for oneself or another, while iktisāb is only what one acquires for oneself; one can be a breadwinner for one's family but not an acquirer for them. RAZI-FULL reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: Imran ibn Husayn reports that the Prophet instructed the ill to pray sitting, or on their side if unable; jurists read inability as severe hardship, not literal incapacity. No grading is stated. QURTUBI-FULL: This categorical statement says no bodily or inward act is imposed beyond a person’s capacity, understanding, and constitution, relieving the Muslims’ anxiety about thoughts. QURTUBI-FULL reporting أبو هريرة: Abu Hurayra’s quoted couplet says God burdens no soul beyond its capacity and a hand gives only what it has. BAYDAWI-FULL: God burdens a soul only with what its ability can contain, by grace and mercy; alternatively, He gives less than its utmost capacity, making the duty manageable and easy. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The apparent sense is a fresh divine report: God burdens people in inward and bodily acts only with what their capacity, faculties, and constitution can bear. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting قيل: An alternative makes these words part of the Messenger's and believers' speech: after saying “we hear and obey,” they reason that God asks only what they can bear. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting الضحاك: Al-Dahhak, with Ibn Zayd, interprets the prayer as asking not to be burdened with deeds beyond one's capacity. IBNKATHIR-FULL reporting جابر: Jabir reports that after this verse was revealed, Gabriel told the Prophet that God had praised him and his community and invited him to ask; the Prophet then asked for the closing supplication beginning, 'God does not burden a soul beyond its capacity.' BURHAN: The author cites the verse's concise statement of capacity, responsibility, and earned reward as an example of eloquent concision. BIQAI-FULL: Ṭāqa means ability or capacity; the request asks not to be made to bear what they cannot manage. ALUSI-FULL: God's practice is to impose only what a person can bear, indeed sometimes less: five daily prayers despite capacity for more, and Ramadan despite capacity for additional fasting. ALUSI-FULL reporting قيل: An alternative makes both clauses part of believers' separate utterances, praising them for gratitude that duties fit their capacity and that good benefits them while evil harms them, not God; Al-Alusi calls this remote in one respect and close in another. ELMALILI: He reads the clause as showing the benefit of obligatory duties and urging believers to spend capacity beyond them on voluntary worship and good works, a recommendation called nadb. ELMALILI reporting الله: A qudsi hadith cited here says the believing servant continues drawing near to God through supererogatory acts, supporting the exhortation to spend surplus capacity in good works. ELMALILI: A second construal says properly directed effort earns benefit, while blind striving after harm costs the person; capacity-calibrated divine duties guide people toward benefit and away from loss. ELMALILI: A person who fails to learn religious and legal duties, or fails to review them after learning them, is not excused for later forgetting; some causes of forgetfulness are within human capacity to prevent. ELMALILI: “Pardon us” asks God to erase the effects of sins. People pledge sincere obedience but still fall short, cannot fully spend the God-given capacity and will in His path, and therefore ask through grace rather than earned merit; though deeds remain in divine knowledge, God can remove their consequences. ELMALILI: An obligation beyond given capacity would be torment rather than a duty; God appoints such torment as just punishment for refusing His law, as in the command to enter Hell at 40:76, with worldly examples among lawbreaking peoples and individuals. ELMALILI: The supplications that follow are connected to the wisdom that duties are proportionate to capacity and direct people away from harm. ELMALILI: God's obligations leave capacity beyond what is required, allowing rest and voluntary good deeds after duties; the five daily prayers do not exhaust a person's ability to act. ELMALILI: The same capacity principle governs how communal obligations fall on individuals, and the total duties assigned to one person must not exceed that person's capacity. ELMALILI: Elmalılı summarizes the greatest principle of legislative wisdom as duties being proportionate to the capacity of the person charged. IBNASHUR-FULL: The clause is circumstantial from nafs and explains capacity: good benefits the soul and evil harms it, marked respectively by li and ʿala. TABATABAI: Wasʿ means capacity; it first denotes spatial breadth, then metaphorically the capacity that contains a person’s possible acts. TABATABAI: Earning is evidence that a person acquires benefit or harm through acts that lie within their capacity. [44 notes: FARRA; MUSNAD-AHMAD(أبو هريرة); TUSTARI; TAB-FULL(الضحاك); ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(سفيان,سفيان الثوري); THALABI(بعضهم); JISHUMI(أبو مسلم); BAGHAWI-FULL; TABRISI(سفيان بن عيينة); RAZI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,بعض الناس); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو هريرة); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الضحاك,قيل); IBNKATHIR-FULL(جابر); BURHAN; BIQAI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL(قيل); ELMALILI(الله); IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI]
- 2:286/q01/p2 God requires only what a person can perform, with ease in religion. — TAB-FULL reporting ابن عباس: Ibn Abbas says the believers were given breadth in religion, citing the absence of hardship, God’s desire for ease, and the command to fear Him as able. IBNABIHATIM reporting ابن عباس: Ibn ʿAbbās says God eased believers' religion, linking this to no hardship (22:78), willing ease (2:185), and fear as much as possible (64:16). IBNABIHATIM reporting محمد بن كعب القرظي: Muḥammad ibn Kaʿb says people were not charged with work they could not bear; others are reported to agree. IBNATIYYA-FULL: This accords with verses saying God intends ease, places no hardship in religion, and requires mindfulness as far as possible. QURTUBI-FULL reporting ابن خويزمنداد: Ibn Khuwayzimandad says the verse’s apparent sense can rebut claims that a religious duty is unduly burdensome, in line with no hardship in religion and the report that religion is easy. NASAFI-FULL reporting شرح التأويلات: Obligation is limited to acts a person can perform, since responsibility concerns deeds within one’s ability. IBNKATHIR-FULL reporting رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم: A Prophetic report says he was sent with the upright, easy religion. DURR-FULL reporting ابن عباس: Ibn ʿAbbas says God eased religious duties for believers, linking this to no hardship in religion (22:78), God’s desire for ease (2:185), and fearing God as much as possible (64:16). [8 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن عباس); IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس,محمد بن كعب القرظي); IBNATIYYA-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(ابن خويزمنداد); NASAFI-FULL(شرح التأويلات); IBNKATHIR-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 2:286/q01/p3 Capacity here is manageable ability below a person's utmost exertion. — THALABI reporting ابن عباس: In another account, the verse concerns believers specifically: God eased religious duties and required only what they can do, as shown by verses affirming ease, no hardship, and duty according to capacity. QUSHAYRI: In perfect mercy, God sets people's charge at their capacity and far below that limit, out of gentleness and grace. WAHIDI-BASIT reporting ابن عباس: In another transmission, Ibn Abbas says God imposed only what believers can do, citing God's desire for ease, removal of hardship, and the command to fear Him as able. WAHIDI-BASIT: The best explanation is that capacity here is below full ability: God graciously burdens people with less than their maximum; Sufyan ibn Uyayna glosses it as ease, since a full-capacity burden would exhaust them. TABRISI: God commands or forbids only what a person can do; another explanation says He requires ease rather than hardship and does not exhaust capacity. ELMALILI: Some duties require not only enabling capacity without hardship but an additional, facilitative capacity grounded in greater ease. [6 notes: THALABI(ابن عباس); QUSHAYRI; WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن عباس); TABRISI; ELMALILI; WAHIDI-BASIT+]
- 2:286/q01/p4 The limit relieves believers of unchosen inward thoughts. — IBNKATHIR-FULL: God may reckon and question, but punishes only what a person can repel; involuntary inner whispers are not charged, and aversion to evil whispers is faith. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 2:286/q01/p5 God requires only what one can do. — MUQATIL: God requires only what one can do. [1 notes: MUQATIL]

## 2:286/q02 What does وُسْعَهَا mean?  [وُسْعَهَا · meaning]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q02/p1 God requires only what a person can perform. — IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB: Wusʿ means a person's capacity. TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari explains wusʿ as capacity, citing the expression “this matter has room for me,” like the terms juhd and wijd for hardship or ability. TAB-FULL reporting السدي: Al-Suddi glosses capacity as what a person can bear and says inner talk was beyond their capacity. IBNABIHATIM reporting عامر الشعبي: Al-Shaʿbī glosses capacity as what the soul has done for itself. MAWARDI-FULL: Wusʿ means a person's capacity. WAHIDI-BASIT reporting الفراء: Al-Farra says wusʿ is a noun like wajd and jahd, meaning what a person can accommodate without being squeezed or burdened. TABRISI: Wusʿ is less than ṭāqa: capacity is what a person can bear without being constrained; akhṭaʾnā means committing an error or sin. IBNJAWZI-ZAD reporting ابن عباس، قتادة: Wusʿ means capacity. RAZI-FULL reporting الفراء: Al-Farrāʾ treats wusʿ as a noun, like wajd and juhd. QURTUBI-FULL: Taklīf is an order that entails difficulty; Jawhari glosses takallaftu as undertaking a burdensome matter, and wusʿ as capacity or means. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Capacity is less than the utmost exertion or hardship: what lies within a person's ability. SAMIN-DURR: Wusʿahā is the second object of yukallifu. Ibn ʿAṭiyya says another object is omitted, but the Shaykh says that is true only at the verb's underlying valency, not in the surface syntax. Against: SAMIN-DURR reporting الشيخ: The Shaykh rejects an omitted object in the grammatical parse: wusʿahā itself is the second object, like dirhaman after an exceptive in “I gave Zayd only a dirham.” SAMIN-DURR: Wusʿ is what a person can contain or bear without constriction or exceeding capacity. BIQAI-FULL: Capacity is what a soul can bear and perform without incapacity: an intrinsically possible act within the person’s choice. DURR-FULL reporting سعيد بن جبير: Sa'id ibn Jubayr explains wusʿ as a soul's capacity. DURR-FULL reporting الضحاك: Al-Dahhak glosses wusʿ as what a person can bear. Against: TABRISI: The alternative that wusʿ means only what is permitted is mistaken: it makes the command circular, since every command itself permits the commanded act. [16 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB; TAB-FULL(السدي); IBNABIHATIM(عامر الشعبي); MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT(الفراء); TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن عباس، قتادة); RAZI-FULL(الفراء); QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; BIQAI-FULL; DURR-FULL(الضحاك,سعيد بن جبير); SAMIN-DURR(الشيخ)-; TABRISI-]
- 2:286/q02/p2 Capacity here is manageable ability below a person's utmost exertion. — THALABI reporting سفيان بن عيينة: Sufyan glosses capacity as ease rather than hardship: God did not require the soul’s full strength, since that would exhaust it; al-Tha'labi calls this a good view because capacity is below full ability. RAZI-FULL reporting بعضهم: Others define capacity as what a person can bear without constriction or hardship, below the utmost degree of exertion. QURTUBI-FULL: Wusʿ means capacity, and may also mean less than one’s full capacity. NASAFI-FULL reporting صاحب الكشاف: Capacity is what one can bear without constraint or embarrassment, short of the utmost exertion; people could pray, fast, or make pilgrimage more than required. BURSEVI: Taklīf imposes a burden; wusʿ is what a person can accommodate without hardship. God, through favor and mercy to this community, assigns only what fits their capacity, as in 2:185. ALUSI-FULL: Taklīf is imposing something burdensome; wusʿ is what human capacity can contain, or what is easy within capacity and below its limit. ELMALILI: The petition remains necessary because wusʿ, though broader than capacity, is also commonly used for capacity; a burden proportionate to capacity may still be severe, so the prayer asks God to make the apparent sense one of ease. [7 notes: RAZI-FULL(بعضهم); QURTUBI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL(صاحب الكشاف); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; THALABI(سفيان بن عيينة)+]
- 2:286/q02/p3 The limit relieves believers of unchosen inward thoughts. — BAGHAWI-FULL reporting ابن عباس وعطاء: Ibn Abbas, Ata, and most exegetes take wusʿ to refer to the inner thoughts mentioned in the preceding verse. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL(ابن عباس وعطاء)]

## 2:286/q03 How is وُسْعَهَا read and parsed?  [وُسْعَهَا · readings]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q03/p1 He reads وسعها with fatḥa on waw and kasra on sin as a verb, meaning God’s command encompassed the soul, or ‘what encompassed it’ with ما omitted. — THALABI reporting إبراهيم ابن أبي عبلة الشامي: He reads وسعها with fatḥa on waw and kasra on sin as a verb, meaning God’s command encompassed the soul, or ‘what encompassed it’ with ما omitted. [1 notes: THALABI(إبراهيم ابن أبي عبلة الشامي)]
- 2:286/q03/p2 All readers agree on the noun reading with ḍamma on wāw and sukūn on sīn. — JISHUMI reporting القراء: All readers agree on the noun reading with ḍamma on wāw and sukūn on sīn. [1 notes: JISHUMI(القراء)]
- 2:286/q03/p3 Ibrahim ibn Abi ʿAbla reads the word with fatḥa on wāw and kasra on sīn as a verb; the common reading is a verbal noun. — JISHUMI reporting إبراهيم بن أبي عبلة: Ibrahim ibn Abi ʿAbla reads the word with fatḥa on wāw and kasra on sīn as a verb; the common reading is a verbal noun. [1 notes: JISHUMI(إبراهيم بن أبي عبلة)]
- 2:286/q03/p4 Ibn Abi Abla reads wus'aha with open waw and kasra on the sin; Ibn Atiyya calls this a metathesis. — IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting ابن أبي عبلة: Ibn Abi Abla reads wus'aha with open waw and kasra on the sin; Ibn Atiyya calls this a metathesis. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن أبي عبلة)]

## 2:286/q04 How does يُكَلِّفُ govern its complements?  [يُكَلِّفُ ٱللَّهُ نَفْسًا إِلَّا وُسْعَهَا · grammar]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q04/p1 Takallafa means to impose a burden; takalluf is the verb, and kulfa is the noun. — WAHIDI-BASIT: Takallafa means to impose a burden; takalluf is the verb, and kulfa is the noun. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT]
- 2:286/q04/p2 Here taklīf means commanding or prohibiting a soul only to the extent it can perform; the easy is what is not hard. — JISHUMI: Here taklīf means commanding or prohibiting a soul only to the extent it can perform; the easy is what is not hard. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 2:286/q04/p3 Takallafa takes two objects, with one omitted here, understood as worship or another thing. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Takallafa takes two objects, with one omitted here, understood as worship or another thing. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]
- 2:286/q04/p4 The verb is used in the construction “I burdened him with a thing, so he undertook it”; taklīf is its verbal noun. — RAZI-FULL: The verb is used in the construction “I burdened him with a thing, so he undertook it”; taklīf is its verbal noun. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:286/q04/p5 Yukallifu takes two objects, with one omitted: the estimate is “an act of worship” or “something.”. — QURTUBI-FULL: Yukallifu takes two objects, with one omitted: the estimate is “an act of worship” or “something.” [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:286/q04/p6 The intensive form here makes the verb take a second object. — BAYDAWI-FULL: The intensive form here makes the verb take a second object. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 2:286/q04/p7 وسعها is the second object of يكلف; if Ibn ʿAṭiyya means that the verb originally has two objects, he is right, but no object is syntactically omitted here. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: وسعها is the second object of يكلف; if Ibn ʿAṭiyya means that the verb originally has two objects, he is right, but no object is syntactically omitted here. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:286/q04/p8 Ibn Abī ʿAbla reads وسعها as a past-tense verb; interpreters supply a relative ما, making the relative clause the second object of يكلف. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting ابن أبي عبلة: Ibn Abī ʿAbla reads وسعها as a past-tense verb; interpreters supply a relative ما, making the relative clause the second object of يكلف. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن أبي عبلة)]
- 2:286/q04/p9 A better parse leaves the second object understood from context and takes وسعها as a circumstantial clause: God imposes nothing except a thing while that soul can bear it. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: A better parse leaves the second object understood from context and takes وسعها as a circumstantial clause: God imposes nothing except a thing while that soul can bear it. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL+]
- 2:286/q04/p10 Ibn Abī ʿAblah reads the phrase as the past verb wasiʿahā; one parse supplies a deleted relative mā, making it the second object, a construction Basrans reject and Kufans allow, though the omission is. — SAMIN-DURR reporting ابن أبي عبلة: Ibn Abī ʿAblah reads the phrase as the past verb wasiʿahā; one parse supplies a deleted relative mā, making it the second object, a construction Basrans reject and Kufans allow, though the omission is weak. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR(ابن أبي عبلة)]
- 2:286/q04/p11 Another parse of Ibn Abī ʿAblah's reading deletes the second object for clarity and makes the clause an adjective of it; Ibn ʿAṭiyya calls the reading permissible through inversion, comparing 2:255 and 20:98. — SAMIN-DURR reporting ابن عطية: Another parse of Ibn Abī ʿAblah's reading deletes the second object for clarity and makes the clause an adjective of it; Ibn ʿAṭiyya calls the reading permissible through inversion, comparing 2:255 and 20:98. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR(ابن عطية)]
- 2:286/q04/p12 The doubled form here serves only to make the verb take a second object, not to intensify or multiply the action. — ALUSI-FULL: The doubled form here serves only to make the verb take a second object, not to intensify or multiply the action. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:286/q04/p13 Kesb can mean causing someone to gain and take two objects; in that sense iktisāb is the corresponding acquisition, gaining through another's action. — ELMALILI: Kesb can mean causing someone to gain and take two objects; in that sense iktisāb is the corresponding acquisition, gaining through another's action. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 2:286/q04/p14 Taklīf means laying a burden upon the will; every burden requires enabling power, and the duty must not strain that power. — ELMALILI: Taklīf means laying a burden upon the will; every burden requires enabling power, and the duty must not strain that power. [1 notes: ELMALILI]

## 2:286/q05 Can God impose an impossible duty?  [لَا يُكَلِّفُ ٱللَّهُ نَفْسًا إِلَّا وُسْعَهَا مَا لَا طَاقَةَ لَنَا بِهِۦ · theology]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q05/p1 An impossible duty is rationally possible, and some argue it has occurred. — ZAJJAJ: Imposing what is absolutely beyond capacity is impossible; if the phrase means burdensome, God may do as He wills. THALABI reporting الزجاج: Al-Zajjaj says burdening beyond ability is impossible if it means beyond one's capacity, but possible if it means what feels heavy; Israel's commanded self-killing was burdensome in the latter sense. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Those denying rational possibility of impossible duties read the verse as saying God neither does nor has imposed one; Ibn Atiyya says this is not explicit. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting أبو الحسن الأشعري وجماعة من المتكلمين: Al-Ashari and other theologians hold that imposing an impossible duty is rationally possible, not religiously forbidden, and signals certain punishment. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting فرقة: One group says impossible duty occurred in Abu Lahab's case: he was foretold as doomed to disbelief while commanded to believe. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting الطبري والزجاج: Al-Tabari and al-Zajjaj deny impossible duties: forgetting means omitted duties, error intentional disobedience, and the burden Israelite obligations and punishments. RAZI-FULL reporting المعتزلة: Their second premise is that ability precedes the act; otherwise an unbeliever commanded to believe would not be able to do so, making the command impossible. RAZI-FULL reporting أصحابنا: Al-Rāzī's Ashʿarite colleagues say rational proofs establish that impossible commands do occur, so this verse must be interpreted. RAZI-FULL: The second argument says acts depend on motives created by God; if motives instead came from the person, an infinite regress follows, while a determining motive makes the alternative impossible, yet the unbeliever is commanded to believe. RAZI-FULL: The third argument says a command issued when motives are equal requires contradictory equality and preference; if one motive prevails, the preferred is necessary and the other impossible. RAZI-FULL: The fifth argument says people do not know the detailed count of movements and rests in an act such as moving a finger; since they did not intend that precise count, they do not create it, and an undetermined preference is impossible. RAZI-FULL: A second answer defines command as notice that action brings reward and omission brings punishment; where an act is impossible, the statement is not a real command but notice of punishment and one's creation for Hell. RAZI-FULL: Punishing a forgetful person is not rationally impossible: fear of later punishment can sustain memory, though constant vigilance is difficult, making a request for pardon fitting. QURTUBI-FULL reporting أبو الحسن الأشعري وجماعة من المتكلمين: Al-Ashari and a group of theologians hold that imposing the impossible is rationally possible, as a sign that the person will be punished, without compromising doctrine. QURTUBI-FULL: The scholars agree that impossible obligations do not occur in revealed law, and this verse signals their absence. QURTUBI-FULL reporting فرقة: One group says the impossible occurred in Abu Lahab’s case: he was commanded to accept a whole law that included the prediction he would never believe. QURTUBI-FULL reporting فرقة: Another group says impossible obligation never occurred; a consensus is reported on this position. BAYDAWI-FULL: The plea concerns tribulation and punishment, or duties beyond human capacity; its wording indicates that imposing an impossible duty is possible, since the prayer seeks escape from it. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The verse resolves the believers' concern over thoughts in 2:284 and establishes that impossible demands are not legally imposed; he compares it with God's wish for ease in 2:185. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Some infer from this verse that God may impose a duty beyond human capacity. BIQAI-FULL: The supplications proceed from lighter requests to heavier ones, teaching that God did not punish forgetfulness or error, impose burdens, demand the impossible, or withhold pardon and mercy. BURSEVI: This shows that God has not actually imposed an impossible burden, not that such imposition is intrinsically impossible: its occurrence would make God's statement false, while an unrestricted negation does not establish impossibility. ALUSI-FULL: On either gloss of wusʿ, the verse shows that impossible burdens do not actually occur, but it does not prove that such imposition is logically impossible. ALUSI-FULL reporting قيل: Another view makes the request broader than the preceding one: the burden is pictured as impossible to bear for emphasis. ALUSI-FULL reporting قيل: A further view treats this as asking to avoid literally impossible obligations and argues that the request proves such obligations are possible; Al-Alusi judges the argument weak. TABATABAI: God may prevent wrongdoing by blocking its means or requiring precaution; burdens imposed as punishment for a person's prior bad choice are not intrinsically impossible obligations. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: Against theologians who deem an impossible divine burden permissible to protect unrestricted power and will, the author reads the verse as confirming that a wise God does not impose what exceeds capacity, in keeping with justice and a rational basis for accountability. [27 notes: ZAJJAJ; THALABI(الزجاج); IBNATIYYA-FULL(أبو الحسن الأشعري وجماعة من المتكلمين,الطبري والزجاج,فرقة); RAZI-FULL(أصحابنا,المعتزلة); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو الحسن الأشعري وجماعة من المتكلمين,فرقة); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(قيل); TABATABAI; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR+]
- 2:286/q05/p2 An intrinsically impossible duty cannot be imposed. — ZAJJAJ: In ordinary speech, saying one cannot bear speaking with someone means it is burdensome, not literally impossible. TABRISI: The verse disproves the Mujabbira's allowance of obligations beyond ability: capacity lies within human power, beyond which effort is exhausted. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: The verse means God does not impose what a person cannot do, since that would be impossible, like requiring a paralytic to run or a blind person to see. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: The prayer later in the verse asks not to be burdened with what cannot be borne; if impossible obligations could never be imposed, that request would be pointless. RAZI-FULL reporting المعتزلة: The Muʿtazila cite this verse and 22:78, 4:28, and the promise of ease to argue that God does not impose what a person cannot do. RAZI-FULL reporting أصحابنا: They argue that a genuinely impossible act cannot be requested in supplication, like combining opposites or making the eternal originated; abandoning this rule in particular cases needs specific evidence. RAZI-FULL: A difficult burden can be carried or imposed, while what is beyond capacity cannot be carried at all; therefore the verse uses “impose” for the impossible and both verbs for hardship. BAYDAWI-FULL: This supports the nonoccurrence of an impossible duty, but does not prove that such a duty is impossible in principle; compare 2:185. BURSEVI reporting التيسير: The request means not to impose duties that are difficult to sustain continually; it does not ask for the literally impossible, which cannot occur. ALUSI-FULL reporting قيل: An objection says the easier-than-capacity gloss concerns this community alone and so cannot establish a universal rule against impossible burdens; Al-Alusi says the verse in any case does not prove their impossibility. ALUSI-FULL reporting المحققين من أهل السنة والمعتزلة: An objection says the third view conflicts with the rational impossibility of charging beyond capacity accepted by leading Sunnis and Muʿtazilites, since exemption would not then be a continuing favor. TABATABAI: The plea concerns punishment for sins—unbearably hard duties, inflicted torment, or a scourge such as transformation—not an initial impossible duty, which reason excludes. [12 notes: ZAJJAJ; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL(أصحابنا,المعتزلة); BAYDAWI-FULL; BURSEVI(التيسير); ALUSI-FULL(المحققين من أهل السنة والمعتزلة,قيل); TABATABAI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD+]
- 2:286/q05/p3 Al-Ḥasan applies the verse to an unpayable vow: a person is not charged beyond capacity. — IBNABIHATIM reporting الحسن: Al-Ḥasan applies the verse to an unpayable vow: a person is not charged beyond capacity. [1 notes: IBNABIHATIM(الحسن)]
- 2:286/q05/p4 The plea not to be burdened with what is beyond capacity can mean not to be loaded with what is heavy and difficult, even if people are capable of bearing it. — FARISI-HUJJA: The plea not to be burdened with what is beyond capacity can mean not to be loaded with what is heavy and difficult, even if people are capable of bearing it. [1 notes: FARISI-HUJJA]
- 2:286/q05/p5 The Qadari objection is that if God decrees someone's disbelief yet commands faith, He has burdened that person with what is beyond his capacity. — WAHIDI-BASIT reporting القدرية: The Qadari objection is that if God decrees someone's disbelief yet commands faith, He has burdened that person with what is beyond his capacity. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(القدرية)]
- 2:286/q05/p6 The request not to be held accountable refutes determinism about human acts, since the request presupposes that people act. — JISHUMI: The request not to be held accountable refutes determinism about human acts, since the request presupposes that people act. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 2:286/q05/p7 The statement that God burdens a soul only within its capacity refutes the determinists’ account of ability. — JISHUMI: The statement that God burdens a soul only within its capacity refutes the determinists’ account of ability. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 2:286/q05/p8 The verse answers an implied request not to be burdened beyond capacity; wusʿ means what a person can bear without hardship. — BAGHAWI-FULL: The verse answers an implied request not to be burdened beyond capacity; wusʿ means what a person can bear without hardship. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL]
- 2:286/q05/p9 Sufyan ibn Uyayna glosses wusʿ as ease, not a burden beyond ability; the author calls this a good view because wusʿ is what lies below the limit of full capacity. — BAGHAWI-FULL reporting سفيان بن عيينة: Sufyan ibn Uyayna glosses wusʿ as ease, not a burden beyond ability; the author calls this a good view because wusʿ is what lies below the limit of full capacity. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL(سفيان بن عيينة)+]
- 2:286/q05/p10 The verse states God assigns no act of heart or body beyond capacity, comprehension, and constitution, relieving anxiety about passing thoughts. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: The verse states God assigns no act of heart or body beyond capacity, comprehension, and constitution, relieving anxiety about passing thoughts. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]
- 2:286/q05/p11 Impossible duties are not imposed in revealed law, though their rational possibility is distinct. — IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting فرقة: Another group says no impossible duty occurred; the threat that Abu Lahab enters the Fire means if he persists in disbelief. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(فرقة)]
- 2:286/q05/p12 Al-Dahhak and Ibn Zayd understand the plea as asking not to be burdened with acts beyond capacity. — IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting الضحاك وابن زيد: Al-Dahhak and Ibn Zayd understand the plea as asking not to be burdened with acts beyond capacity. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(الضحاك وابن زيد)]
- 2:286/q05/p13 Their first premise is that people create their own acts: if God created the commanded act, the person could neither produce it again nor resist God's power; if God did not, the person could. — RAZI-FULL reporting المعتزلة: Their first premise is that people create their own acts: if God created the commanded act, the person could neither produce it again nor resist God's power; if God did not, the person could not bring it about. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(المعتزلة)]
- 2:286/q05/p14 The fourth argument says Abū Lahab was commanded to believe even though God had announced that he would not believe, so he was required to believe that he would not believe. — RAZI-FULL: The fourth argument says Abū Lahab was commanded to believe even though God had announced that he would not believe, so he was required to believe that he would not believe. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:286/q05/p15 Al-Rāzī notes that the same interpretive method is routinely used by the Muʿtazila against anthropomorphic readings; thus the verse certainly admits interpretation even if its precise interpretation is left unspecified. — RAZI-FULL: Al-Rāzī notes that the same interpretive method is routinely used by the Muʿtazila against anthropomorphic readings; thus the verse certainly admits interpretation even if its precise interpretation is left unspecified. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:286/q05/p16 A group of early determinists says people are commanded while it remains unknown whether God knows they will die unbelievers; after such a death, one learns the obstacle was present and the condition for. — RAZI-FULL reporting طائفة من قدماء أهل الجبر: A group of early determinists says people are commanded while it remains unknown whether God knows they will die unbelievers; after such a death, one learns the obstacle was present and the condition for obligation had been absent during life. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(طائفة من قدماء أهل الجبر)]
- 2:286/q05/p17 The Muʿtazilites infer that people create their own acts: attributing good and evil to them would be invalid if God created those acts, as with involuntary traits. — RAZI-FULL reporting المعتزلة: The Muʿtazilites infer that people create their own acts: attributing good and evil to them would be invalid if God created those acts, as with involuntary traits. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(المعتزلة)]
- 2:286/q05/p18 The Qadi argues that if God creates human acts, obligation loses its purpose; on that view, asking for lighter duties is also pointless because God creates both burdens and ease without fatigue. — RAZI-FULL reporting القاضي: The Qadi argues that if God creates human acts, obligation loses its purpose; on that view, asking for lighter duties is also pointless because God creates both burdens and ease without fatigue. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(القاضي)]
- 2:286/q05/p19 The Muʿtazilites say different duties suit different people: Jews’ harsh temperaments required severe commands, whereas this community’s refined character made lighter duties beneficial. — RAZI-FULL reporting المعتزلة: The Muʿtazilites say different duties suit different people: Jews’ harsh temperaments required severe commands, whereas this community’s refined character made lighter duties beneficial. Against: RAZI-FULL: Al-Razi rejects the Muʿtazilite temperamental explanation as unsound, affirming that God acts as He wills and is not questioned, as stated at 21:23. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(المعتزلة); RAZI-FULL-]
- 2:286/q05/p20 Al-Razi’s associates argue that the prayer proves the rational permissibility of imposing an obligation beyond capacity, since it asks God to refrain from it. — RAZI-FULL reporting أصحابنا: Al-Razi’s associates argue that the prayer proves the rational permissibility of imposing an obligation beyond capacity, since it asks God to refrain from it. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(أصحابنا)]
- 2:286/q05/p21 The Muʿtazilites read “beyond our capacity” as an act that is extremely difficult, as in colloquial claims of being unable to look at someone or bear a task. — RAZI-FULL reporting المعتزلة: The Muʿtazilites read “beyond our capacity” as an act that is extremely difficult, as in colloquial claims of being unable to look at someone or bear a task. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(المعتزلة)]
- 2:286/q05/p22 The cited poem uses ṭāqa for what is difficult to bear, supporting the Muʿtazilite reading of the phrase as severe hardship. — RAZI-FULL reporting الشاعر: The cited poem uses ṭāqa for what is difficult to bear, supporting the Muʿtazilite reading of the phrase as severe hardship. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(الشاعر)]
- 2:286/q05/p23 The Muʿtazilites read “they could not hear” at 11:20 as meaning that hearing was difficult for them. — RAZI-FULL reporting المعتزلة: The Muʿtazilites read “they could not hear” at 11:20 as meaning that hearing was difficult for them. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(المعتزلة)]
- 2:286/q05/p24 Ibn Atiyya reads “he shall burn” as conditional on Abu Lahab dying in the predicted state. — QURTUBI-FULL reporting ابن عطية: Ibn Atiyya reads “he shall burn” as conditional on Abu Lahab dying in the predicted state. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عطية)]
- 2:286/q05/p25 The verse supports the scholars’ use of kasb and iktisab for human acts; the author rejects attributing creation to people as an innovating excess. — QURTUBI-FULL: The verse supports the scholars’ use of kasb and iktisab for human acts; the author rejects attributing creation to people as an innovating excess. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:286/q05/p26 The prayer implies that God could hold people accountable for forgetfulness or error, contrary to the Muʿtazila, since these can be avoided in some measure; otherwise the request is pointless. — NASAFI-FULL: The prayer implies that God could hold people accountable for forgetfulness or error, contrary to the Muʿtazila, since these can be avoided in some measure; otherwise the request is pointless. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 2:286/q05/p27 Ibn Abbas said Muslims shared Ibn Umar’s fear, then the assurance that God burdens no soul beyond capacity was revealed. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting ابن عباس: Ibn Abbas said Muslims shared Ibn Umar’s fear, then the assurance that God burdens no soul beyond capacity was revealed. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 2:286/q05/p28 The request for anything beyond capacity is broader than the earlier request for a burden like that imposed on predecessors; the intensified verb also underscores the request. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The request for anything beyond capacity is broader than the earlier request for a burden like that imposed on predecessors; the intensified verb also underscores the request. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:286/q05/p29 Without duties people would be idle, while demands beyond capacity would waste effort; either result would mean God did not will His servants’ good, contrary to the mercy of the Compassionate. — ELMALILI: Without duties people would be idle, while demands beyond capacity would waste effort; either result would mean God did not will His servants’ good, contrary to the mercy of the Compassionate. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 2:286/q05/p30 The verse rules out religious obligations beyond customary human ability, without making claims about capacity under the hidden decree; Ibn Ashur uses it against the Jabriyya. — IBNASHUR-FULL: The verse rules out religious obligations beyond customary human ability, without making claims about capacity under the hidden decree; Ibn Ashur uses it against the Jabriyya. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:286/q05/p31 Alternatively, God may be teaching the believers this prayer, as He taught the praise in al-Fatiha, after establishing that He does not impose beyond capacity. — IBNASHUR-FULL: Alternatively, God may be teaching the believers this prayer, as He taught the praise in al-Fatiha, after establishing that He does not impose beyond capacity. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:286/q05/p32 The prayer seeks continued relief from burdens beyond capacity, so it will not be withdrawn in anger as earlier permitted foods were withheld from the Jews. — IBNASHUR-FULL: The prayer seeks continued relief from burdens beyond capacity, so it will not be withdrawn in anger as earlier permitted foods were withheld from the Jews. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:286/q05/p33 The verse states God’s customary practice: He does not require belief beyond understanding or obedience beyond human power, in keeping with rational beings’ practice. — TABATABAI: The verse states God’s customary practice: He does not require belief beyond understanding or obedience beyond human power, in keeping with rational beings’ practice. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 2:286/q05/p34 It connects forward by distinguishing the petitions about error, forgetfulness, and hardship from obligations beyond capacity. — TABATABAI: It connects forward by distinguishing the petitions about error, forgetfulness, and hardship from obligations beyond capacity. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 2:286/q05/p35 Imposed hardship beyond capacity is punishment for rebellion, not a legal duty; error and forgetfulness are involuntary themselves but can arise through voluntary precursors. — TABATABAI: Imposed hardship beyond capacity is punishment for rebellion, not a legal duty; error and forgetfulness are involuntary themselves but can arise through voluntary precursors. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 2:286/q05/p36 The author surveys Qur'anic uses of kasaba and iktasaba for deeds and their consequences, including spending from earnings, Abu Lahab's earnings, and inheritance shares. — BINTSHATI-IJAZ: The author surveys Qur'anic uses of kasaba and iktasaba for deeds and their consequences, including spending from earnings, Abu Lahab's earnings, and inheritance shares. [1 notes: BINTSHATI-IJAZ]
- 2:286/q05/p37 God creates humans’ universal will and power, usable for good or evil; their particular willing and power is its direction toward one, resolved act, called kasb, which affects the act’s moral quality rather than. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR reporting Mâtürîdî mezhebi: God creates humans’ universal will and power, usable for good or evil; their particular willing and power is its direction toward one, resolved act, called kasb, which affects the act’s moral quality rather than its creation, grounding responsibility. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Mâtürîdî mezhebi)+]
- 2:286/q05/p38 The Jabriyya deny that people possess a will or power of their own in the acts and behaviors they perform. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR reporting Cebriyye ekolü: The Jabriyya deny that people possess a will or power of their own in the acts and behaviors they perform. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Cebriyye ekolü)]
- 2:286/q05/p39 The Muʿtazila say human acts independently result from human will and power, and that a person creates their act from nothing. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR reporting Mu‘tezile mezhebi: The Muʿtazila say human acts independently result from human will and power, and that a person creates their act from nothing. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Mu‘tezile mezhebi)]
- 2:286/q05/p40 The principle that God tasks no soul beyond capacity also appears in 2:233, 6:152, 7:42, and 23:62. — STUDYQURAN: The principle that God tasks no soul beyond capacity also appears in 2:233, 6:152, 7:42, and 23:62. [1 notes: STUDYQURAN]
- 2:286/q05/p41 Ibn Atiyya compares this assignment to a hadith's command that a sculptor make a hair. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Ibn Atiyya compares this assignment to a hadith's command that a sculptor make a hair. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]
- 2:286/q05/p42 Impossibility may be logical, customary, physically fatal, or due to being occupied with another task; the last is figurative usage. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Impossibility may be logical, customary, physically fatal, or due to being occupied with another task; the last is figurative usage. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]
- 2:286/q05/p43 It is possible to impose a task a person cannot fulfill because of absent means, such as faith on someone eternally known not to believe; this verse concerns impossibility in itself. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD: It is possible to impose a task a person cannot fulfill because of absent means, such as faith on someone eternally known not to believe; this verse concerns impossibility in itself. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD]
- 2:286/q05/p44 Biqāʿī distinguishes actual assignment from what God may command: an act such as sleep, or a possible act known not to occur, is not assigned here even though such a command is conceptually permissible. — BIQAI-FULL: Biqāʿī distinguishes actual assignment from what God may command: an act such as sleep, or a possible act known not to occur, is not assigned here even though such a command is conceptually permissible. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:286/q05/p45 He illustrates impossibility with being ordered to hear using sight, occupy more than one place, or be born again to one’s parents; no such act can receive a command of obedience. — TABATABAI: He illustrates impossibility with being ordered to hear using sight, occupy more than one place, or be born again to one’s parents; no such act can receive a command of obedience. [1 notes: TABATABAI]

## 2:286/q06 What do the soul's gains and liabilities include?  [لَهَا مَا كَسَبَتْ وَعَلَيْهَا مَا ٱكْتَسَبَتْ · interpretation]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q06/p1 Good deeds and their reward benefit the soul; evil deeds and their liability weigh against it. — TUSTARI: What the soul earns is the reward of its good deeds. TUSTARI: What the soul acquires against itself is the burden of its sins. TAB-FULL reporting الشعبي: Al-Shaʿbi said the discussion reached this point and returned to the end of the verse, namely the assignment of what each soul earned or bore. TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari accepts that each soul is liable only for its own earning: liability requires doing what God forbade or leaving what He commanded. TAB-FULL: Each soul receives what it earned in good and bears what it acquired in evil. TAB-FULL reporting قتادة: Qatada glosses what a soul earns as good and what it bears as evil or wrongdoing. TAB-FULL reporting السدي: Al-Suddi likewise says the soul’s earnings are its good deeds and its burdens are its evil deeds. TAB-FULL reporting عبد الله بن عباس: Ibn Abbas identifies the soul’s earnings and burdens as the deeds of hand, foot, and tongue. TAB-FULL: Forgetting caused by inability to retain what one sincerely tried to learn is not a sin, so seeking forgiveness for it has no point. IBNABIHATIM reporting ابن عباس: Ibn ʿAbbās explains what the soul earned as deeds it performed. IBNABIHATIM reporting محمد بن كعب القرظي: Muḥammad b. Kaʿb glosses what the soul earns for itself as good deeds. THALABI: Good deeds belong to the soul with their reward, while its evil deeds and their burden are against it. MAWARDI-FULL: What a person earns is good deeds, while what is against them is sins. QUSHAYRI: The soul receives what it earns of good deeds. QUSHAYRI: What the soul acquires of evil lies against it, and repentance rescues it from that burden. WAHIDI-BASIT: Each person is accountable for their own earnings and is not punished for another person's sin. WAHIDI-WAJIZ: What one earns is obedience; what one bears is sin, and nobody is charged with another's sin. JISHUMI: Each soul receives the reward for what it has earned through good and righteous deeds. BAGHAWI-FULL: What a person earns of good is for that person as reward and recompense. BAGHAWI-FULL: What a person acquires of evil is against that person, carrying its burden. KASHSHAF-FULL: Good deeds benefit the soul and evil deeds harm it; no one bears another’s sin or receives reward for another’s obedience. KASHSHAF-FULL: The intensified form for evil signals effort: the soul desires and is drawn toward evil, so works harder to acquire it; good uses the unmarked form. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting السدي وجماعة من المفسرين: Al-Suddi and other exegetes say what one earns for oneself is good deeds and what lies against one is bad deeds; thoughts are not acquired. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Good deeds follow God's path without strain, while sins require the effort of crossing His prohibition; Ibn Atiyya prefers this as the reason for differing forms. TABRISI: A person receives the reward of obedience and bears the consequence of sins; both may be called earnings because they result from one's acts. TABRISI reporting عبد الله بن مسعود: Ibn Masʿūd says that when a man learned Sūrat al-Baqara, he became earnest and respected among the community. RAZI-FULL reporting صاحب الكشاف: Al-Kashshāf says good is called kasb and evil iktisāb because evil is desired by the commanding soul, which works harder to acquire it, whereas the good term does not imply such exertion. RAZI-FULL reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: A Prophetic report says each person has a stronger claim to their earnings than their parents, children, or other people; al-Razi gives no grading. QURTUBI-FULL: Passing thoughts and similar things are not a person’s acquired deeds. QURTUBI-FULL reporting السدي وجماعة المفسرين: Al-Suddi and, according to Ibn Atiyya, the exegetes agree that what a person earns for themselves is good deeds and what is against them is evil deeds. QURTUBI-FULL: Good deeds accrue to a person and evil deeds count against them. BAYDAWI-FULL: A soul receives the good it has earned, while bearing the evil it has acquired; neither another’s obedience nor disobedience benefits or harms it. NASAFI-FULL: Mercy may mean making the scales of our deeds heavy despite our having no good deeds to offer. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting السدي، وجماعة المفسرين: Al-Suddī and the commentators unanimously gloss what a soul gains as good deeds and what it acquires as sins; fleeting thoughts are not human acquisitions. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting الزمخشري: Al-Zamakhsharī says good benefits the soul and evil harms it: no one bears another's sin or receives another's reward. SAMIN-DURR: The author prefers a distinction: good is earned without strain along God's prescribed way, while sin requires the intensified effort of breaking His prohibition. SAMIN-DURR: The sūra contains many branches of learning; most have already been indicated, and an intelligent reader can infer what is suggested without explicit detail. IBNKATHIR-FULL: What a person earns is good and what is charged against them is evil, in deeds that fall within moral obligation. ITQAN: The form اكتسبت suggests effort and excess in the clause about evil deeds, which weigh more heavily. BURSEVI: The soul alone receives the reward for all the good it has earned and bears the punishment for all the evil it has acquired; no one else independently shares either account. ELMALILI: The first plea asks that the community not be punished in this world or the next for unintentional omission or error: despite renewed resolve, generic liability for what one acquires could include a forgotten duty or unintended prohibited act. ELMALILI: A third construal treats gains made for oneself and others as truly in one’s own favor, while selfish gain driven by appetite harms the self, since even earning depends on other people; divine duty reconciles self-interest and altruism. ELMALILI: A fourth construal says one’s own earnings secure rights and benefit, whereas living on another’s earnings is against oneself; duties protect people from dependence and secure rights through charity and fair exchange, while forbidding begging, usury, and aggression. ELMALILI reporting Râgıb: Rāghib distinguishes kesb, which can benefit oneself or others, from iktisāb, which benefits only oneself; every iktisāb is kesb, but not every kesb is iktisāb, illustrated by forms for cooking, roasting, and baking. ELMALILI: These distinctions associate iktisāb with desire and kesb with wisdom, and allow the verse's paired terms to convey multiple meanings. ELMALILI: On one reading, each person's good deeds and their reward benefit that person, while their sins and the burden and punishment they incur harm that same person; 41:46 and 45:15 support the point. ELMALILI: Divine commands require good deeds, while prohibitions forbid evil; performing or abandoning each kind brings benefit or harm respectively to the person charged. ELMALILI: Al-Qāmūs glosses kesb, iktisāb, and tekassub as seeking provision or something beneficial even without obtaining it; in Turkish, kesb here is working or exerting power. IBNASHUR-FULL: Souls that seek good and salvation must expose themselves to one of the two paths after reflection; each bears what it has earned. IBNASHUR-FULL reporting الزمخشري: Al-Kashshaf says iktasaba signals exertion because the soul desires evil and works to obtain it. Against: IBNASHUR-FULL reporting بعض المفسرين: Some exegetes distinguish kasb as acquiring good from iktisab as acquiring evil, but Ibn Ashur rejects this as inaccurate and cites Quranic usage of kasaba for evil. Against: IBNASHUR-FULL: Believers prayed this before learning that God had lifted such accountability, so there is no need to follow al-Kashshaf's reading of forgetfulness and error as negligence that causes them. BINTSHATI-IJAZ reporting الفراء: Al-Farra defines iqtiraf as earning and cites the Arab expression for someone going out to earn for his family. BINTSHATI-IJAZ reporting أبو مسحل الأعرابي: Lexicographers connect qarf and iqtiraf with earning; Abu Mishal records six further verb pairs with the same meaning. BINTSHATI-IJAZ reporting أبو عبيدة: Abu Ubayda glosses iqtiraf in 6:120 as accusation or imputation, citing Ru'ba's line about the accusation of lying; the segment leaves the comparison with iktisab open. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: The author identifies kesb as the verse’s explicit term for human contribution to action, meaning to acquire, earn, or deserve; the aphorism says the servant earns while God creates. STUDYQURAN reporting R, Ṭ: What a soul earns adds to its merit; sins it perpetrates are held against it. ZAJJAJ: No one is held accountable for another's sin, as stated by the verse that no bearer bears another's burden. JISHUMI: Each soul bears the burden for the sins it has committed. RAZI-FULL reporting كثير من المتكلمين: Many theologians use the verse to argue that God does not punish children for their parents’ sins, as also stated by 6:164. DURR-FULL reporting ابن عباس: Ibn Abbas glosses what accrues to a soul for or against it as its deeds. ELMALILI: The benefits and harms of obedience or sin accrue to those charged, not to God. Against: IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting المهدوي وغيره: Al-Mahdawi and others say no one is held accountable for another's sin; Ibn Atiyya says the principle is true but not established by this verse. [60 notes: TUSTARI; TAB-FULL(السدي,الشعبي,عبد الله بن عباس,قتادة); IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس,محمد بن كعب القرظي); THALABI; MAWARDI-FULL; QUSHAYRI; WAHIDI-BASIT; WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL; KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(السدي وجماعة من المفسرين); TABRISI(عبد الله بن مسعود); RAZI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,صاحب الكشاف,كثير من المتكلمين); QURTUBI-FULL(السدي وجماعة المفسرين); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري,السدي، وجماعة المفسرين); SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR-FULL; ITQAN; BURSEVI; ELMALILI(Râgıb); IBNASHUR-FULL(الزمخشري); BINTSHATI-IJAZ(أبو عبيدة,أبو مسحل الأعرابي,الفراء); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; STUDYQURAN(R, Ṭ); ZAJJAJ; DURR-FULL(ابن عباس); IBNATIYYA-FULL+; SAMIN-DURR+; IBNASHUR-FULL(بعض المفسرين)-; IBNATIYYA-FULL(المهدوي وغيره)-]
- 2:286/q06/p2 A person gains good and bears sin. — MUQATIL: A person gains good and bears sin. [1 notes: MUQATIL]

## 2:286/q07 Why do كَسَبَتْ and ٱكْتَسَبَتْ differ?  [كَسَبَتْ ٱكْتَسَبَتْ · rhetoric]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q07/p1 The augmented form for evil suggests effort or intensity. — QURTUBI-FULL reporting ابن عطية: Ibn Atiyya says good deeds come without strain along God’s path, while evil requires forcefully breaching His prohibition; the two verb forms suit that distinction. NASAFI-FULL: Good is called kasb and evil iktisab because the iftiʿāl form suggests recoil: the soul recoils from evil and exerts itself for good. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting السجاوندي: Al-Sajāwandī explains the intensified form by saying evil clings to its doer, while good may be shared through guidance and intercession; iftiʿāl also suggests contraction, as the soul contracts into evil. BURHAN: The statement that every soul acquires only against itself concerns evil alone, by the occasion of revelation or by construing the verb as committing sin; the parallel verse states both good and evil, hence its contrast in verb forms. BIQAI-FULL reporting الحرالي: Al-Ḥarālī says the unaugmented verb denotes the least degree of earning, so even intending a good deed without doing it earns a good deed. ALUSI-FULL: The unaugmented kasabat suits good, which does not involve the same striving; iktasabat signals the soul's inclination to work at evil. MAWARDI-FULL: The forms may differ only in wording while sharing a meaning, or kasabat may be reserved for good and iktasabat for evil. IBNJAWZI-ZAD reporting قوم: One view distinguishes kasabat for a single occurrence and iktasabat for repeated acts over time. IBNJAWZI-ZAD reporting آخرون: Another view treats kasabat and iktasabat as two forms with the same meaning, as in 86:17. QURTUBI-FULL: The verb kasaba is repeated with different morphology to suit the pleasing pattern of the speech. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting ابن عطية: Ibn ʿAṭiyya says the verb is repeated in different forms simply to vary the style, as in the pairing of مهل and أمهل. BURHAN: The extra syllable in اكتسبت suits the burden and effort of sin, contrasting with the lighter form كسبت for good. BIQAI-FULL: The augmented iftiʿāl form for evil signals effortful engagement: sin is recorded only with firm resolve, and a person who intends evil but does not act is not charged. BURSEVI: The verse uses the more effortful form اكتسبت for evil because the desired act involves striving, unlike good; the iftiʿāl form conveys exertion. IBNASHUR-FULL: It is also said that iktasaba denotes effort when paired with kasaba, though the Quran never uses iktasabat for good. [15 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عطية); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عطية,السجاوندي); BURHAN; BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); ALUSI-FULL; MAWARDI-FULL; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(آخرون,قوم); BURSEVI; IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:286/q07/p2 The forms contrast the effort associated with good and evil. — BAYDAWI-FULL: The verse uses the lighter earning verb for good and the effortful form for evil because evil is desired by the soul and pursued more energetically, unlike good. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting الزمخشري: Al-Zamakhsharī explains the added effort in iktisāb for evil by the soul's desire and inclination toward it; good needs no marked effort, so its verb is unaugmented. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Abū Ḥayyān instead says good deeds are acquired without strain by following God's command, while sins require effort to cross the boundary of His prohibition, explaining the two verb forms. [3 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري)]
- 2:286/q07/p3 The two forms vary the wording without a fixed lexical difference. — SAMIN-DURR reporting ابن عطية: Ibn ʿAṭiyya first suggests the different forms simply vary the wording elegantly, as in 86:17; the author instead sees a semantic distinction in the effort involved in good and evil. SAMIN-DURR reporting أبو البقاء: Abū al-Baqāʾ reports both the view that the forms are synonymous and the view that iftaʿala signals effort, making sin weighty in its consequences. [2 notes: SAMIN-DURR(أبو البقاء,ابن عطية)]

## 2:286/q08 How do the earning verbs differ in meaning?  [كَسَبَتْ ٱكْتَسَبَتْ · meaning]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q08/p1 Kasb and iktisāb can have the same meaning. — WAHIDI-BASIT: The correct linguistic view is that kasb and iktisab are synonymous; Dhu al-Rumma uses both forms for earning in one line. RAZI-FULL reporting الواحدي: Al-Wāḥidī says the language treats kasb and iktisāb as synonyms; Dhū al-Rummah's verse and Qurʾānic uses interchange them in 74:38, 6:164, 2:81, and 33:58. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting أهل اللغة: The sounder linguistic view treats kasb and iktisāb as synonyms, as several Qur'anic usages attest. SAMIN-DURR reporting الواحدي: Al-Wāḥidī says the sound linguistic view treats kasb and iktisāb as equivalent, citing Dhū al-Rummah's line that uses both forms together. ELMALILI: If kesb and iktisāb are synonymous, the contrast between benefit and liability comes from their objects; the first mā is read as commanded good and the second as forbidden evil. STUDYQURAN: The verbs for earning and perpetrating share a root and can be synonymous depending on context. [6 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT; RAZI-FULL(الواحدي); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أهل اللغة); SAMIN-DURR(الواحدي); ELMALILI; STUDYQURAN]
- 2:286/q08/p2 Kasb can extend beyond oneself; iktisāb is more restricted or effortful. — WAHIDI-BASIT reporting بعضهم: Some say iktisab is narrower: kasb may benefit oneself or another, while iktisab is only for oneself; one can be a provider for family but not an iktisab-earner for them. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting منهم: Others distinguish them: earning may benefit oneself or another, whereas acquisition is only for oneself; Arabs say كاسب أهله but not مكتسب أهله, and a cited poem uses كاسبهم. SAMIN-DURR: Some distinguish the verbs: kasaba can mean earning for oneself or another, whereas iktasaba is narrower; Arabic says “he earns for his family” but not “he acquires his family,” and al-Ḥuṭayʾa's line uses kāsib. ELMALILI: Kesb can mean successfully attaining what one sought, while iktisāb means effort or action whether successful or not, making it broader in one respect and narrower in another; the terms may substitute for one another. [4 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(بعضهم); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(منهم); SAMIN-DURR; ELMALILI]

## 2:286/q09 How do لَهَا and عَلَيْهَا frame the deeds?  [لَهَا وَعَلَيْهَا · rhetoric]
- 2:286/q09/p1 The lām conveys benefit or possession; ʿalā conveys a burden or liability. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: Good deeds belong to the person because they bring joy and are counted as property; evil deeds are burdens and debts. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Good is framed as belonging to the soul, while evil is placed over it as a weight it bears, like having wealth but owing a debt. ALUSI-FULL: The pronoun in lahā refers to the generic soul; reward and punishment are understood by ellipsis, with good implied by the beneficial lām. ALUSI-FULL: The second mā denotes evil through the harmful ʿalā; iktasabat adds a sense of exertion because souls are drawn more intensely to acquiring evil. IBNJAWZI-ZAD reporting أبو بكر النقاش: Al-Naqqash says the wording “for her” points to good and “against her” points to evil. QURTUBI-FULL: Good is “for” a person as a joyful possession, while evil is “against” them as a heavy liability, like having property and owing debt. [6 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; ALUSI-FULL; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أبو بكر النقاش); QURTUBI-FULL]

## 2:286/q10 What legal and theological consequences follow from personal earning?  [لَهَا مَا كَسَبَتْ وَعَلَيْهَا مَا ٱكْتَسَبَتْ · law]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q10/p1 A guarantor’s payment does not transfer ownership of the guaranteed property: the original basis for ownership remains, while usurpation or liability does not remove it, as illustrated by an umm walad and a mudabbara. — RAZI-FULL: A guarantor’s payment does not transfer ownership of the guaranteed property: the original basis for ownership remains, while usurpation or liability does not remove it, as illustrated by an umm walad and a mudabbara. Against: RAZI-FULL: Those denying obligatory zakat cite the verse, but al-Razi answers that the specific proofs requiring zakat take precedence over this general text. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL; RAZI-FULL-]
- 2:286/q10/p2 A neighbor has no right of preemption: a co-owner has priority, the parties are not equal, neighborly harm is less, and a neighbor’s sale requires no partition. — RAZI-FULL: A neighbor has no right of preemption: a co-owner has priority, the parties are not equal, neighborly harm is less, and a neighbor’s sale requires no partition. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:286/q10/p3 Amputation does not end ownership or the duty to compensate; if stolen property still exists, it must be returned to its owner. — RAZI-FULL: Amputation does not end ownership or the duty to compensate; if stolen property still exists, it must be returned to its owner. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:286/q10/p4 Jurists infer that ownership ordinarily continues and remains attached to what a person has acquired. — RAZI-FULL reporting الفقهاء: Jurists infer that ownership ordinarily continues and remains attached to what a person has acquired. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(الفقهاء)]
- 2:286/q10/p5 Al-Kiya al-Tabari argues that killing by a heavy object, strangling, or drowning entails retaliation or blood money on the killer, not the killer’s kin; a father’s exemption does not free a co-killer. — QURTUBI-FULL reporting الكيا الطبري: Al-Kiya al-Tabari argues that killing by a heavy object, strangling, or drowning entails retaliation or blood money on the killer, not the killer’s kin; a father’s exemption does not free a co-killer. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(الكيا الطبري)]
- 2:286/q10/p6 Ibn al-Arabi says the verse supports retaliation against a co-killer with a father, against Abu Hanifa, and against a co-killer with an accidental killer, against al-Shafiʿi and Abu Hanifa. — QURTUBI-FULL reporting القاضي أبو بكر بن العربي: Ibn al-Arabi says the verse supports retaliation against a co-killer with a father, against Abu Hanifa, and against a co-killer with an accidental killer, against al-Shafiʿi and Abu Hanifa. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(القاضي أبو بكر بن العربي)]
- 2:286/q10/p7 Effects of error or forgetfulness vary: financial liabilities, blood money, and obligatory prayers remain; retaliation and an utterance of unbelief lapse; fasting while forgetful and an inadvertent broken oath are disputed. — QURTUBI-FULL: Effects of error or forgetfulness vary: financial liabilities, blood money, and obligatory prayers remain; retaliation and an utterance of unbelief lapse; fasting while forgetful and an inadvertent broken oath are disputed. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:286/q10/p8 The lām in lahā conveys ownership and the pleasure of good; ʿalā in ʿalayhā conveys evil's burden weighing upon its bearer. — SAMIN-DURR: The lām in lahā conveys ownership and the pleasure of good; ʿalā in ʿalayhā conveys evil's burden weighing upon its bearer. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:286/q10/p9 Examples of Israelite burdens include killing oneself in repentance or retaliation, cutting impurity from clothing (some say the body), and giving a quarter of wealth as alms. — ALUSI-FULL: Examples of Israelite burdens include killing oneself in repentance or retaliation, cutting impurity from clothing (some say the body), and giving a quarter of wealth as alms. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:286/q10/p10 Individual obligations scale with differing capacities, which is justice and equality of rights: zakat is absent for the poor and varies by wealth while remaining at one rate, unlike a flat per-person levy. — ELMALILI: Individual obligations scale with differing capacities, which is justice and equality of rights: zakat is absent for the poor and varies by wealth while remaining at one rate, unlike a flat per-person levy. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 2:286/q10/p11 The excerpt also attributes the opaque gloss بياض to Muḥammad b. Kaʿb for the liability clause, as printed. — IBNABIHATIM reporting محمد بن كعب الرظي: The excerpt also attributes the opaque gloss بياض to Muḥammad b. Kaʿb for the liability clause, as printed. [1 notes: IBNABIHATIM(محمد بن كعب الرظي)]
- 2:286/q10/p12 Al-Razi calls this verse a major foundation for numerous branches of jurisprudence. — RAZI-FULL: Al-Razi calls this verse a major foundation for numerous branches of jurisprudence. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:286/q10/p13 When the imams call a human an actor, that attribution is purely metaphorical. — QURTUBI-FULL: When the imams call a human an actor, that attribution is purely metaphorical. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]

## 2:286/q11 Whose speech is the opening assurance and the prayer?  [رَبَّنَا · grammar]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q11/p1 The assurance or prayer continues the believers' quoted speech. — WAHIDI-BASIT reporting قيل: Another explanation treats it as reported speech: they say, Our Lord. JISHUMI: Alternatively, the prayer is quoted speech—“they say, Our Lord”—with rabbana as a vocative meaning “O our Lord.” TABRISI reporting الحسن: Al-Ḥasan understands the prayer as an instruction to say these words, teaching believers how to supplicate. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The supplication is implicitly introduced by “say”; prayer is the core of worship because the petitioner sees personal need and abasement and God's self-sufficiency and generosity, fittingly closing the sūra. ELMALILI: The petitions are taught as the close of the believers’ earlier prayers, without an explicit command to say them; their opening renews resolve to obey while fearing disobedience. TAB-FULL: The prayer is God’s instruction to believers about how to call upon Him and what to say. ZAJJAJ: God reports the prayer from the Prophet and believers and places it in scripture for later generations to pray. MAWARDI-FULL reporting الحسن: Al-Hasan says the verse instructs believers to say this prayer to God. JISHUMI: The statement may continue the believers’ speech: “We hear and obey; You have burdened us only with what we can bear”; the following prayer is part of their quoted words. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Rabbana la tu'akhidhna means: say this in your supplication. TABRISI: Another reading supplies they say, presenting the words as reported praise and supplication. IBNJAWZI-ZAD: This prayer is God's instruction to people to say these words. SAMIN-DURR: The sentence has no syntactic position, since it gives information; another analysis makes it an accusative supplement to “we hear and obey,” meaning they also said this. ELMALILI: Although the response is separated by a pause, it is phrased as if the believers themselves were voicing it: they would immediately accept and repeat the promised ease because it matches their own hopes. IBNASHUR-FULL: The supplication may be quoted from the believers after their assent in 2:285 as they turn to seek recompense and intimate conversation with God. [15 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(قيل); JISHUMI; TABRISI(الحسن); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; ELMALILI; TAB-FULL; ZAJJAJ; MAWARDI-FULL(الحسن); IBNATIYYA-FULL; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; IBNASHUR-FULL; SAMIN-DURR+]
- 2:286/q11/p2 The assurance is God's own statement; the prayer is taught to believers. — ELMALILI: He lists three parses: everything from “we heard and obeyed” to the surah’s end is governed by “they said”; the assurance in 2:286 is parenthetical within that speech; or an unexpressed “say” introduces the supplications. [1 notes: ELMALILI]

## 2:286/q12 Why is the prayer taught and repeated?  [رَبَّنَا · rhetoric]
- 2:286/q12/p1 The taught prayer expresses humility and seeks continued divine favor. — ZAJJAJ: He recommends memorizing this supplication and praying it often. FARISI-HUJJA: The taught prayer can request an assured divine act, as in the prayers for judgment and fulfillment of promises and the angels' intercession for believers. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Ibn Atiyya says Muadh's practice may be a Prophetic report, or sound analogy with al-Fatiha because both contain supplication. TABRISI: A third reading means accidentally failing an obligation or unintentionally doing a forbidden act; the plea expresses humility even though such acts are not punished. RAZI-FULL reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: A Prophetic report calls supplication the core of worship; al-Razi states no grading. RAZI-FULL reporting المعتزلة: Their third reply is that praying against an act does not establish that God could do it, just as prayers against unjust judgment, disgrace, disobedience, or loss of deeds do not establish those outcomes as possible. RAZI-FULL: The plural prayer seeks fuller acceptance: when people’s spiritual intentions and desires converge on one request, its realization is stronger. QURTUBI-FULL: The prayer’s wording teaches people how to supplicate. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Each petition begins Rabbana to invoke God as their nurturer and reformer and to acknowledge their servitude and need; final clauses omit it because they conclude earlier petitions. ITQAN reporting كعب: Ka'b says Muhammad received four verses not given to Moses: the passage beginning لله ما في السماوات and ending al-Baqara, plus Ayat al-Kursi; Moses instead received a supplication. ITQAN: The end of al-Baqara closes with supplication. ALUSI-FULL: The passage resumes the believers' supplications after explaining the wisdom of obligation; it may also complete the narration of their utterances. ELMALILI: Elmalılı closes by praying that God grant worshippers a great share of the promised response when they understand these supplications and follow what they require. IBNASHUR-FULL: Repeating the address to God between coordinated clauses, though unnecessary, displays humility and supplication. [14 notes: ZAJJAJ; FARISI-HUJJA; IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABRISI; RAZI-FULL(المعتزلة,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; ITQAN(كعب); ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL]

## 2:286/q13 What does تُؤَاخِذْنَا mean and why this form?  [لَا تُؤَاخِذْنَآ · meaning]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q13/p1 The plea asks God not to punish or call the speakers to account. — THALABI: تؤاخذنا means ‘do not punish us.’ WAHIDI-BASIT: La tuʾakhidhna means do not punish us. RAZI-FULL: “Do not take us to account” means “do not punish us.” RAZI-FULL: One explanation for the reciprocal form is that the forgetful person exposed himself to harm through his own action, making the punisher seem to assist in harming him. ALUSI-FULL: Muʾākhadha means punishment; the form fāʿala can carry the simple sense of fāʿala/afaʿala rather than reciprocal action. IBNASHUR-FULL: Muakhadha derives from taking in the sense of punishment, and the reciprocal form intensifies the plea not to punish for forgetfulness or error. [6 notes: THALABI; WAHIDI-BASIT; RAZI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:286/q13/p2 The reciprocal form pictures the sinner as contributing to his own punishment. — THALABI reporting أهل المعاني: Although the verb is in a reciprocal form, it describes one actor: the sinner has effectively opened a way for the punisher and thus shares in the taking to account. WAHIDI-BASIT: The reciprocal verb is used though punishment has one agent: the sinner exposes himself and opens the way through his own deed, as if helping the punisher seize him. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting قيل: Another explanation retains the reciprocal form because the sinner makes himself vulnerable to punishment through his deed, as though aiding his own injury. SAMIN-DURR: The reciprocal form is used for one action because the sinner exposes himself to punishment by his deed, as if helping the punisher act against him; it may also be a conventional reciprocal such as sāfartu. ALUSI-FULL reporting قيل: An alternative takes the reciprocal form literally: God holds the sinner accountable while the sinner, having no rescuer but God, appeals to Him as though demanding release; Al-Alusi calls this unsound without strained interpretation. [5 notes: THALABI(أهل المعاني); WAHIDI-BASIT; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قيل); SAMIN-DURR; ALUSI-FULL(قيل)]

## 2:286/q14 What does نَّسِينَا mean here?  [نَّسِينَآ · interpretation]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q14/p1 Forgetting is literal absence of recollection. — ABDURRAZZAQ reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: A report from the Prophet says God has excused this community for mistakes, forgetfulness, and what its members are compelled to do; no grading is given. ABDURRAZZAQ reporting قتادة: Qatāda says he was told that God has excused this community for forgetfulness and for thoughts that arise within them. TAB-FULL reporting قوم: Some held that asking not to be charged for forgetfulness or error is obedience and humility, not an actual request for pardon; al-Tabari says he will treat their view in a fuller work. TAB-FULL: The petition asks pardon for forgetting a required duty or mistakenly doing something forbidden without intending disobedience, through ignorance or error. TAB-FULL reporting ابن زيد: Ibn Zayd paraphrases the prayer as asking not to be charged for forgetting a duty or mistakenly committing a prohibition. TAB-FULL reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: Qatada relays the Prophet’s statement that God has excused this community for forgetfulness and for thoughts that occur within themselves. ZAJJAJ reporting النبي: A prophetic report says this community is pardoned for forgetfulness and inward thoughts. IBNABIHATIM reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: The Prophet says God has waived three things for his community: error, forgetfulness, and coercion. FARISI-HUJJA: نسينا may mean ordinary forgetfulness and أخطأنا an unintentional mistake; the prayer asks not to be punished for either, although believers know God does not punish them for these. FARISI-HUJJA reporting النبي: A transmitted Prophetic report says the community is absolved for error, forgetfulness, and what it is compelled to do; the author gives no grading. THALABI reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: Ibn Umar reports the Prophet saying that error, forgetfulness, and what people are coerced into have been lifted from his community. WAHIDI-BASIT: Even when religious law excuses forgetfulness, believers may still be taught to pray for pardon, as prophets and angels petition for things God will certainly grant. JISHUMI: The phrase may also ask God not to hold them accountable if they are exposed to forgetfulness. BAGHAWI-FULL reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: The Prophet said that error, forgetfulness, and coercion have been lifted from his community. KASHSHAF-FULL: The prayer asks that they not be taken to task for forgetfulness or error if either occurs through them. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Many scholars, including Ibn Atiyya, hold that the prayer concerns involuntary forgetfulness and unintentional error; he calls this correct. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting النبي: Qatada says he was told the Prophet stated that God excused his community for forgetfulness and error.
