RAZI-FULL: The first of two interpretations takes forgetfulness in its ordinary sense, as the opposite of remembering. RAZI-FULL reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: A Prophetic report says error, forgetfulness, and coercion have been lifted from the community; al-Razi gives no grading. RAZI-FULL: If forgetting and error mean intentionally doing wrong, the commanded prayer gives grave sinners grounds to hope for pardon: God instructs believers to ask not to be punished for such sins. RAZI-FULL reporting أصحابنا: Al-Razi’s Ashʿari associates argue that the verse supports the rational possibility of obligating what cannot be done: a forgetful person cannot avoid the act, yet asks God not to punish him for it. QURTUBI-FULL: The prayer asks that sin committed through forgetfulness or error be pardoned, as in the Prophetic report that these are lifted from the community. QURTUBI-FULL reporting النبي: The Prophet said that error, forgetfulness, and what people are coerced into have been lifted from his community; the author says this certainly lifts sin, while legal effects differ by case. QURTUBI-FULL: The account glosses “if we forgot” as ignorance and “or erred” as deliberate action, while also offering the reading of acting through forgetfulness and error. BAYDAWI-FULL reporting رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم: The Prophet’s saying that error and forgetfulness have been lifted from his community supports the promised pardon. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Forgetting is absence of recollection, and forgetfulness and error are excused from accountability. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting ابن عطية: Ibn Atiyya says many scholars correctly understand the prayer as asking pardon for prevailing forgetfulness and error from the intended course. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting قتادة: Qatada reports that the Prophet said God has pardoned his community for forgetfulness and error. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting قيل: One view finds here forgiveness for major sinners: the prayer would be improper if forgetfulness and error meant only unaccountable acts, so they signify deliberate sin, omission and commission respectively. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: God commands believers to ask not to be punished for sins committed in forgetfulness or error; this command supports that He grants what they request. IBNKATHIR-FULL reporting أم الدرداء: Ibn Abi Hatim transmits from Umm al-Darda that the Prophet said God overlooks three for his community: error, forgetfulness, and coercion. IBNKATHIR-FULL: Forgetfulness can mean leaving an obligation or committing a prohibition unintentionally; error means missing the sound legal form out of ignorance. IBNKATHIR-FULL reporting ابن عباس: A Prophetic report transmitted through Ibn Abbas says God lifted mistake, forgetfulness, and coercion from the community; Ibn Majah and Ibn Hibban narrate it, but Ahmad and Abu Hatim faulted it. BIQAI-FULL: Forgetting means doing what was forbidden without remembering; error means doing it while remembering but without intending evil. DURR-FULL reporting ابن عمر: The Prophet said God pardons his community for mistakes, forgetfulness, and actions done under compulsion. DURR-FULL reporting عقبة بن عامر: The Prophet said God has lifted from his community mistakes, forgetfulness, and actions done under compulsion. DURR-FULL reporting ابن عباس: The Prophet said God pardons his community for mistakes, forgetfulness, and actions done under compulsion. DURR-FULL reporting أبو ذر: The Prophet said God pardons his community for mistakes, forgetfulness, and actions done under compulsion. DURR-FULL reporting ثوبان: The Prophet said God pardons his community for mistakes, forgetfulness, and actions done under compulsion. DURR-FULL reporting أبو بكرة: The Prophet said God lifted from this community mistakes, forgetfulness, and things they are forced to do. DURR-FULL reporting الحسن: Al-Hasan transmits from the Prophet that this community is excused for errors, forgetfulness, and compulsion. DURR-FULL reporting الشعبي: Al-Shaʿbi attributes to the Prophet the report that God pardons three things: error, forgetfulness, and coercion. DURR-FULL reporting الحسن: Al-Hasan reports that God excuses a person for what he errs in, forgets, is compelled to do, or is overcome by. ALUSI-FULL reporting قيل: A third view means actual forgetfulness and error: they could rationally bring punishment, like poison can kill when taken inadvertently, but God promised to overlook them as grace. ALUSI-FULL reporting الطبراني: Al-Tabarani transmits a hadith that al-Nawawi grades ḥasan: the Prophet says his community is exempted for error, forgetfulness, and coercion. ELMALILI reporting رسول الله: Elmalılı relates the plea to the Prophetic hadith, “My community’s error and forgetfulness are lifted,” while preserving liability for harm to others. IBNASHUR-FULL: Forgetfulness and error mean the resulting deed or omission that God does not approve. IBNASHUR-FULL reporting النبي: A report recorded by Ibn Majah says error, forgetfulness, and compulsion were lifted from the Prophet's community; it also circulates with وضع, al-Nawawi graded it hasan, and Ahmad rejected it. TABATABAI: Although the Prophet is protected from error and forgetfulness, he relies on God's protection and may ask for what he cannot secure from himself, joining the community of believers. TABATABAI: The requested forgiveness is specific to sin through error or forgetfulness, unlike the absolute “Your forgiveness, our Lord” answering the believers' absolute assent; the requests are not redundant. STUDYQURAN: Moses’ plea about forgetting recalls the believers’ prayer in 2:286 not to be taken to task for forgetting or error. STUDYQURAN reporting IK: Forgetting or erring may include omissions such as missing canonical prayers and commission such as adultery. STUDYQURAN reporting the Prophet: A hadith says God absolved the Prophet's community of mistakes, forgetfulness, and acts done under compulsion. [53 notes: ABDURRAZZAQ(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,قتادة); TAB-FULL(ابن زيد,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,قوم); ZAJJAJ(النبي); IBNABIHATIM(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); FARISI-HUJJA(النبي); THALABI(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); WAHIDI-BASIT; JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(النبي); RAZI-FULL(أصحابنا,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); QURTUBI-FULL(النبي); BAYDAWI-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قتادة,قيل); IBNKATHIR-FULL(أم الدرداء,ابن عباس); BIQAI-FULL; DURR-FULL(أبو بكرة,أبو ذر,ابن عباس,ابن عمر,الحسن,الشعبي,ثوبان,عقبة بن عامر); ALUSI-FULL(الطبراني,قيل); ELMALILI(رسول الله); IBNASHUR-FULL(النبي); TABATABAI; STUDYQURAN(IK,the Prophet); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عطية)+; IBNATIYYA-FULL+]
- 2:286/q14/p2 Forgetting includes abandoning or neglecting an obligation. — TUSTARI: A person recognizes sins by guarding the heart's light and weighing everything allowed to enter or leave it. TUSTARI: A heart occupied with what does not concern it is immediately punished by neglect of what does; only those who know God recognize this. TAB-FULL: Neglectful forgetting is a failure to perform a command; the petition seeks pardon for it, except when the neglect amounts to unbelief. Al-Tabari cites Adam’s lapse and later neglect of prayer, fasting, or memorized Quran. TAB-FULL reporting ابن زيد: Ibn Zayd says people can obey commands and leave prohibitions only through God’s mercy, and no one is saved except by it. ZAJJAJ reporting قيل: An alternative gloss takes if we forget as if we omit, and or err as committing a sin. FARISI-HUJJA: When نسي means leave, attributing it to God is figurative: people leave obedience and God leaves them without mercy or rescue; the Qurʾan also uses ترك for leaving people alone. THALABI reporting بعضهم: Another view understands forgetting as abandoning or neglecting; al-Tha'labi prefers the literal sense, though he cites 9:67 for the alternative usage. THALABI reporting ابن زيد: Ibn Zayd applies forgetting to neglecting a required duty and error to doing something forbidden. MAWARDI-FULL: Nasiya can mean deliberately neglecting God's command or simply leaving it; Qutrub cites 'they forgot God, so He forgot them' for the latter sense. WAHIDI-BASIT: Here forgetting can mean abandoning an obligation; in 9:67, people abandon God's service and are left without reward. JISHUMI reporting الحسن: Hasan supplies an omitted imperative: “and say, Our Lord.” JISHUMI: Another reading of “we forgot” is that people abandoned something because of doubt or bad interpretation, like the verse saying they forgot God and He forgot them. BAGHAWI-FULL: Another interpretation takes forgetting to mean neglecting or abandoning God, as in the verse saying they forgot God and He forgot them. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting الطبري وغيره: Al-Tabari and others take forgetting as neglecting obedience and error as intentional sin; involuntary forgetfulness and error are already excused. TABRISI: One interpretation takes forgetting as abandoning obedience and error as committing sins, even deliberately; the prayer seeks forgiveness for neglected duties and wrongful acts. TABRISI: The author cites 9:67 and 2:44 for nasiya meaning abandonment, and an unnamed poet who uses nāsiyan for leaving off spear thrusts in battle. TABRISI reporting ابن عباس وقتادة ومجاهد والربيع والسدي: One explanation asks God not to impose a duty people cannot carry out or punish them for abandoning or violating it. IBNJAWZI-ZAD reporting ابن الأنباري: Ibn al-Anbari takes forgetting here as deliberate neglect, since involuntary forgetfulness is already exempt from sin; he likewise takes error here as intentional, though the word can also mean inadvertence. RAZI-FULL: Al-Razi counts four kinds of supplication: the first three begin with “Our Lord,” while the fourth omits that vocative. RAZI-FULL: Al-Razi answers that forgetfulness may be culpable when someone neglects precautions, but excusable when warning signs were absent or the person took reasonable care; he gives examples involving impurity, mistaken shooting, and forgetting memorized Qurʾan. RAZI-FULL: Although neglecting precautions may be culpable, the person is then punished for deliberately failing to take care, not for the lapse itself; the verse therefore still supports hope of pardon for grave sinners. RAZI-FULL: A second sense of forgetfulness is abandonment, supported by Qurʾanic usage at 20:115 and 9:67. RAZI-FULL: On the abandonment reading, error means doing an act on the basis of a mistaken interpretation. RAZI-FULL: The fourth prayer omits “Our Lord” because a call is needed at a distance; its omission signals that persistent entreaty brings the servant near to God. QURTUBI-FULL reporting قيل: A report says sincere raiders who leave home beating drums cast fear among unbelievers for a month, whether or not the enemy knows they have departed; the verses were revealed after the Prophet returned. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting الزمخشري: Zamakhshari likewise supplies an omitted verb, “we seek Your forgiveness and do not deny You,” making the clause declarative. Against: ABUHAYYAN-FULL: He finds the omitted-relative analysis weak because the deleted relative has no preceding relative to license its omission; Ḥassān's verse has a repeated من as its antecedent. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting قطرب، والطبري: Quṭrub and al-Ṭabarī gloss نسينا as “we left/omitted”; the source then begins their gloss of وأخطأنا. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting قيل: Some lapses are culpable when a person neglects precautions, such as forgetting known impurity in clothing after failing to take steps to remember. SAMIN-DURR: Mawlā is a mafʿal from wali/yali; here it is a verbal noun used for an agent, possibly with an omitted “possessor of our guardianship,” and it may also denote place or time. BURSEVI: This asks deliverance from unbearable punishments, after asking to avoid the onerous duties whose neglect could bring them; punishment is described as a load because those duties lead to it. BURSEVI reporting التيسير: The two requests are not repetitive: pardon leaves the person unpunished and erases the sin, while forgiveness hides it from view; one may pardon while still mentioning and exposing a deed. ALUSI-FULL reporting مفتي الديار الرومية: The fresh statement encourages observance and warns against neglect: each person's benefit from obedience and harm from disobedience return to that person, not to God. ALUSI-FULL reporting قيل: One view takes forgetting as abandoning obligations and error as committing prohibitions, even deliberately, since sins are errors opposed to correctness. IBNASHUR-FULL: The closing pleas omit another ربنا because it has already been repeated three times, and Arabs avoid repeating a word more than three times except for emphasis or alarm. IBNASHUR-FULL reporting مرة بن عداء الفقعسي: Ibn Ashur cites Murra ibn Adāʾ al-Faqʿasi's poem lamenting patrons who abandon their client as the days turn. [35 notes: TUSTARI; TAB-FULL(ابن زيد); ZAJJAJ(قيل); FARISI-HUJJA; THALABI(ابن زيد); MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT; JISHUMI(الحسن); BAGHAWI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(الطبري وغيره); TABRISI(ابن عباس وقتادة ومجاهد والربيع والسدي); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن الأنباري); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(قيل); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري,قطرب، والطبري,قيل); SAMIN-DURR; BURSEVI(التيسير); ALUSI-FULL(قيل,مفتي الديار الرومية); IBNASHUR-FULL(مرة بن عداء الفقعسي); THALABI(بعضهم)+; ABUHAYYAN-FULL-]
- 2:286/q14/p3 The plea concerns culpable negligence that causes forgetting. — TAB-FULL: Because this follows prayers for pardon over forgetfulness, error, and an unbearable covenant, Ṭabarī says the request for ease in religious duties best fits the context. JISHUMI reporting الأصم: Al-Asamm reads “if we forget” as ordinary absent-minded forgetfulness; the author rejects this because accidental forgetfulness is not charged or punishable. KASHSHAF-FULL: Because the God-fearing only lapse through forgetfulness or error, naming these in prayer disavows culpable causes and acknowledges their protection. KASHSHAF-FULL: Although forgetfulness and error are excused, they are named to mean the negligence and inattention that can cause them. TABRISI: A second reading asks pardon if people expose themselves to circumstances that cause forgetfulness or error about a command or duty. BAYDAWI-FULL: The prayer may ask pardon for negligence that caused forgetting or error, or for forgetting and error themselves; they do not rationally preclude accountability, like mistakenly taking poison can still cause death. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting الزمخشري: Zamakhshari permits taking forgetting and error literally as excused lapses, and also reads them as negligence and inattention that caused the lapse; God’s faithful servants seek continued protection from such lapses. BURSEVI: The plea seeks pardon for negligence and carelessness that lead to forgetfulness or error, since such causes fall within human responsibility. BURSEVI: The request implies that accountability for forgetfulness and error is possible because avoiding them is possible in some measure; otherwise the prayer would be meaningless. ALUSI-FULL reporting قيل: A second view means the prior negligence and inattention that usually cause forgetting or mistakes, and asks pardon for that antecedent shortcoming. ELMALILI: Forgetfulness and error fall into two kinds: some may excuse the person, while others do not, because a person can be responsible for failing to take reasonable precautions. ELMALILI: Someone who notices impurity on clothing but delays removing it and later forgets before praying is not excused, because the initial delay was negligent; someone who never noticed it is excused. ELMALILI: Some legal duties remain in error cases such as accidental killing, and forgetfulness or error does not remove compensation for harm to other people; the plea seeks freedom from punishment, not freedom from obligation, including for causes and consequences. TABATABAI: Because these three pleas follow the request not to be charged for forgetting or error, they concern sins arising from forgetfulness, error, and similar failings. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR reporting hadis: A hadith cited above gives glad tidings that God forgives the Muslim community’s lapses caused by forgetting or error, affirming that this supplication was accepted. [15 notes: JISHUMI(الأصم); KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI; BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(قيل); ELMALILI; TABATABAI; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(hadis); TAB-FULL+]
- 2:286/q14/p4 Forgetting may be literal, but failure to take precautions can be culpable. — RAZI-FULL reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: A report says the Prophet tied a thread around his finger when he wanted to remember a need; al-Razi uses it to show deliberate memory precautions. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]

## 2:286/q15 What does أَخْطَأْنَا mean here?  [أَخْطَأْنَا · interpretation]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q15/p1 Error includes failing to do the right act. — ABDURRAZZAQ reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: A report from the Prophet says God has excused this community for mistakes, forgetfulness, and what its members are compelled to do; no grading is given. TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari reads the anonymous poet’s خطئوا الصواب as meaning that they missed the right answer, illustrating his distinction between خطئ and أخطأ. TAB-FULL reporting قوم: Some held that asking not to be charged for forgetfulness or error is obedience and humility, not an actual request for pardon; al-Tabari says he will treat their view in a fuller work. TAB-FULL: Because this follows prayers for pardon over forgetfulness, error, and an unbearable covenant, Ṭabarī says the request for ease in religious duties best fits the context. ZAJJAJ reporting قيل: An alternative gloss takes if we forget as if we omit, and or err as committing a sin. IBNABIHATIM reporting ابن عباس: Ibn ʿAbbās explains the liability clause as referring to what a person has done. IBNABIHATIM reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: The Prophet says God has waived three things for his community: error, forgetfulness, and coercion. FARISI-HUJJA reporting النبي: A transmitted Prophetic report says the community is absolved for error, forgetfulness, and what it is compelled to do; the author gives no grading. THALABI reporting ابن زيد: Ibn Zayd applies forgetting to neglecting a required duty and error to doing something forbidden. THALABI reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: Ibn Umar reports the Prophet saying that error, forgetfulness, and what people are coerced into have been lifted from his community. THALABI reporting النظام: Al-Nazzam imagines separation as so terrible that it could shake mountains, and says punishment by separation would seem worse than the fire's heat. MAWARDI-FULL: Linguists distinguish akhta'a, which can apply to sinful or non-sinful error, from khati'a, which always entails sin; a cited verse of poetry illustrates the distinction. WAHIDI-WAJIZ: To err means to miss what is correct. JISHUMI: Akhaṭaʾa can refer to sin or something other than sin, while khaṭiʾa is used only for sin. JISHUMI: “Or we err” may mean committing a sin because of an ambiguity. BAGHAWI-FULL reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: The Prophet said that error, forgetfulness, and coercion have been lifted from his community. KASHSHAF-FULL: Because the God-fearing only lapse through forgetfulness or error, naming these in prayer disavows culpable causes and acknowledges their protection. KASHSHAF-FULL: The prayer asks that they not be taken to task for forgetfulness or error if either occurs through them. KASHSHAF-FULL: Although forgetfulness and error are excused, they are named to mean the negligence and inattention that can cause them. KASHSHAF-FULL: An unnamed authority disliked one way of referring to the sūra and said one should call it the sūra in which al-Baqara is mentioned. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting النبي: Qatada says he was told the Prophet stated that God excused his community for forgetfulness and error. TABRISI reporting أبو عبيدة: Abū ʿUbayda says akhṭaʾa and khaṭiʾa are both Arabic forms, but the former can be sinful or non-sinful error while the latter means sin alone. TABRISI: An unnamed poet uses khaṭiʾū for sinning against what is right, witnessing the sin-specific sense of khaṭiʾa. TABRISI: A second reading asks pardon if people expose themselves to circumstances that cause forgetfulness or error about a command or duty. TABRISI: The prayer asks God to pardon sins, cover errors, and show mercy through blessing in this world, pardon in the next, and admission to Paradise. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA: Linguistically, error is an act that occurs contrary to the actor’s intention. RAZI-FULL reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: A Prophetic report says error, forgetfulness, and coercion have been lifted from the community; al-Razi gives no grading. RAZI-FULL: At the close of his tafsir, Razi says he wrote seeking God's pleasure, asks acceptance for what is correct, and pardon for any error. QURTUBI-FULL reporting النبي: The Prophet said he was granted victory through terror cast over enemies as far as a month’s journey. QURTUBI-FULL: The prayer asks that sin committed through forgetfulness or error be pardoned, as in the Prophetic report that these are lifted from the community. QURTUBI-FULL reporting النبي: The Prophet said that error, forgetfulness, and what people are coerced into have been lifted from his community; the author says this certainly lifts sin, while legal effects differ by case. BAYDAWI-FULL reporting رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم: The Prophet’s saying that error and forgetfulness have been lifted from his community supports the promised pardon. NASAFI-FULL: Some disapproved of saying «I read al-Baqara» and preferred «I read the sūra in which al-Baqara is mentioned». ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting قطرب، والطبري: Quṭrub and al-Ṭabarī gloss نسينا as “we left/omitted”; the source then begins their gloss of وأخطأنا. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Forgetting is absence of recollection, and forgetfulness and error are excused from accountability. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting الطبري: Al-Tabari glosses the wording about error as “we intended,” while Qutrub says it means we missed the correct interpretation. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting الزمخشري: Zamakhshari permits taking forgetting and error literally as excused lapses, and also reads them as negligence and inattention that caused the lapse; God’s faithful servants seek continued protection from such lapses. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting ابن عطية: Ibn Atiyya says many scholars correctly understand the prayer as asking pardon for prevailing forgetfulness and error from the intended course. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting قتادة: Qatada reports that the Prophet said God has pardoned his community for forgetfulness and error. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: God commands believers to ask not to be punished for sins committed in forgetfulness or error; this command supports that He grants what they request. IBNKATHIR-FULL reporting أم الدرداء: Ibn Abi Hatim transmits from Umm al-Darda that the Prophet said God overlooks three for his community: error, forgetfulness, and coercion. IBNKATHIR-FULL reporting ابن عباس: A Prophetic report transmitted through Ibn Abbas says God lifted mistake, forgetfulness, and coercion from the community; Ibn Majah and Ibn Hibban narrate it, but Ahmad and Abu Hatim faulted it. BIQAI-FULL reporting الحرالي: Al-Ḥarālī connects the closing assurance to the believers’ request for the straight path: the sura describes its followers, Jews as those under wrath, and Christians as astray, warning seekers to avoid their errors and submit to God. BIQAI-FULL: They ask God not to treat them like an adversary interrogating a litigant over every small and large matter. BIQAI-FULL: Forgetting means doing what was forbidden without remembering; error means doing it while remembering but without intending evil. BIQAI-FULL reporting الحرالي: Al-Ḥarālī defines error as an unintended departure from the proper limit despite intending or wishing to be right. DURR-FULL reporting ابن عمر: The Prophet said God pardons his community for mistakes, forgetfulness, and actions done under compulsion. DURR-FULL reporting عقبة بن عامر: The Prophet said God has lifted from his community mistakes, forgetfulness, and actions done under compulsion. DURR-FULL reporting ابن عباس: The Prophet said God pardons his community for mistakes, forgetfulness, and actions done under compulsion. DURR-FULL reporting أبو ذر: The Prophet said God pardons his community for mistakes, forgetfulness, and actions done under compulsion. DURR-FULL reporting ثوبان: The Prophet said God pardons his community for mistakes, forgetfulness, and actions done under compulsion. DURR-FULL reporting أبو بكرة: The Prophet said God lifted from this community mistakes, forgetfulness, and things they are forced to do. DURR-FULL reporting الحسن: Al-Hasan transmits from the Prophet that this community is excused for errors, forgetfulness, and compulsion. DURR-FULL reporting الشعبي: Al-Shaʿbi attributes to the Prophet the report that God pardons three things: error, forgetfulness, and coercion. DURR-FULL reporting الحسن: Al-Hasan reports that God excuses a person for what he errs in, forgets, is compelled to do, or is overcome by. BURSEVI: The plea seeks pardon for negligence and carelessness that lead to forgetfulness or error, since such causes fall within human responsibility. BURSEVI: The request implies that accountability for forgetfulness and error is possible because avoiding them is possible in some measure; otherwise the prayer would be meaningless. ALUSI-FULL reporting قيل: Another alternative places pardon at death, forgiveness in the darkness of graves, and mercy amid the terrors of resurrection. ALUSI-FULL reporting قيل: A second view means the prior negligence and inattention that usually cause forgetting or mistakes, and asks pardon for that antecedent shortcoming. ALUSI-FULL reporting الطبراني: Al-Tabarani transmits a hadith that al-Nawawi grades ḥasan: the Prophet says his community is exempted for error, forgetfulness, and coercion. ELMALILI: Forgetfulness and error fall into two kinds: some may excuse the person, while others do not, because a person can be responsible for failing to take reasonable precautions. ELMALILI: Involuntary error does not change the intrinsic harm or unlawfulness of the resulting act, just as swallowed poison remains harmful or an accidental fall into a well remains dangerous; people must avoid such outcomes as far as possible. ELMALILI: Some legal duties remain in error cases such as accidental killing, and forgetfulness or error does not remove compensation for harm to other people; the plea seeks freedom from punishment, not freedom from obligation, including for causes and consequences. ELMALILI reporting رسول الله: Elmalılı relates the plea to the Prophetic hadith, “My community’s error and forgetfulness are lifted,” while preserving liability for harm to others. IBNASHUR-FULL: Ibn Ashur's preferred reading takes the assurance as God's own parenthetical speech among the believers' quoted words, displaying the fruit of faith and obedience and promising no undue religious hardship. IBNASHUR-FULL: Forgetfulness and error mean the resulting deed or omission that God does not approve. IBNASHUR-FULL reporting النبي: A report recorded by Ibn Majah says error, forgetfulness, and compulsion were lifted from the Prophet's community; it also circulates with وضع, al-Nawawi graded it hasan, and Ahmad rejected it. IBNASHUR-FULL: The report's meaning is sound for exemption from religious address, but liability for destruction and compensation remains; therefore the prayer can still ask not to be punished for an error. TABATABAI: Although the Prophet is protected from error and forgetfulness, he relies on God's protection and may ask for what he cannot secure from himself, joining the community of believers. TABATABAI: Because these three pleas follow the request not to be charged for forgetting or error, they concern sins arising from forgetfulness, error, and similar failings. TABATABAI: The requested forgiveness is specific to sin through error or forgetfulness, unlike the absolute “Your forgiveness, our Lord” answering the believers' absolute assent; the requests are not redundant. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR reporting hadis: A hadith cited above gives glad tidings that God forgives the Muslim community’s lapses caused by forgetting or error, affirming that this supplication was accepted. STUDYQURAN: Moses’ plea about forgetting recalls the believers’ prayer in 2:286 not to be taken to task for forgetting or error. STUDYQURAN reporting IK: Forgetting or erring may include omissions such as missing canonical prayers and commission such as adultery. STUDYQURAN reporting the Prophet: A hadith says God absolved the Prophet's community of mistakes, forgetfulness, and acts done under compulsion. [75 notes: ABDURRAZZAQ(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); TAB-FULL(قوم); ZAJJAJ(قيل); IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); FARISI-HUJJA(النبي); THALABI(ابن زيد,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,النظام); MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(النبي); TABRISI(أبو عبيدة); IBNJAWZI-NUZHA; RAZI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); QURTUBI-FULL(النبي); BAYDAWI-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري,الطبري,قتادة,قطرب، والطبري); IBNKATHIR-FULL(أم الدرداء,ابن عباس); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); DURR-FULL(أبو بكرة,أبو ذر,ابن عباس,ابن عمر,الحسن,الشعبي,ثوبان,عقبة بن عامر); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(الطبراني,قيل); ELMALILI(رسول الله); IBNASHUR-FULL(النبي); TABATABAI; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(hadis); STUDYQURAN(IK,the Prophet); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عطية)+; IBNASHUR-FULL+; TAB-FULL+]
- 2:286/q15/p2 Error includes deliberate wrongdoing. — TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari says the intentional kind of error is a chosen violation for which one may ask pardon, apart from unbelief. TAB-FULL: An unintentional mistake based on a reasonable but mistaken assumption carries no sin: examples are eating at night believing dawn has not arrived or delaying prayer on a cloudy day while awaiting its time. TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari distinguishes intentional wrongdoing, which is sinful and calls for pardon except in unbelief, from an innocent mistake. FARISI-HUJJA: نسينا may mean ordinary forgetfulness and أخطأنا an unintentional mistake; the prayer asks not to be punished for either, although believers know God does not punish them for these. THALABI reporting بعضهم: Some take أخطأنا as deliberate intention, using خطيء for someone who sins intentionally; a line by Umayya ibn Abi al-Salt illustrates the usage. THALABI reporting الزهري: Al-Zuhri reports that Umar corrected a man’s prayer for forgiveness of his errors, saying errors are forgiven and he should ask forgiveness for deliberate acts. MAWARDI-FULL: Mistakes may mean sins interpreted under ambiguities or sins deliberately committed despite being wrong. WAHIDI-BASIT: The correct linguistic distinction is خطئ for intentional sin and أخطأ for failing to hit the right answer; Abu al-Haytham likewise distinguishes deliberate from accidental acts. WAHIDI-BASIT reporting أبو عبيدة: Abu Ubayda treats أخطأ and خطئ as two forms of one word, citing poetry; on this reading the verse asks pardon for deliberate sin. DAMGHANI: Akhaṭaʾnā means making an unintentional mistake. JISHUMI reporting الأصم: Al-Asamm takes error to mean an unintentional act. JISHUMI: The author rejects the claim that this prayer is invalid because unintentional error is excused: it remains proper to pray even for what is already waived. BAGHAWI-FULL: One view says the word for erring can mean acting deliberately, supported by its use for a grave intentional act elsewhere. BAGHAWI-FULL reporting الأكثرون: Most interpreters take error as ignorance or inadvertence: deliberate sin is not simply absolved, but left to God’s will, whereas error is forgiven. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting الطبري وغيره: Al-Tabari and others take forgetting as neglecting obedience and error as intentional sin; involuntary forgetfulness and error are already excused. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Many scholars, including Ibn Atiyya, hold that the prayer concerns involuntary forgetfulness and unintentional error; he calls this correct. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Akhṭa'a can describe intentional action as well as genuine mistake or deliberated action. TABRISI: One interpretation takes forgetting as abandoning obedience and error as committing sins, even deliberately; the prayer seeks forgiveness for neglected duties and wrongful acts. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA: Khaṭaʾ can mean an unintentional mistake. IBNJAWZI-ZAD reporting ابن الأنباري: Ibn al-Anbari takes forgetting here as deliberate neglect, since involuntary forgetfulness is already exempt from sin; he likewise takes error here as intentional, though the word can also mean inadvertence. RAZI-FULL: If forgetting and error mean intentionally doing wrong, the commanded prayer gives grave sinners grounds to hope for pardon: God instructs believers to ask not to be punished for such sins. QURTUBI-FULL: The account glosses “if we forgot” as ignorance and “or erred” as deliberate action, while also offering the reading of acting through forgetfulness and error. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting عطاء: ʿAṭāʾ glosses “we forgot” as “we did not know” and “we erred” as “we acted deliberately.” ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting الأصمعي: Al-Asmai distinguishes أخطأ as an unintended mistake from خطئ as deliberate wrongdoing; the cited poem witnesses the latter. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting قيل: One view finds here forgiveness for major sinners: the prayer would be improper if forgetfulness and error meant only unaccountable acts, so they signify deliberate sin, omission and commission respectively. IBNKATHIR-FULL: Forgetfulness can mean leaving an obligation or committing a prohibition unintentionally; error means missing the sound legal form out of ignorance. ALUSI-FULL reporting قيل: One view takes forgetting as abandoning obligations and error as committing prohibitions, even deliberately, since sins are errors opposed to correctness. JISHUMI reporting عطاء: ʿAta interprets the prayer as asking not to be held to account for ignorance or deliberate action. TABRISI reporting ابن عباس وعطاء: Ibn ʿAbbās and ʿAṭāʾ understand it as asking not to be punished for disobedience, whether done in ignorance or deliberately. [29 notes: TAB-FULL; FARISI-HUJJA; THALABI(الزهري,بعضهم); MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT(أبو عبيدة); DAMGHANI; JISHUMI(الأصم,عطاء); BAGHAWI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(الطبري وغيره); TABRISI(ابن عباس وعطاء); IBNJAWZI-NUZHA; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن الأنباري); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الأصمعي,عطاء,قيل); IBNKATHIR-FULL; ALUSI-FULL(قيل); BAGHAWI-FULL(الأكثرون)+; IBNATIYYA-FULL+]
- 2:286/q15/p3 Error is an unintended or mistaken act. — TAB-FULL: The petition asks pardon for forgetting a required duty or mistakenly doing something forbidden without intending disobedience, through ignorance or error. TAB-FULL reporting ابن زيد: Ibn Zayd paraphrases the prayer as asking not to be charged for forgetting a duty or mistakenly committing a prohibition. WAHIDI-BASIT: The supplication for pardon over inadvertent error is also a devotional prayer believers are instructed to make. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA: In legal usage, error is committing a prohibited act with the mistaken person’s intent. RAZI-FULL: Al-Razi answers that forgetfulness may be culpable when someone neglects precautions, but excusable when warning signs were absent or the person took reasonable care; he gives examples involving impurity, mistaken shooting, and forgetting memorized Qurʾan. RAZI-FULL: On the abandonment reading, error means doing an act on the basis of a mistaken interpretation. BAYDAWI-FULL: The prayer may ask pardon for negligence that caused forgetting or error, or for forgetting and error themselves; they do not rationally preclude accountability, like mistakenly taking poison can still cause death. ALUSI-FULL reporting قيل: A third view means actual forgetfulness and error: they could rationally bring punishment, like poison can kill when taken inadvertently, but God promised to overlook them as grace. ELMALILI: The idea that God's mercy would be greater if He imposed no duties is mistaken: mercy does not permit harmful desires to go unchecked or withhold obligations that lead to lasting benefit. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA reporting علي بن عبيد الله: ʿAlī ibn ʿUbayd Allāh distinguishes khāṭiʾ, who hits without intending, from mukhṭiʾ, who intends but misses. [10 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن زيد); WAHIDI-BASIT; IBNJAWZI-NUZHA(علي بن عبيد الله); RAZI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL(قيل); ELMALILI]

## 2:286/q16 What liability remains for forgetfulness, error, or coercion?  [إِن نَّسِينَآ أَوْ أَخْطَأْنَا · law]
- 2:286/q16/p1 This community is excused for error, forgetfulness, and coercion. — IBNABIHATIM reporting ابن عباس، أبو هريرة: In a dialogue, Abū Hurayra asks Ibn ʿAbbās whether adultery and theft are therefore permitted; Ibn ʿAbbās says no, only Israel's special burden was lifted. DURR-FULL reporting الفضيل: Al-Fudayl says an Israelite sinner had to kill himself as repentance, and that this severity was lifted from this community. BURSEVI reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: The Prophet says this community is exempted from liability for error, forgetfulness, and coercion; earlier communities were liable for the first two. ELMALILI: A hunter who shoots without checking whether a person may be present, then hits someone, is not excused because he failed to take the necessary care. [4 notes: IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس، أبو هريرة); DURR-FULL(الفضيل); BURSEVI(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); ELMALILI]

## 2:286/q17 What does إِصْرًا mean?  [إِصْرًۭا · meaning]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q17/p1 Iṣr is a heavy, stringent burden. — IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB: Isr means a burden; the supplication asks not to be burdened with obligations as heavy as those imposed on the Israelites. TAB-FULL reporting الربيع: Al-Rabiʿ explains iṣr as the severe restrictions imposed on earlier People of the Book. TAB-FULL reporting مالك: Malik defines iṣr as a weighty or stringent command. ZAJJAJ: The prayer asks not to be tested with a heavy command like Israel's order to kill themselves, or another burdensome trial. IBNABIHATIM reporting الربيع: Al-Rabīʿ explains the iṣr as the severity God imposed on earlier People of the Book. IBNABIHATIM reporting الفضيل: Al-Fuḍayl says an Israelite who sinned was told to repent by killing himself, a burden removed from this community. IBNABIHATIM reporting السدي: Al-Suddī identifies the unbearable burden with the severe restrictions and prohibitions imposed on the Israelites. THALABI reporting بعضهم: One view reads forgetting literally; al-Kalbi explains that Israelites were punished when they forgot or erred, with foods or drinks forbidden in proportion to the offense, and believers were taught to ask for exemption. THALABI reporting خباب: Khabbab recounts that eleven people died during a gathering where Dhu al-Nun al-Misri spoke of divine love and its overpowering effects on lovers. THALABI reporting بعضهم: One interpretation associates the burden with separation and rupture, saying severed bonds can hurt more than physical pain. MAWARDI-FULL reporting مالك، الربيع، النابغة: Malik and al-Rabi interpret isr as a great burden; al-Nabigha's poetry supports the sense of carrying a burden for others. MAWARDI-FULL: The people before them are the Israelites, who were charged with killing themselves. MAWARDI-FULL: One view takes the unbearable burden as obligations imposed on the Israelites. WAHIDI-BASIT reporting الكلبي: Al-Kalbi says Israelites were punished immediately when they forgot, so believers ask God not to hold them accountable for forgetfulness. WAHIDI-BASIT reporting الكلبي: If أخطأنا means an inadvertent slip, it shares the two explanations given for forgetfulness, including Al-Kalbi's account of immediate punishment for Israelites. WAHIDI-WAJIZ: Isr means a burden; they ask not to be given a heavy command like those imposed on the Israelites. WAHIDI-WAJIZ: The burdensome commands imposed on those before include the loads prescribed for the Israelites. WAHIDI-WAJIZ: This is taught supplication asking not to be punished for forgetfulness; Israelites faced immediate punishment when forgetting a prescription. WAHIDI-WAJIZ: The Israelites faced immediate punishment when they forgot a prescription, so the Prophet and believers were taught to ask not to be held accountable for this. JISHUMI reporting عطاء وعمر ومالك: An alternative takes isr as a heavy burden, asking that commands not be made too strict. JISHUMI reporting قتادة والضحاك والسدي وابن زيد: They interpret this as burdensome commands, such as the Israelites’ order to kill themselves; “I cannot bear speaking to him” means the speech is burdensome. BAGHAWI-FULL reporting الكلبي: Al-Kalbi reads forgetfulness as actual forgetting: Israelites who forgot or erred were punished at once, sometimes by losing foods; believers were taught to ask for no such reckoning. KASHSHAF-FULL: Iṣr is a heavy load that pins its bearer in place; metaphorically it means onerous obligations such as killing oneself or cutting away contaminated skin or clothing. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting مالك: Malik defines isr as a weighty, difficult matter. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting السدي: Al-Suddi identifies the unbearable burden as prohibitions and fetters imposed on the Israelites. TABRISI reporting النابغة: Al-Nābigha's poetry uses iṣr for the burden of protecting one's people from oppression. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA: A maʾṣar is where a watcher stops a passing animal to collect a toll; aṣartu-hu means I detained it. IBNJAWZI-ZAD reporting ابن قتيبة: Another interpretation is a burden: a request not to impose the heavy obligations imposed on the Children of Israel. RAZI-FULL reporting النابغة: Al-Nabigha’s verse uses isr for a burden borne to shield a tribe from oppression, evidencing the word’s sense of weight and hardship. QURTUBI-FULL reporting مالك والربيع: Malik and al-Rabiʿ define iṣr as a severe, difficult command. QURTUBI-FULL reporting سعيد بن جبير: Saʿid ibn Jubayr says iṣr is hard labor and the burdens placed on the Israelites, such as rules about urine. QURTUBI-FULL reporting الضحاك: Al-Dahhak interprets iṣr as weighty, stringent obligations, close to Malik and al-Rabiʿ’s view. QURTUBI-FULL reporting النابغة: Al-Nabigha’s line about bearing the iṣr for people is cited as poetic evidence for the sense of a burden. QURTUBI-FULL reporting السدي: Al-Suddi interprets the unbearable burden as the severity and shackles imposed on the Israelites. QURTUBI-FULL: The Miʿraj account glosses iṣr as a burden: earlier peoples had wholesome things forbidden for wrongdoing, sins written on their doors, and fifty prayers imposed before relief. BAYDAWI-FULL: An iṣr is a heavy burden that restrains its bearer in place; here it means difficult obligations. BAYDAWI-FULL: The comparison may mean a burden like the one imposed on us, like that imposed on earlier people, or describe the iṣr itself. NASAFI-FULL: Isr is a heavy load that pins its bearer in place; metaphorically it denotes hard obligations such as killing oneself or cutting impurity from skin or clothing. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting قتادة: A report from Qatada interprets isr as the hastening of punishment. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting الزجاج: Al-Zajjaj takes isr as a trial that tests people, like killing and wounding among the Israelites or making a silver ceiling for a disbeliever. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting مالك: Malik glosses isr as a severe, difficult command. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting ثعلب: Thalab transmits the gloss that isr means sin. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting قيل: Another gloss takes isr to mean an obligation whose performance is difficult. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting السدي: Al-Suddi identifies the burden imposed on earlier people as Israel’s restrictive prohibitions and heavy restraints. SAMIN-DURR reporting أبي: Ubayy reads rabbanā wa-lā tuḥammil ʿalaynā iṣran with a doubled mīm; al-Zamakhsharī explains this as intensifying “lay a burden upon.” SAMIN-DURR reporting النابغة: Iṣr fundamentally means weight or severity; al-Nābigha's verse calls it a burden borne after the people have been exhausted. SAMIN-DURR: The noun iṣr is also transmitted with ḍamma on its hamza in a rare reading. BIQAI-FULL: Biqāʿī traces iṣr to a root meaning to bend or incline; a heavy branch bends under its weight, explaining the word’s sense of burden. DURR-FULL reporting الربيع: Al-Rabiʿ explains isr as the severe burden imposed on the People of the Book. DURR-FULL reporting عبد الرحمن بن حسنة: The Prophet reports that Israelites cut away urine with scissors when it touched them, illustrating a former legal hardship. DURR-FULL reporting أبو موسى: Abu Musa says Israelites used shears to cut away the place touched by urine. ALUSI-FULL: Iṣr is a weighty burden that confines its bearer; here it means difficult obligations. ALUSI-FULL: The comparison is either an adjective of an omitted verbal noun, meaning a burden like the one imposed earlier, or an adjective of iṣr itself. ELMALILI: Actions can be broader than divine legislation: oppressive conditions may come as discipline through tyrants such as Pharaoh acting against God’s law. The verb “load” allows for this, and the surah’s Israelite stories have signaled both sources of hardship. IBNASHUR-FULL reporting الزجاج، الزمخشري: Al-Zajjaj and al-Zamakhshari explain isr here and in 7:157 as something burdensome to perform and obey; Ibn Ashur says that sense is intended here and that حمل reinforces the burden metaphor. IBNASHUR-FULL: The phrase qualifies isr as a religious burden like difficult rules imposed on earlier peoples, such as Israel's forty years in the wilderness and the rare features of the cow they had to slaughter; these disciplined disobedience and disregard for their messengers. TABATABAI: The account of Israel recalls God’s gifts of scripture, prophethood, and rule and Israel’s disobedience; the ending turns to believers seeking God’s help against unbelievers. TABATABAI: Israel faced heavy burdens and punishments, while the believers humbly ask not to be held to account for lapses or burdened as earlier peoples were, and ask forgiveness and victory. ASAD-NOTES reporting Asad: The requested burden recalls both the heavy ritual law imposed on the children of Israel and the world-renunciation Jesus recommended to his followers. [59 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB; TAB-FULL(الربيع,مالك); ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(الربيع,السدي,الفضيل); THALABI(بعضهم,خباب); MAWARDI-FULL(مالك، الربيع، النابغة); WAHIDI-BASIT(الكلبي); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI(عطاء وعمر ومالك,قتادة والضحاك والسدي وابن زيد); BAGHAWI-FULL(الكلبي); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(السدي,مالك); TABRISI(النابغة); IBNJAWZI-NUZHA; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن قتيبة); RAZI-FULL(النابغة); QURTUBI-FULL(السدي,الضحاك,النابغة,سعيد بن جبير,مالك والربيع); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزجاج,السدي,ثعلب,قتادة,قيل,مالك); SAMIN-DURR(أبي,النابغة); BIQAI-FULL; DURR-FULL(أبو موسى,الربيع,عبد الرحمن بن حسنة); ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL(الزجاج، الزمخشري); TABATABAI; ASAD-NOTES(Asad)]
- 2:286/q17/p2 Iṣr is a binding covenant or pledge. — MAJAZ: إصر means a burden or binding tie; a covenant or kinship that attaches someone to another is an إصر, as in the expression آصرة رحم. ABDURRAZZAQ reporting قتادة: Qatāda glosses “isr” as a covenant and pledge. TAB-FULL: Ṭabarī reads iṣr as a covenant: believers ask not to be bound by a covenant they cannot fulfill, lest failure, error, or forgetfulness bring the punishment suffered by earlier communities. TAB-FULL reporting قتادة: Qatadah glosses iṣr as a covenant and pledge, and says the covenant was made onerous for those before the Muslims. TAB-FULL reporting مجاهد: Mujahid explains iṣr as a covenant. TAB-FULL reporting ابن عباس: Ibn Abbas also glosses iṣr as a covenant. TAB-FULL reporting السدي: Al-Suddi identifies the covenant as the one imposed on the Jews before the Muslims. TAB-FULL reporting ابن جريج: Ibn Jurayj says it is a covenant they could not bear; Jews and Christians failed to keep it and were destroyed. TAB-FULL reporting الضحاك: Al-Dahhak glosses iṣr as covenants. TAB-FULL reporting الربيع: Al-Rabiʿ glosses iṣr as a covenant, citing the same word in 3:81 as meaning God’s covenant. TAB-FULL reporting ابن عباس: Ibn Abbas explains iṣr in 3:81 as God’s covenant. ZAJJAJ: Iṣr can mean a bond of kinship or covenant; Arabs say no kinship tie or favor binds someone to another. ZAJJAJ reporting الحطيئة: Al-Ḥuṭayʾa uses āṣira for kinship or covenant, describing affection without such a tie. IBNABIHATIM reporting ابن عباس، مجاهد، السدي: Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, and al-Suddī understand iṣr as a covenant or binding commitment. IBNABIHATIM reporting الحسن، الضحاك، مقاتل بن حيان: Al-Ḥasan, al-Ḍaḥḥāk, and Muqātil b. Ḥayyān give the related gloss mīthāq, a binding covenant. THALABI reporting مجاهد وعطاء وقتادة والضحاك والربيع ومقاتل والسدي والكلبي وابن جريج والفراء: This group glosses إصر as an unbearable covenant or pledge, whose breach brought punishment on earlier people, especially the Jews; they cite 3:81’s use of إصر for a covenant. THALABI: The word can name what binds things together, a kinship bond, or a relationship formed by covenant; the cited poem uses آصرة for such a tie. MAWARDI-FULL reporting ابن عباس، مجاهد، قتادة: Ibn Abbas, Mujahid, and Qatada interpret the burden as a covenant too difficult to fulfill. WAHIDI-BASIT: Isr fundamentally conveys binding or attachment; it names burdens, covenants, and kin ties, supported by poetry and by the covenant called isr in 3:81. WAHIDI-BASIT reporting ابن عباس: Ibn Abbas, in reports from Al-Walibi, Ata, and Atiyya, and Mujahid, Qatada, Muqatil, Al-Suddi, Al-Kalbi, and Al-Farra say it means an unbearable covenant whose breach brought punishment on the Jews. JISHUMI: Isr can mean a heavy burden or a binding covenant. JISHUMI reporting ابن عباس وقتادة ومجاهد والضحاك والربيع والسدي: They interpret isr as a covenant too difficult to fulfill. BAGHAWI-FULL reporting مجاهد: Mujahid, ʿAta, Qatada, al-Suddi, al-Kalbi, and others say the burden is a severe covenant the Israelites could not fulfill and were punished for breaking; the wording is supported by the covenant verse. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting الطبري والزجاج: For these authors the burden included killing themselves, cutting their bodies, bodily punishment for sins, and difficult covenants. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting قتادة ومجاهد وابن عباس والسدي وابن جريج والربيع وابن زيد: These exegetes explain isr as a firm covenant or solemn pact. TABRISI: Iṣr means weight or burden; it can also denote a binding covenant or kinship tie. TABRISI reporting النابغة: Al-Nābigha also uses iṣr to mean a covenant or binding promise. TABRISI: Earlier communities were punished for sins, had lawful foods forbidden, and received heavier covenants and obligations; this community's lighter burden is a mercy. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA reporting ابن فارس: Ibn Fāris gives isr the senses of burden and covenant, and relates it to bonds of kinship or agreement. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA: Arabic usage can call any bond, kinship, or covenant an isr. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA: Some interpreters distinguish two Qurʾānic senses of isr: burden in 2:286 and covenant elsewhere. Against: IBNJAWZI-NUZHA reporting ابن عباس، ومجاهد، والضحاك، والسدي: These authorities take isr in 2:286 as a covenant; the author says this invalidates the two-sense division. IBNJAWZI-ZAD reporting ابن عباس، مجاهد، الضحاك، السدي: One interpretation of iṣr is a covenant. RAZI-FULL reporting أهل التفسير: The second interpretation asks that God not impose a covenant as severe as that imposed on earlier people; al-Razi says it essentially returns to the first view with an extra unstated element, so the first is preferable. RAZI-FULL: A covenant is called an isr because it is weighty; 3:81 uses the word for God’s covenant and pact. RAZI-FULL reporting القفال: Al-Qaffal says examining the fifth book of the Torah claimed by the Jews reveals many severe covenants and remarkable details. QURTUBI-FULL: Iṣr can mean covenant, as in the covenant taken in another verse, as well as narrowness, sin, or burden. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Iṣr means a burdensome, difficult command; āṣira is a binding tie such as a pact, kinship, or pledge, while iṣār is a rope that binds loads. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting القفال: Al-Qaffal says readers of the fifth book of the Torah attributed to the Jews will find many remarkable instances of their grave covenants and pledges. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting ابن عباس: Ibn Abbas, Mujahid, Qatada, al-Suddi, Ibn Jurayj, al-Rabiʿ, and Ibn Zayd explain isr as a solemn covenant and pledge. SAMIN-DURR: Because a covenant is heavy, iṣr also means covenant or compact, as in God's covenant taken from the prophets in 3:81. BIQAI-FULL reporting الحرالي: Al-Ḥarālī explains iṣr as a weighty covenant whose bearing entails severe hardship. BIQAI-FULL reporting الرب: Israelites are told not to take a widow's garment as pledge and to leave forgotten sheaves and olives for resident outsiders, orphans, and widows, remembering their slavery in Egypt. DURR-FULL reporting ابن عباس: Ibn Abbas glosses isr as a covenant or binding charge. DURR-FULL reporting مجاهد: Mujahid also explains isr as a covenant. DURR-FULL reporting ابن عباس: Ibn Abbas tells Nafiʿ ibn al-Azraq that isr is the covenant imposed on the Jews, whose breach led to transformation into apes and swine; Abu Talib's verse attests the Arabic usage. DURR-FULL reporting ابن جريج: Ibn Jurayj reads isr as an unbearable covenant imposed on Jews and Christians who failed it and were destroyed; he identifies the unbearable burden with transformation into apes and swine. ITQAN reporting أبو القاسم: Abu al-Qasim says isr means “covenant” in Nabataean. ELMALILI: Isr is a heavy burden or bond that holds the bearer in place, associated with captivity and confinement; it can also refer to a weighty covenant, subjection, the womb, and kinship. IBNASHUR-FULL: Isr originally means what is used to bind and fasten things; it later denotes a solemn covenant difficult to fulfill and then a burdensome obligation. IBNASHUR-FULL reporting ابن عباس: Ibn Abbas interprets isr as a covenant the community could not fulfill, whose breach would bring punishment. STUDYQURAN reporting Ṭ: The burden may refer to covenants imposed on earlier religious communities under different conditions, including Jews and Christians; it may also mean other burdens. [51 notes: MAJAZ; ABDURRAZZAQ(قتادة); TAB-FULL(ابن جريج,ابن عباس,الربيع,السدي,الضحاك,قتادة,مجاهد); ZAJJAJ(الحطيئة); IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس، مجاهد، السدي,الحسن، الضحاك، مقاتل بن حيان); THALABI(مجاهد وعطاء وقتادة والضحاك والربيع ومقاتل والسدي والكلبي وابن جريج والفراء); MAWARDI-FULL(ابن عباس، مجاهد، قتادة); WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن عباس); JISHUMI(ابن عباس وقتادة ومجاهد والضحاك والربيع والسدي); BAGHAWI-FULL(مجاهد); IBNATIYYA-FULL(الطبري والزجاج,قتادة ومجاهد وابن عباس والسدي وابن جريج والربيع وابن زيد); TABRISI(النابغة); IBNJAWZI-NUZHA(ابن فارس); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن عباس، مجاهد، الضحاك، السدي); RAZI-FULL(أهل التفسير,القفال); QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عباس,القفال); SAMIN-DURR; BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي,الرب); DURR-FULL(ابن جريج,ابن عباس,مجاهد); ITQAN(أبو القاسم); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL(ابن عباس); STUDYQURAN(Ṭ); IBNJAWZI-NUZHA(ابن عباس، ومجاهد، والضحاك، والسدي)-]
- 2:286/q17/p3 Its root denotes binding, including kinship ties and cords. — TAB-FULL: Ṭabarī distinguishes fatḥa-initial aṣr from the verse’s iṣr: aṣr can mean a bond of kinship that draws one toward another, as in Arabic usage. ZAJJAJ: Maʾṣir and iṣār refer to a binding restraint or the cord used to tie things. THALABI: The poem’s آصرة is evidence for the word’s sense of a bond or tie between people. TABRISI reporting الكميت: Al-Kumayt's verse uses āṣirat al-arḥām for the bond of kinship. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA: Al-Ḥuṭayʾa’s line describes people showing kindness without a bond or kinship. RAZI-FULL: Isr can also mean affection or kinship: caring for someone makes the hardships that befall them weigh on one’s heart. QURTUBI-FULL reporting الجوهري: The word can also mean a rope binding loads; aṣara means to confine, and Jawhari gives maʾṣir/maʾṣar and plural maʾāṣir, noting common mispronunciation. BAYDAWI-FULL: The earlier people’s burdens may be Israelite duties—killing one another, cutting away impurity, fifty daily prayers, and giving a quarter of wealth as alms—or their hardships and ordeals. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting الزمخشري: Al-Zamakhshari derives isr from a burden that holds its bearer in place, metaphorically describing harsh duties such as self-killing and cutting impurity from skin or clothing. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting قيل: Another explanation is severance of kinship ties. SAMIN-DURR: Iṣr in 7:157 means burdensome legal duties; it broadens to anything heavy, and some reportedly interpret it here as enemies' gloating, citing an unnamed poet. SAMIN-DURR reporting الحطيئة: Iṣr can also mean affection or kinship; al-Ḥuṭayʾa says that no tie of kinship draws him to the person. SAMIN-DURR: Iṣr may be the thing by which objects are bound; iṣār is the cord securing loads, from the verb aṣara, “to bind.” BURSEVI: Iṣr denotes a heavy load that pins its bearer in place, and here means burdensome religious duties. BURSEVI: Israelites faced severe duties: self-killing in repentance, cutting mistaken limbs and impurity, fifty daily prayers, mosque-only prayer, fasting restrictions, forbidden good things, quarter-wealth alms, and written records of nightly sins. [15 notes: TAB-FULL; ZAJJAJ; THALABI; TABRISI(الكميت); IBNJAWZI-NUZHA; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(الجوهري); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري,قيل); SAMIN-DURR(الحطيئة); BURSEVI]
- 2:286/q17/p4 Iṣr is a sin without repentance or expiation. — THALABI reporting ابن زيد: Ibn Zayd says the plea is not to impose a sin for which there is no repentance or expiation; the word can also mean a tie or bond. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting ابن زيد: Ibn Zayd also explains isr as a sin for which there is neither expiation nor repentance. QURTUBI-FULL reporting ابن زيد: Ibn Zayd also says iṣr means a sin for which there is neither repentance nor expiation. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting ابن زيد: Ibn Zayd also explains isr as a sin for which there is neither expiation nor repentance. DURR-FULL reporting ابن زيد: Ibn Zayd takes the plea against isr to mean asking not to bear a sin for which there is no repentance or expiation. ALUSI-FULL reporting قيل: Another interpretation takes iṣr as a sin for which there is no repentance, so the supplication asks protection from committing it. TAB-FULL reporting ابن زيد: Ibn Zayd takes the prayer to ask that God not impose a sin without repentance or expiation. BAGHAWI-FULL: Another explanation calls the burden a sin for which there is no repentance and takes the plea to ask protection from such a sin. [8 notes: THALABI(ابن زيد); IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن زيد); QURTUBI-FULL(ابن زيد); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن زيد); DURR-FULL(ابن زيد); ALUSI-FULL(قيل); TAB-FULL(ابن زيد); BAGHAWI-FULL]
- 2:286/q17/p5 Its root denotes binding; in context it names a heavy burden. — BAGHAWI-FULL: The word’s underlying sense is restraint and binding firmly. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Ibn Atiyya links these senses to the root for something binding, such as a cord tying loads. TABATABAI reporting قيل: One reported definition of الإصر is a heavy burden. [3 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABATABAI(قيل)]
- 2:286/q17/p6 Iṣr can refer to metamorphosis into apes and swine. — QURTUBI-FULL reporting عطاء وابن زيد: Ata and Ibn Zayd interpret iṣr as metamorphosis into apes and pigs. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting عطاء: Ata explains isr as transformation into apes and pigs. [2 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(عطاء وابن زيد); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(عطاء)]
- 2:286/q17/p7 Iṣr is a covenant with Israelite restrictions. — MUQATIL: Iṣr is a covenant with Israelite restrictions. [1 notes: MUQATIL]

## 2:286/q18 How is إِصْرًا read?  [إِصْرًۭا · readings]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q18/p1 The word is also read in the plural, “āṣāran”; Ubayy’s reading adds a shadda to “do not place.”. — KASHSHAF-FULL reporting أبي: The word is also read in the plural, “āṣāran”; Ubayy’s reading adds a shadda to “do not place.” [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL(أبي)]
- 2:286/q18/p2 A reading transmitted from Asim has isran with damma on the hamza. — IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting عاصم: A reading transmitted from Asim has isran with damma on the hamza. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(عاصم)]
- 2:286/q18/p3 A reading reported from Asim has the hamza of isr with a damma. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting عاصم: A reading reported from Asim has the hamza of isr with a damma. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(عاصم)]
- 2:286/q18/p4 Tuʾākhidhnā is read with hamza from taking someone to task; the wāw reading either eases hamza to wāw by regular phonology or derives from wākhadhahu, as Abū al-Baqāʾ says. — SAMIN-DURR: Tuʾākhidhnā is read with hamza from taking someone to task; the wāw reading either eases hamza to wāw by regular phonology or derives from wākhadhahu, as Abū al-Baqāʾ says. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]

## 2:286/q19 What earlier burden does the first petition recall?  [وَلَا تَحْمِلْ عَلَيْنَآ إِصْرًۭا كَمَا حَمَلْتَهُۥ عَلَى ٱلَّذِينَ مِن قَبْلِنَا · interpretation]
- 2:286/q19/p1 The first petition recalls stringent duties and punishments imposed on earlier peoples. — ABDURRAZZAQ reporting قتادة: Qatāda explains the comparison as God having imposed a heavier burden on earlier communities. TAB-FULL reporting عطاء بن أبي رباح: ʿAta interprets the prayer as asking not to be transformed into apes and swine as earlier peoples were. TAB-FULL reporting قتادة: Qatadah takes the phrase as a plea not to impose the severe strictness imposed on earlier communities. TAB-FULL reporting السدي: Al-Suddi identifies the burden as the severe restrictions and prohibitions imposed on earlier people. IBNABIHATIM reporting أبو هريرة: Abū Hurayra transmits the answer نعم to the request not to impose the former burden. QUSHAYRI: Earlier communities voiced needs through an intermediary, asking Moses to pray, while this community is directly told to call upon God. QUSHAYRI: Unlike earlier communities, this community is directly commanded: “Your Lord says, call upon Me, and I will answer you.” QUSHAYRI: Earlier peoples needed time to pass before repentance was accepted; for this community remorse itself is repentance. QUSHAYRI: Earlier peoples asked for an idol like others, but this community is distinguished by the radiance of unity and countless special merits. KASHSHAF-FULL: The prayer asks to be spared punishments imposed on earlier peoples, then punishment for failing to preserve difficult duties imposed on them. TABRISI: The ban on good foods for the earlier community is illustrated by 4:160, which says they were forbidden lawful good things because of wrongdoing. QURTUBI-FULL: Revelation includes weighty duties such as one facing ten and emigration, but God eased this community rather than imposing the painful burdens placed on earlier peoples. QURTUBI-FULL reporting قتادة: Qatada interprets the prayer as asking not to be burdened as harshly as earlier peoples were. NASAFI-FULL: This request concerns protection from punishments that befell earlier peoples. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting قتادة: Qatada reads the prayer as asking God not to impose the severity imposed on earlier people. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Another interpretation distinguishes a first request to be spared difficult duties from a second request to be spared punishments inflicted on earlier communities for failing to keep them. IBNKATHIR-FULL: The plea asks not to be burdened with arduous duties, even if bearable, like the shackles imposed on earlier peoples; Muhammad’s merciful law removes such burdens through its easy, tolerant religion. BIQAI-FULL: God rewards their faith by lifting the burdens, hardship, and accountability for mistakes and forgetfulness that marked the earlier community. BIQAI-FULL: The plea recalls that earlier communities received laws that could break their backs, making the present community’s lighter law a reason for gratitude. BIQAI-FULL reporting الرب: The quoted rules isolate people with leprosy and prescribe cutting away or burning affected clothing, leather, or houses; chronic flow also brings lengthy impurity rules. BIQAI-FULL: The author presents these extensive prior-scripture burdens as examples of what earlier peoples were charged with and says Christian burdens will be discussed in al-Ma'ida. BIQAI-FULL: Biqāʿī cites the command that the Gospel community judge by God’s revelation in the Gospel while discussing the burdens imposed on earlier communities. DURR-FULL reporting قتادة: Qatada contrasts the harsh restrictions imposed on earlier communities with the easing, facility, and well-being given this community. DURR-FULL reporting السدي: Al-Suddi identifies the unbearable load with the stringent prohibitions and fetters imposed on earlier people. BURSEVI reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: God spared this community comparable strictures and earlier peoples' punishments; the Prophet describes his mission as the easy, tolerant upright way and says his community is spared sinking, metamorphosis, and drowning. IBNASHUR-FULL: Some distinguish the first prayer as relief from earlier peoples' punishments and the second as relief from severe obligations; Ibn Ashur treats both prayers like the plea not to be taken to task. TABATABAI: After their unqualified assent, the believers acknowledge their weakness and the fate of earlier peoples, asking for mercy because they have no power or protection apart from God. STUDYQURAN reporting IK: Islam is usually understood as easing and reducing ritual and legal obligations borne by earlier peoples. BIQAI-FULL: As an example of earlier burdens, the second book of the Torah prescribes sprinkling sacrificial blood on the altar’s corners. BIQAI-FULL reporting الرب: The cited Mosaic law requires animal offerings at the sanctuary and severe punishment for slaughter or eating blood outside the prescribed rite. BIQAI-FULL reporting الرب: Purification from leprosy requires birds, cedar, ritual washing and shaving, waiting outside the home, and a further offering on the eighth day. BIQAI-FULL reporting الرب: The cited laws prescribe washing and impurity after emissions or contact with menstruants, and lengthy purification periods after childbirth: shorter for a son and longer for a daughter. BIQAI-FULL reporting الرب: The Torah forbids harvesting every olive or grape and delays eating fruit from newly planted trees for three years, dedicates the fourth year's fruit, then permits eating in year five. DURR-FULL reporting عائشة: A Jewish woman told Aisha that grave punishment comes from urine and that it is cut from skin and clothes; after Aisha relayed this, the Prophet confirmed it. [34 notes: ABDURRAZZAQ(قتادة); TAB-FULL(السدي,عطاء بن أبي رباح,قتادة); IBNABIHATIM(أبو هريرة); QUSHAYRI; KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI; QURTUBI-FULL(قتادة); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قتادة); IBNKATHIR-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(الرب); DURR-FULL(السدي,عائشة,قتادة); BURSEVI(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI; STUDYQURAN(IK)]

## 2:286/q20 Who are ٱلَّذِينَ مِن قَبْلِنَا?  [ٱلَّذِينَ مِن قَبْلِنَا · referent]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q20/p1 Those before are the Israelites or Jews. — THALABI reporting عثمان بن عطاء ومالك بن أنس وأبو عبيدة والمؤرخ والقتيبي وابن الأنباري: Another group glosses إصر as a burden or severity and asks that burdens like those imposed on the Jews not be laid on believers; examples include fifty prayers, alms on profits, cutting away impurity, and sins displayed at the door. They connect it to 7:157. JISHUMI: The earlier people are the Jews, who faced severe obligations. BAGHAWI-FULL reporting عثمان: A second view, attributed to ʿUthman, ʿAta, Malik, Abu ʿUbayda, and others, asks that the law not be made as severe as it was for earlier Jews: fifty prayers, a quarter of wealth in alms, cutting away impurity from clothes, and sins displayed on the door; the author links this to the verse about removing burdens. RAZI-FULL reporting أهل التفسير: Exegetes explain the prayer as asking not to impose the severe obligations placed on Jews: traditions cited include fifty prayers, a quarter of wealth in alms, cutting away garment impurity, worldly penalties for lapses, and loss of formerly lawful foods. NASAFI-FULL: The people burdened with such earlier obligations are the Jews. ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The people before the believers here are the Jews. ALUSI-FULL: Al-Baqara opens by describing belief in what was revealed to Muhammad and earlier prophets; Al ʿImran closes by affirming that some People of the Book believe in both revelations. ELMALILI reporting Kaffâl: Kaffal says the fifth book claimed as Torah contains further examples of the Jews’ harsh laws; believers prayed for protection from such severity, which God later removed, as 7:157 states. ELMALILI reporting müfessirîn: Exegetes describe severe requirements imposed on earlier peoples, especially Jews: fifty daily prayers, a quarter of property as tax, cutting away impurity on clothing, exile, swift executions, self-killing for repentance, immediate punishment for rebellion, and prohibition of some lawful foods after an error. TABATABAI: “Those before us” refers to the People of the Book, especially the Jews, as the sūra's stories and 7:157 suggest. [10 notes: THALABI(عثمان بن عطاء ومالك بن أنس وأبو عبيدة والمؤرخ والقتيبي وابن الأنباري); JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL(عثمان); RAZI-FULL(أهل التفسير); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI(Kaffâl,müfessirîn); TABATABAI]
- 2:286/q20/p2 Those before include Jews and Christians. — IBNABIHATIM reporting مقاتل بن حيان: Muqātil b. Ḥayyān identifies those burdened before as the Jews and Christians, whom God destroyed. IBNATIYYA-FULL: Those who came before are the Jews; al-Dahhak adds the Christians. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting الضحاك: Al-Dahhak says the people before them include Christians as well. [3 notes: IBNABIHATIM(مقاتل بن حيان); IBNATIYYA-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الضحاك)]

## 2:286/q21 What is the unbearable load in the second petition?  [وَلَا تُحَمِّلْنَا مَا لَا طَاقَةَ لَنَا بِهِۦ · interpretation]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q21/p1 The burden is punishment people cannot endure. — TAB-FULL reporting ابن جريج: Ibn Jurayj identifies the unbearable burden as transformation into apes and swine. THALABI reporting ابن جريج: Ibn Jurayj identifies the unbearable burden with transformation into apes and swine. THALABI reporting بعضهم: Some distinguish the petitions as pardon from metamorphosis, forgiveness of sins, and mercy from slander; another distinction assigns them respectively to deeds, words, and beliefs or vows. MAWARDI-FULL reporting عطاء: Ata interprets the burden as being transformed into apes and swine. MAWARDI-FULL: The other view understands the unbearable burden as punishment. WAHIDI-BASIT: One explanation is that the petition asks God not to subject them to Hellfire, whose punishment no one can bear. WAHIDI-WAJIZ: They ask God not to punish them with the Fire. JISHUMI: Another interpretation asks not to be burdened with punishment beyond our strength. BAGHAWI-FULL reporting ابن جريج: Ibn Jurayj says it means transformation into apes and swine. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting ابن جريج: Ibn Jurayj interprets it as asking God not to transform believers into apes and swine. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting عطاء: Ata interprets the burden as transformation into apes and swine. RAZI-FULL reporting المعتزلة: Their second reply says taḥmīl is placing an unbearable load, so the phrase means punishment that cannot be endured; reading it as obligation is figurative. QURTUBI-FULL: One gloss takes pardon, forgiveness, and mercy as protection from the past nations’ punishments of metamorphosis, sinking, and pelting. QURTUBI-FULL reporting ابن جريج: Ibn Jurayj interprets it as asking not to be transformed into apes and pigs. NASAFI-FULL: An alternative gloss assigns pardon, forgiveness, and mercy protection respectively from metamorphosis, being swallowed by the earth, and drowning. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting ابن جريج: Ibn Jurayj interprets the burden as transformation into apes and pigs. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting قيل: Other explanations of the burden are hellfire or the whispers of the soul. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting قيل: Specific readings assign pardon to protection from metamorphosis or actions, forgiveness to sinking or speech, mercy to the weighing of deeds, or each plea to death, the grave, and Judgment; Abu Hayyan says these restrictions lack evidence. DURR-FULL reporting عطاء بن أبي رباح: Ata ibn Abi Rabah interprets the plea as asking not to be transformed into apes and swine. ALUSI-FULL reporting بعض المحققين: Some investigators connect al-Baqara's fire prepared for unbelievers and opening mention of believers and unbelievers with Al ʿImran's concluding paradise prepared for the righteous, making the sūras like one sūra. ELMALILI: The plea covers unbearable burdens whether legal, which may drive people to revolt, or existential, which become torment and destruction; God could impose such things as a test or punishment, so believers ask for protection in every form. IBNASHUR-FULL: The unbearable load here means punishments people cannot endure, with the doubled verb form serving a causative function. TABRISI: A second explanation asks protection from punishment that people cannot endure in this life or the next. IBNJAWZI-ZAD reporting قوم: The fifth view takes it as the punishment of Hell. RAZI-FULL: Believers request easier obligations because severity invites shortcoming, which brings punishment they cannot endure; hence they ask for ease in duties. [25 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن جريج); THALABI(ابن جريج,بعضهم); MAWARDI-FULL(عطاء); WAHIDI-BASIT; WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL(ابن جريج); IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن جريج,عطاء); RAZI-FULL(المعتزلة); QURTUBI-FULL(ابن جريج); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن جريج,قيل); DURR-FULL(عطاء بن أبي رباح); ALUSI-FULL(بعض المحققين); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(قوم)]
- 2:286/q21/p2 The burden is an onerous or impossible duty. — TAB-FULL reporting ابن زيد: Ibn Zayd interprets it as asking not to be obligated to follow a religion beyond their ability. THALABI reporting قتادة والضحاك والسدي وابن زيد: They take the plea to mean: do not require deeds beyond our ability. TABRISI: This humble appeal resembles the following plea not to impose what people cannot bear, which may also be devotional. IBNKATHIR-FULL: The plea covers unbearable obligations, calamities, and trials: believers ask not to be tested with what they cannot bear. DURR-FULL reporting الضحاك: Al-Dahhak reads the plea as asking not to be assigned acts beyond one's ability. TAB-FULL: Ṭabarī prefers the request not to impose deeds too heavy to perform, reading it as a plea for ease in religious duties. THALABI reporting إبراهيم: Ibrahim, in a report from Sufyan al-Thawri through Mansur, glosses it as hardship. WAHIDI-BASIT: Another explanation is a burden that is technically possible but onerous and painful; Arabs say they cannot bear someone's sight despite being physically able, as in 11:20. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting قتادة: Qatada asks not to be burdened with the same harshness as those before us. TABRISI: One explanation asks not to be burdened with crushing duties or trials, such as killing oneself as repentance for sin. RAZI-FULL reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: A Prophetic report says a servant should receive food and clothing and not be assigned work beyond what he can bear; al-Razi cites it for the hardship sense and gives no grading. QURTUBI-FULL: The request means not to burden people with work they cannot bear and thereby punish them, or with work they could do but could not sustain because of its hardship. QURTUBI-FULL reporting الكيا الطبري: Al-Kiya al-Tabari likewise uses the verse to deny hardship and constriction incompatible with the tolerant, upright religion. QURTUBI-FULL reporting الضحاك وابن زيد: Al-Dahhak and Ibn Zayd say the prayer asks not to be burdened with deeds people cannot bear. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: The Old Testament, still practically valid for Christianity, has strict rules and restrictions on eating, drinking, and purification; Islam’s duties fit human nature and are manageable, with dispensations for personal hardship. [15 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن زيد); THALABI(إبراهيم,قتادة والضحاك والسدي وابن زيد); TABRISI; IBNKATHIR-FULL; DURR-FULL(الضحاك); WAHIDI-BASIT; IBNATIYYA-FULL(قتادة); RAZI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); QURTUBI-FULL(الضحاك وابن زيد,الكيا الطبري); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; TAB-FULL+]
- 2:286/q21/p3 The burden is overpowering sexual desire. — TAB-FULL reporting سالم بن شابور: Salim ibn Shabur glosses the unbearable burden as overpowering sexual desire. IBNABIHATIM reporting مكحول: Makhūl glosses the burden as estrangement and intense sexual desire. BAGHAWI-FULL reporting مكحول: Makḥul identifies the burden as intense sexual desire. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting سلام بن سابور: Salam ibn Sabur says the unbearable thing is sexual desire; al-Naqqash transmits this from Mujahid, Ata, and Mak'hul. IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting أبو الدرداء: Abu al-Darda seeks refuge from sexual desire for which there is no means of satisfaction. IBNJAWZI-ZAD reporting مكحول: A third view identifies it as sexual desire. QURTUBI-FULL reporting سلام بن سابور ومجاهد وعطاء: Salam ibn Sabur says the unbearable thing is sexual urge; al-Naqqash also transmits this interpretation from Mujahid and Ata. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting مكحول: Makhul and Salam ibn Sabur understand the unbearable burden as overpowering sexual desire; al-Naqqash also transmits this from Mujahid, Ata, and Makhul. ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting أبو الدرداء: Abu al-Darda used to pray for refuge from sexual desire that could not be restrained. IBNKATHIR-FULL reporting مكحول: Makḥul glosses the unbearable burden as estrangement and intense sexual desire; Ibn Abi Hatim transmits the gloss, followed by the divine answer, Yes, or in another report, I have done so. DURR-FULL reporting مكحول: Makhul interprets the unbearable matters as exile, sexual desire, and erection. DURR-FULL reporting سلام بن سابور: Salam ibn Sabur glosses the phrase as sexual desire. IBNABIHATIM reporting مكحول: Makhūl gives a second gloss: erection. QURTUBI-FULL reporting أبو الدرداء: Abu al-Darda used to pray for refuge from sexual urge without the means to satisfy it. [14 notes: TAB-FULL(سالم بن شابور); IBNABIHATIM(مكحول); BAGHAWI-FULL(مكحول); IBNATIYYA-FULL(أبو الدرداء,سلام بن سابور); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(مكحول); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو الدرداء,سلام بن سابور ومجاهد وعطاء); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو الدرداء,مكحول); IBNKATHIR-FULL(مكحول); DURR-FULL(سلام بن سابور,مكحول)]
- 2:286/q21/p4 The burden is inner speech and whispering. — TAB-FULL reporting ابن عباس: Ibn Abbas reports that believers asked how to repent of involuntary whisperings; Gabriel brought 2:286 to assure them they could not prevent such thoughts. TAB-FULL: Al-Tabari concludes that God neither strains nor constricts a soul in religion, and does not charge it for an unacted intention, passing whisper, or fleeting thought. THALABI reporting بعضهم: Another view applies the phrase to inner speech and whisperings. BAGHAWI-FULL: An unnamed view takes the unbearable burden to mean inner speech and whispering. IBNJAWZI-ZAD reporting قوم: A fourth view identifies it as inner thoughts and their whisperings.
