- 2:286/q37/p13 A hadith calls the chapter in which the cow is mentioned the fortress of the Qurʾan. — THALABI reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: A hadith calls the chapter in which the cow is mentioned the fortress of the Qurʾan. [1 notes: THALABI(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 2:286/q37/p14 Abu Sa'id al-Khudri reports the Prophet calling al-Baqara a pavilion of the Qur'an and urging its study: learning it is blessed, abandoning it regrettable, and sorcerers cannot withstand it. — MAWARDI-FULL reporting أبو سعيد الخدري: Abu Sa'id al-Khudri reports the Prophet calling al-Baqara a pavilion of the Qur'an and urging its study: learning it is blessed, abandoning it regrettable, and sorcerers cannot withstand it. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL(أبو سعيد الخدري)]
- 2:286/q37/p15 Ibn Abbas reports that as the Prophet recited the successive petitions, God answered that He had forgiven, excused, shown mercy, and granted victory in response to each. — MAWARDI-FULL reporting ابن عباس: Ibn Abbas reports that as the Prophet recited the successive petitions, God answered that He had forgiven, excused, shown mercy, and granted victory in response to each. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 2:286/q37/p16 Abu Ishaq says God placed this prayer of the Prophet and believers in scripture so later generations could preserve and frequently recite it. — WAHIDI-BASIT reporting أبو إسحاق: Abu Ishaq says God placed this prayer of the Prophet and believers in scripture so later generations could preserve and frequently recite it. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(أبو إسحاق)]
- 2:286/q37/p17 Muʿadh is reported to say “Amen” when he finishes reciting the end of Surat al-Baqara. — JISHUMI reporting معاذ: Muʿadh is reported to say “Amen” when he finishes reciting the end of Surat al-Baqara. [1 notes: JISHUMI(معاذ)]
- 2:286/q37/p18 The Prophet is reported to say that the closing verses of al-Baqara are Qur’an and supplication, and please the Merciful; no grading is stated. — JISHUMI reporting النبي: The Prophet is reported to say that the closing verses of al-Baqara are Qur’an and supplication, and please the Merciful; no grading is stated. [1 notes: JISHUMI(النبي)]
- 2:286/q37/p19 Ibn Abbas reports that when the Prophet recited these supplications, he was told after each one, “It has been done.”. — KASHSHAF-FULL reporting ابن عباس: Ibn Abbas reports that when the Prophet recited these supplications, he was told after each one, “It has been done.” [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 2:286/q37/p20 The Prophet calls al-Baqara the Qurʾān’s fustāṭ, urges learning it for its blessing, warns that neglect is regret, and says sorcerers cannot master it. — KASHSHAF-FULL reporting رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم: The Prophet calls al-Baqara the Qurʾān’s fustāṭ, urges learning it for its blessing, warns that neglect is regret, and says sorcerers cannot master it. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 2:286/q37/p21 Muadh ibn Jabal is reported to say amin after reciting the sura. — IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting معاذ بن جبل: Muadh ibn Jabal is reported to say amin after reciting the sura. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(معاذ بن جبل)]
- 2:286/q37/p22 Al-Suddi says that after believers recited the prayer at its revelation, Gabriel told the Prophet God had granted it. — IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting السدي: Al-Suddi says that after believers recited the prayer at its revelation, Gabriel told the Prophet God had granted it. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(السدي)]
- 2:286/q37/p23 Ali says he doubts anyone who understands Islam would sleep without reciting the two verses. — IBNATIYYA-FULL reporting علي بن أبي طالب: Ali says he doubts anyone who understands Islam would sleep without reciting the two verses. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(علي بن أبي طالب)]
- 2:286/q37/p24 Ibn al-Munkadir transmits from the Prophet that the final verses of al-Baqara are Qur'an and supplication and please the Merciful. — TABRISI reporting ابن المنكدر: Ibn al-Munkadir transmits from the Prophet that the final verses of al-Baqara are Qur'an and supplication and please the Merciful. [1 notes: TABRISI(ابن المنكدر)]
- 2:286/q37/p25 Muadh ibn Jabal reportedly said “Amen” after reciting this sura; Ibn Atiyya says this may be a report from the Prophet, or a sound analogy with al-Fatiha’s supplication. — QURTUBI-FULL reporting معاذ بن جبل: Muadh ibn Jabal reportedly said “Amen” after reciting this sura; Ibn Atiyya says this may be a report from the Prophet, or a sound analogy with al-Fatiha’s supplication. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(معاذ بن جبل)]
- 2:286/q37/p26 This report counters an objection to calling it Sūrat al-Baqara: the Prophet called it the sūra in which the cow is mentioned, a fortress of the Qur’an whose study is blessed, whose neglect is. — BAYDAWI-FULL reporting رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم: This report counters an objection to calling it Sūrat al-Baqara: the Prophet called it the sūra in which the cow is mentioned, a fortress of the Qur’an whose study is blessed, whose neglect is regretted, and which sorcerers cannot master; he explained “the impotent” as sorcerers. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 2:286/q37/p27 After the command to say that they hear, obey, and seek forgiveness, the people recited it; revelation then came with the Messenger’s faith and the final supplication, replacing the earlier burden through the plea. — BIQAI-FULL: After the command to say that they hear, obey, and seek forgiveness, the people recited it; revelation then came with the Messenger’s faith and the final supplication, replacing the earlier burden through the plea not to be taken for forgetting or error. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:286/q37/p28 Gabriel recited the end of al-Baqara; after Muhammad memorized and recited it, God affirmed each letter as granted to him. — DURR-FULL reporting الضحاك: Gabriel recited the end of al-Baqara; after Muhammad memorized and recited it, God affirmed each letter as granted to him. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(الضحاك)]
- 2:286/q37/p29 As Gabriel recited the pleas, the Prophet responded 'Amen, Lord of the worlds' after each one. — DURR-FULL reporting عطاء: As Gabriel recited the pleas, the Prophet responded 'Amen, Lord of the worlds' after each one. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(عطاء)]
- 2:286/q37/p30 Muadh ibn Jabal would say 'Amen' after reciting the closing plea for victory over the unbelievers. — DURR-FULL reporting معاذ بن جبل: Muadh ibn Jabal would say 'Amen' after reciting the closing plea for victory over the unbelievers. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(معاذ بن جبل)]
- 2:286/q37/p31 Jubayr ibn Nufayr would say 'Amen, amen' when reciting al-Baqara's ending. — DURR-FULL reporting جبير بن نغير: Jubayr ibn Nufayr would say 'Amen, amen' when reciting al-Baqara's ending. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(جبير بن نغير)]
- 2:286/q37/p32 Hudhayfa says that after the Prophet recited al-Baqara in prayer, he praised God as Lord and said 'To You belongs praise' seven or ten times. — DURR-FULL reporting حذيفة: Hudhayfa says that after the Prophet recited al-Baqara in prayer, he praised God as Lord and said 'To You belongs praise' seven or ten times. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(حذيفة)]
- 2:286/q37/p33 Al-Tabarani transmits a report from Uqba ibn Amir urging recitation of the last two verses of al-Baqara, which God selected for Muhammad. — ITQAN reporting عقبة بن عامر: Al-Tabarani transmits a report from Uqba ibn Amir urging recitation of the last two verses of al-Baqara, which God selected for Muhammad. [1 notes: ITQAN(عقبة بن عامر)]
- 2:286/q37/p34 The reverse figure names a part for the whole: 'face' means God's essence or the person facing, body-wide states are expressed by faces or hands, and prayer's standing, reciting, bowing, or prostrating can stand. — ITQAN: The reverse figure names a part for the whole: 'face' means God's essence or the person facing, body-wide states are expressed by faces or hands, and prayer's standing, reciting, bowing, or prostrating can stand for the whole prayer. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 2:286/q37/p35 Al-Daylami reports Muʿādh's capture of a date-stealing jinn; the Prophet foretold its return, and on its second capture it claimed that reciting al-Baqara's ending keeps devils out of a house that night. — BURSEVI reporting أبو الأسلم الديلمي: Al-Daylami reports Muʿādh's capture of a date-stealing jinn; the Prophet foretold its return, and on its second capture it claimed that reciting al-Baqara's ending keeps devils out of a house that night. [1 notes: BURSEVI(أبو الأسلم الديلمي)]
- 2:286/q37/p36 Ibn Abi ʿAblah recites wusʿahā with fatḥa on the sīn. — ALUSI-FULL reporting ابن أبي عبلة: Ibn Abi ʿAblah recites wusʿahā with fatḥa on the sīn. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(ابن أبي عبلة)]
- 2:286/q37/p37 Muslim and al-Tirmidhi report from Ibn Abbas that after the Prophet recited these verses upon their revelation, he was told after each clause: I have done so. — ALUSI-FULL reporting ابن عباس: Muslim and al-Tirmidhi report from Ibn Abbas that after the Prophet recited these verses upon their revelation, he was told after each clause: I have done so. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 2:286/q37/p38 Abu ʿUbayd transmits from Muhammad ibn al-Munkadir that the verses are Qur'an and supplication, bring entry into Paradise, and please the Merciful. — ALUSI-FULL reporting محمد بن المنكدر: Abu ʿUbayd transmits from Muhammad ibn al-Munkadir that the verses are Qur'an and supplication, bring entry into Paradise, and please the Merciful. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(محمد بن المنكدر)]
- 2:286/q37/p39 Musaddad transmits from ʿUmar, and al-Darimi from ʿAli, that neither thought a sensible person would sleep without reciting these final verses of al-Baqara. — ALUSI-FULL reporting عمر: Musaddad transmits from ʿUmar, and al-Darimi from ʿAli, that neither thought a sensible person would sleep without reciting these final verses of al-Baqara. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(عمر)]
- 2:286/q37/p40 God’s answer to the believers’ plea calms their anxiety through ease and relief, making Qur’anic recitation a direct dialogue and intimate prayer for the Prophet and his community; Elmalılı compares this style with al-Fatiha. — ELMALILI: God’s answer to the believers’ plea calms their anxiety through ease and relief, making Qur’anic recitation a direct dialogue and intimate prayer for the Prophet and his community; Elmalılı compares this style with al-Fatiha. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 2:286/q37/p41 Both Umar and Ali are reported to have said that they knew no sensible person who went to sleep without reciting these final verses of al-Baqara. — ELMALILI reporting Ömer ve Ali: Both Umar and Ali are reported to have said that they knew no sensible person who went to sleep without reciting these final verses of al-Baqara. [1 notes: ELMALILI(Ömer ve Ali)]
- 2:286/q37/p42 Reciting these verses and carefully displaying them as calligraphic panels became a way the Muslim community embodied their significance. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: Reciting these verses and carefully displaying them as calligraphic panels became a way the Muslim community embodied their significance. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:286/q37/p43 The final two verses are among the Quran's most recited and memorized passages; verse 285 states faith, verse 286 supplicates, and they are often recited together. — STUDYQURAN: The final two verses are among the Quran's most recited and memorized passages; verse 285 states faith, verse 286 supplicates, and they are often recited together. [1 notes: STUDYQURAN]
- 2:286/q37/p44 After completing the sūrah, the prominent Companion Muʿādh ibn Jabal reportedly said amen, as is customary after reciting the Fātiḥah. — STUDYQURAN reporting Muʿādh ibn Jabal: After completing the sūrah, the prominent Companion Muʿādh ibn Jabal reportedly said amen, as is customary after reciting the Fātiḥah. [1 notes: STUDYQURAN(Muʿādh ibn Jabal)]
- 2:286/q37/p45 The final two verses are among the surah's most recited and are important in Muslim devotional life. — STUDYQURAN: The final two verses are among the surah's most recited and are important in Muslim devotional life. [1 notes: STUDYQURAN]
- 2:286/q37/p46 One explanation is that the verses suffice for night prayer; Ibn Umar reports that reading them twice after the evening prayer fulfills that role. — QURTUBI-FULL reporting ابن عمر: One explanation is that the verses suffice for night prayer; Ibn Umar reports that reading them twice after the evening prayer fulfills that role. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عمر)]
- 2:286/q37/p47 Bursevi uses the Prophet's designation of al-Baqara as the Quran's tent to refute disapproval of calling it Surat al-Baqara. — BURSEVI reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: Bursevi uses the Prophet's designation of al-Baqara as the Quran's tent to refute disapproval of calling it Surat al-Baqara. [1 notes: BURSEVI(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]

## 2:286/q38 What recitation practice follows the final prayer?  [* · reports]
- 2:286/q38/p1 Readers said Amen after the final prayer. — THALABI reporting معاذ بن جبل: Muadh ibn Jabal used to say Amen after completing Surat al-Baqara. BAGHAWI-FULL reporting معاذ بن جبل: Muʿadh ibn Jabal would say amen when he completed Surat al-Baqara. IBNJAWZI-ZAD reporting معاذ: Muʿadh used to say “Amen” after finishing this surah. IBNKATHIR-FULL reporting معاذ بن جبل: Ibn Jarir reports that Muadh said Amin when finishing the sura; Waki reports a route through an unnamed man from Muadh saying he said Amin on completing al-Baqara. DURR-FULL reporting أبو ميسرة: Abu Maysara says Gabriel taught the Prophet to say 'Amen' at the end of al-Baqara. ITQAN reporting أبو ميسرة: Abu Maysara reports that Gabriel taught the Prophet to say “Amen” at the end of al-Baqara. BURSEVI reporting معاذ: Muʿādh used to say amen whenever he completed Surat al-Baqara. ALUSI-FULL reporting أبو ميسرة: Abu ʿUbayd reports from Abu Maysara that Gabriel prompted the Prophet to say āmin at the end of al-Baqara. ELMALILI reporting Ebû Meysere: Abu Maysara reports that Gabriel taught the Prophet to say “amen” at the end of Sura al-Baqara. [9 notes: THALABI(معاذ بن جبل); BAGHAWI-FULL(معاذ بن جبل); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(معاذ); IBNKATHIR-FULL(معاذ بن جبل); DURR-FULL(أبو ميسرة); ITQAN(أبو ميسرة); BURSEVI(معاذ); ALUSI-FULL(أبو ميسرة); ELMALILI(Ebû Meysere)]

## 2:286/q39 How does this verse connect to its surah and other verses?  [* · links]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q39/p1 Abū Qilāba reports that God wrote a book two thousand years before creating the heavens and earth and placed it on, or within, the Throne; the closing verses of al-Baqara came from it. — ABDURRAZZAQ reporting أبو قلابة: Abū Qilāba reports that God wrote a book two thousand years before creating the heavens and earth and placed it on, or within, the Throne; the closing verses of al-Baqara came from it. [1 notes: ABDURRAZZAQ(أبو قلابة)]
- 2:286/q39/p2 Opening a door to good brings seventy doors of divine enabling; opening one to evil brings seventy unseen doors of harm. — TUSTARI: Opening a door to good brings seventy doors of divine enabling; opening one to evil brings seventy unseen doors of harm. [1 notes: TUSTARI]
- 2:286/q39/p3 Saʿid ibn Jubayr adds that the closing verses of al-Baqara were granted to this community and not to earlier nations. — TAB-FULL reporting سعيد بن جبير: Saʿid ibn Jubayr adds that the closing verses of al-Baqara were granted to this community and not to earlier nations. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(سعيد بن جبير)]
- 2:286/q39/p4 وسع is what a person can encompass or manage; another gloss connects it with what is difficult and strenuous. — THALABI: وسع is what a person can encompass or manage; another gloss connects it with what is difficult and strenuous. [1 notes: THALABI]
- 2:286/q39/p5 Al-Hasan takes the prayer opening as an instruction: say, Our Lord. — WAHIDI-BASIT reporting الحسن: Al-Hasan takes the prayer opening as an instruction: say, Our Lord. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(الحسن)]
- 2:286/q39/p6 Mawla in the sense of aid derives from closeness and connection; wali and mawla are equivalent, as in God's being with the believers as their helper. — WAHIDI-BASIT: Mawla in the sense of aid derives from closeness and connection; wali and mawla are equivalent, as in God's being with the believers as their helper. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT]
- 2:286/q39/p7 It is acceptable to say “I read al-Baqara” as shorthand for the surah; prophetic reports and Companion usage attest this, like naming other surahs. — KASHSHAF-FULL: It is acceptable to say “I read al-Baqara” as shorthand for the surah; prophetic reports and Companion usage attest this, like naming other surahs. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 2:286/q39/p8 Saying “I read al-Baqara” plainly means the surah, just as one says “the surah of al-Zukhruf,” al-Mumtahana, or al-Mujadila. — KASHSHAF-FULL: Saying “I read al-Baqara” plainly means the surah, just as one says “the surah of al-Zukhruf,” al-Mumtahana, or al-Mujadila. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 2:286/q39/p9 Al-Rāzī says the opening clause may be God's own statement or a continuation of the believers' words following their confession and prayer in 2:285. — RAZI-FULL: Al-Rāzī says the opening clause may be God's own statement or a continuation of the believers' words following their confession and prayer in 2:285. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:286/q39/p10 Supplication displays the worshipper’s poverty, need, and humility before God’s majesty and self-sufficiency, so the surah closes with entreaty; al-Razi discusses prayer under 2:186. — RAZI-FULL: Supplication displays the worshipper’s poverty, need, and humility before God’s majesty and self-sufficiency, so the surah closes with entreaty; al-Razi discusses prayer under 2:186. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:286/q39/p11 The opening statement may be reported as part of the preceding prayer or begin a new sentence. — NASAFI-FULL: The opening statement may be reported as part of the preceding prayer or begin a new sentence. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 2:286/q39/p12 The endings of long surahs echo their openings, following an Arabic poetic habit of digressing and returning; here the ending identifies those believers as Muhammad’s community. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The endings of long surahs echo their openings, following an Arabic poetic habit of digressing and returning; here the ending identifies those believers as Muhammad’s community. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:286/q39/p13 The closing passage uses antithesis, semantic contrast, repetition, paronomasia, and ellipsis for rhetorical effect. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The closing passage uses antithesis, semantic contrast, repetition, paronomasia, and ellipsis for rhetorical effect. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:286/q39/p14 The closing of al-Baqara is another example of a sura ending made of supplication. — BURHAN: The closing of al-Baqara is another example of a sura ending made of supplication. [1 notes: BURHAN]
- 2:286/q39/p15 Biqāʿī says the revelation sequence establishes the coherence of the closing verses. — BIQAI-FULL: Biqāʿī says the revelation sequence establishes the coherence of the closing verses. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:286/q39/p16 Muslim and others transmit from Ibn Abbas an account similar to Abu Hurayra’s report about the revelation of these closing verses. — SUYUTI-LUBAB reporting ابن عباس: Muslim and others transmit from Ibn Abbas an account similar to Abu Hurayra’s report about the revelation of these closing verses. [1 notes: SUYUTI-LUBAB(ابن عباس)]
- 2:286/q39/p17 The opening clause is God's own statement, not part of the believers' prayer. — BURSEVI: The opening clause is God's own statement, not part of the believers' prayer. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:286/q39/p18 Al ʿImran's close, commanding God-consciousness for success, matches al-Baqara's opening description of the God-conscious as successful. — ALUSI-FULL: Al ʿImran's close, commanding God-consciousness for success, matches al-Baqara's opening description of the God-conscious as successful. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:286/q39/p19 He cites the reported occasion that al-Baqara was the first sūra revealed in Medina as fitting the closing passage’s message. — TABATABAI: He cites the reported occasion that al-Baqara was the first sūra revealed in Medina as fitting the closing passage’s message. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 2:286/q39/p20 The sūra’s purpose is that true worship requires belief in all revelation without distinguishing among messengers; its opening and closing frame that theme. — TABATABAI: The sūra’s purpose is that true worship requires belief in all revelation without distinguishing among messengers; its opening and closing frame that theme. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 2:286/q39/p21 The sūra’s ending complements its opening: after the opening’s account of faithful belief and good works, the conclusion confirms the standing of the Prophet and believers and teaches later Muslims how to relate to. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: The sūra’s ending complements its opening: after the opening’s account of faithful belief and good works, the conclusion confirms the standing of the Prophet and believers and teaches later Muslims how to relate to God. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:286/q39/p22 Al-Baqara's account of Abraham asking for a messenger among his descendants is echoed by Al ʿImran's statement that God favored believers by sending a messenger from among them. — ALUSI-FULL: Al-Baqara's account of Abraham asking for a messenger among his descendants is echoed by Al ʿImran's statement that God favored believers by sending a messenger from among them. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:286/q39/p23 Placing the prayer at the end completes the notice that the sura is ending. — IBNASHUR-FULL: Placing the prayer at the end completes the notice that the sura is ending. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:286/q39/p24 Another reported definition is forcibly confining something; al-Ṭabāṭabāʾī says this is close to burden, since confinement imposes what is disliked and heavy. — TABATABAI reporting قيل: Another reported definition is forcibly confining something; al-Ṭabāṭabāʾī says this is close to burden, since confinement imposes what is disliked and heavy. [1 notes: TABATABAI(قيل)]

## 2:286/q40 What inward meanings are given to the verse?  [* · inward]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q40/p1 God assigns each self only the unveilings and manifestations its vessel can bear, according to primordial preparedness granted through the holiest effusion. — QASHANI: God assigns each self only the unveilings and manifestations its vessel can bear, according to primordial preparedness granted through the holiest effusion. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:286/q40/p2 Good works, knowledge, perfections, and unveilings belong to the self, intentionally acquired or not, as goods are native to its luminous essence; dark ignorance, vice, and sin are alien and incur liability only when. — QASHANI: Good works, knowledge, perfections, and unveilings belong to the self, intentionally acquired or not, as goods are native to its luminous essence; dark ignorance, vice, and sin are alien and incur liability only when deliberately pursued. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:286/q40/p3 A report says the angel on the right records each good deed immediately, while the angel on the left waits six hours; repentance, seeking forgiveness, or remorse prevents recording the sin. — QASHANI: A report says the angel on the right records each good deed immediately, while the angel on the left waits six hours; repentance, seeking forgiveness, or remorse prevents recording the sin. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:286/q40/p4 Here nafs means the self or essence, not the lower soul; kasb marks good that is not deliberately sought, while iktisab marks evil actively pursued by the lower soul. — QASHANI: Here nafs means the self or essence, not the lower soul; kasb marks good that is not deliberately sought, while iktisab marks evil actively pursued by the lower soul. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:286/q40/p5 Forgetting is forgetting the covenant and error is acting for other than God while veiled from Him; the prayer describes spiritual strangers far from Him, tested in darkness, with no standing of their own. — QASHANI: Forgetting is forgetting the covenant and error is acting for other than God while veiled from Him; the prayer describes spiritual strangers far from Him, tested in darkness, with no standing of their own before Him. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:286/q40/p6 The burden is their own essences, attributes, and acts, which bind and exile them from God; no weight is heavier. — QASHANI: The burden is their own essences, attributes, and acts, which bind and exile them from God; no weight is heavier. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:286/q40/p7 Earlier peoples were veiled either by the outward realm of acts or by the inward realm of attributes. — QASHANI: Earlier peoples were veiled either by the outward realm of acts or by the inward realm of attributes. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:286/q40/p8 What they cannot bear is the weight of estrangement and deprivation from divine nearness and witnessing God's beauty, behind veils of majesty. — QASHANI: What they cannot bear is the weight of estrangement and deprivation from divine nearness and witnessing God's beauty, behind veils of majesty. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:286/q40/p9 Pardon means overlooking bad actions and attributes, which veil them from God and deprive them of the coolness of pardon and the pleasure of divine approval. — QASHANI: Pardon means overlooking bad actions and attributes, which veil them from God and deprive them of the coolness of pardon and the pleasure of divine approval. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:286/q40/p10 Forgiveness is sought for the sin of separate existence, which a quoted verse of poetry calls a sin without equal. — QASHANI: Forgiveness is sought for the sin of separate existence, which a quoted verse of poetry calls a sin without equal. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:286/q40/p11 Mercy means receiving gifted existence after annihilation. — QASHANI: Mercy means receiving gifted existence after annihilation. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:286/q40/p12 God is their patron as helper and steward of their affairs, or their master, who is bound to support His servants. — QASHANI: God is their patron as helper and steward of their affairs, or their master, who is bound to support His servants. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:286/q40/p13 The enemies are the powers and attributes of the commanding self and the armies of satanic imaginings and fancies, veiled from God and obscuring the seekers through disbelief and darkness. — QASHANI: The enemies are the powers and attributes of the commanding self and the armies of satanic imaginings and fancies, veiled from God and obscuring the seekers through disbelief and darkness. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:286/q40/p14 What the soul earns is goodness, perfection, and unveiling, whether gained through effort or spontaneously. — ALUSI-FULL: What the soul earns is goodness, perfection, and unveiling, whether gained through effort or spontaneously. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:286/q40/p15 What weighs against the soul is the evil toward which it deliberately turns. — ALUSI-FULL: What weighs against the soul is the evil toward which it deliberately turns. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:286/q40/p16 Forgetting is forgetting the covenant through inclination toward the darkness of nature. — ALUSI-FULL: Forgetting is forgetting the covenant through inclination toward the darkness of nature. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:286/q40/p17 Error means acting in a way unfit for the divine presence. — ALUSI-FULL: Error means acting in a way unfit for the divine presence. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:286/q40/p18 The burden is the load of attributes and acts that imprison hearts and keep them from witnessing God. — ALUSI-FULL: The burden is the load of attributes and acts that imprison hearts and keep them from witnessing God. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:286/q40/p19 Those before are people fixated on outward acts or inward attributes. — ALUSI-FULL: Those before are people fixated on outward acts or inward attributes. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:286/q40/p20 The unbearable burden is abandonment and deprivation from union and witnessing divine beauty while veiled by majesty. — ALUSI-FULL: The unbearable burden is abandonment and deprivation from union and witnessing divine beauty while veiled by majesty. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:286/q40/p21 They ask pardon for sins of acts and attributes that veil them from God and deprive them of intimacy and delight. — ALUSI-FULL: They ask pardon for sins of acts and attributes that veil them from God and deprive them of intimacy and delight. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:286/q40/p22 They ask forgiveness for the sin of their own existence, which is the greatest sin. — ALUSI-FULL: They ask forgiveness for the sin of their own existence, which is the greatest sin. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:286/q40/p23 Mercy is the bestowed existence that follows annihilation. — ALUSI-FULL: Mercy is the bestowed existence that follows annihilation. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:286/q40/p24 God is their master and manager because they are His manifestations and effects of His power. — ALUSI-FULL: God is their master and manager because they are His manifestations and effects of His power. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:286/q40/p25 The unbelievers signify commanding souls, their attributes, and the armies of satanic imaginations that veil people from God. — ALUSI-FULL: The unbelievers signify commanding souls, their attributes, and the armies of satanic imaginations that veil people from God. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:286/q40/p26 The soul is charged only with what its disposition can contain without constriction: spiritual manifestations. — ALUSI-FULL: The soul is charged only with what its disposition can contain without constriction: spiritual manifestations. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]

- 2:286/q41/p11 Participles and adjectives may agree or take accusative under different attachments; inversion is rejected. — SIBAWAYH: Participles and adjectives may agree or take accusative under different attachments; inversion is rejected. - 2:286/q41/p12 Tarḫīm shortens certain vocatives. — SIBAWAYH: Tarḫīm shortens certain vocatives. SIBAWAYH: Tarḫīm shortens certain vocatives. SIBAWAYH: Tarḫīm shortens certain vocatives. SIBAWAYH: Tarḫīm shortens certain vocatives. SIBAWAYH: Tarḫīm shortens certain vocatives. [5 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p13 A noun after wāw may express accompaniment or object relation. — SIBAWAYH: A noun after wāw may express accompaniment or object relation. SIBAWAYH: A noun after wāw may express accompaniment or object relation. SIBAWAYH: A noun after wāw may express accompaniment or object relation. SIBAWAYH: A noun after wāw may express accompaniment or object relation. [4 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p14 Coordination after mā shaʾnaka and similar expressions depends on overt and implied governors. — SIBAWAYH: Coordination after mā shaʾnaka and similar expressions depends on overt and implied governors. SIBAWAYH: Coordination after mā shaʾnaka and similar expressions depends on overt and implied governors. [2 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p15 With ما أنت وزيد, nominative is more frequent and better; with ما شأن عبد الله وزيد, genitive is better because both are understood under شأن. — SIBAWAYH: With ما أنت وزيد, nominative is more frequent and better; with ما شأن عبد الله وزيد, genitive is better because both are understood under شأن. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p16 The accusative in ما لزيد وأخاه can be understood through an omitted كان شأن construction, which supplies the verbal relation. — SIBAWAYH: The accusative in ما لزيد وأخاه can be understood through an omitted كان شأن construction, which supplies the verbal relation. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p17 حسبك وزيدا permits the accusative because حسبك means كفاك; speakers supply an implicit verb rather than attach the noun to the hidden pronoun. — SIBAWAYH: حسبك وزيدا permits the accusative because حسبك means كفاك; speakers supply an implicit verb rather than attach the noun to the hidden pronoun. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p18 In ويلا له وأخاه and ويله وأباه, the accusative is licensed by the understood verb ألزمه الله, even though the verb is not stated. — SIBAWAYH: In ويلا له وأخاه and ويله وأباه, the accusative is licensed by the understood verb ألزمه الله, even though the verb is not stated. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p19 هذا لك وأباك cannot take an accusative second noun because the expression contains neither a verb nor a particle with verbal force. — SIBAWAYH: هذا لك وأباك cannot take an accusative second noun because the expression contains neither a verb nor a particle with verbal force. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p20 Khalil analyzes يا أيها الرجل like يا هذا الرجل: الرجل describes هذا. — SIBAWAYH reporting الخليل: Khalil analyzes يا أيها الرجل like يا هذا الرجل: الرجل describes هذا. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH(الخليل)]
- 2:286/q41/p21 If a descriptor that forms one name with a demonstrative is extended by a genitive or coordination, the extension is nominative because it is not itself addressed. — SIBAWAYH: If a descriptor that forms one name with a demonstrative is extended by a genitive or coordination, the extension is nominative because it is not itself addressed. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p22 يا أيها الجاهل ذو التنزى exemplifies a nominative genitive extension to the raised descriptor. — SIBAWAYH: يا أيها الجاهل ذو التنزى exemplifies a nominative genitive extension to the raised descriptor. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p23 In يا أيها الرجل زيد أقبل, زيد takes tanwīn because the added mudaf is in a nominative position; tanwīn is omitted only in the accusative. — SIBAWAYH: In يا أيها الرجل زيد أقبل, زيد takes tanwīn because the added mudaf is in a nominative position; tanwīn is omitted only in the accusative. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p24 In يا زيد الطويل ذو الجمة, ذو is nominative if it describes الطويل, but accusative if attached to Zayd. — SIBAWAYH: In يا زيد الطويل ذو الجمة, ذو is nominative if it describes الطويل, but accusative if attached to Zayd. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p25 ذا الجمة can coordinate to هذا with accusative but not to أي; ذا الرجل can describe أي because both are demonstrative. — SIBAWAYH: ذا الجمة can coordinate to هذا with accusative but not to أي; ذا الرجل can describe أي because both are demonstrative. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p26 Some Arabs say يا هذا زيد is common in Ṭayyiʾ speech. — SIBAWAYH reporting بعض العرب: Some Arabs say يا هذا زيد is common in Ṭayyiʾ speech. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH(بعض العرب)]
- 2:286/q41/p27 In يا زيد الناكي العدو وذا الفضل, ذا الفضل is accusative if attached to Zayd, but can form a new vocative if addressed to someone else. — SIBAWAYH: In يا زيد الناكي العدو وذا الفضل, ذا الفضل is accusative if attached to Zayd, but can form a new vocative if addressed to someone else. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p28 أي needs an explanation and cannot stand alone, so it and الرجل form one name and the descriptor is nominative. — SIBAWAYH: أي needs an explanation and cannot stand alone, so it and الرجل form one name and the descriptor is nominative. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p29 Demonstratives such as هذا، هؤلاء, and أولئك take definite-article descriptions like أي; this differs from a complete proper-name address followed by an afterthought. — SIBAWAYH: Demonstratives such as هذا، هؤلاء, and أولئك take definite-article descriptions like أي; this differs from a complete proper-name address followed by an afterthought. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p30 ذا الجمة after يا هذا is apposition or coordination, not a description; it confirms an already completed address. — SIBAWAYH: ذا الجمة after يا هذا is apposition or coordination, not a description; it confirms an already completed address. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p31 Ibn Lūdhān’s يا صاح ياذا الضامر العنس and Ibn al-Abras’s examples show descriptive phrases following ذا. — SIBAWAYH: Ibn Lūdhān’s يا صاح ياذا الضامر العنس and Ibn al-Abras’s examples show descriptive phrases following ذا. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p32 ذا الجمة identifies ذا, but الضامر and الحسن are not identified by what follows; those phrases explain the respect in which the quality applies. — SIBAWAYH: ذا الجمة identifies ذا, but الضامر and الحسن are not identified by what follows; those phrases explain the respect in which the quality applies. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p33 الحسن is general and الوجه specifies what is beautiful; العنس likewise identifies the respect in which الضامر applies. — SIBAWAYH: الحسن is general and الوجه specifies what is beautiful; العنس likewise identifies the respect in which الضامر applies. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p34 يا هذا الحسن الوجه is remote, since the construction resembles a verb with its object, as in يا هذا الضارب زيدا. — SIBAWAYH: يا هذا الحسن الوجه is remote, since the construction resembles a verb with its object, as in يا هذا الضارب زيدا. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p35 Khalil treats يا زيد الحسن الوجه like يا زيد الحسن; the following phrase does not become a second vocative. — SIBAWAYH reporting الخليل: Khalil treats يا زيد الحسن الوجه like يا زيد الحسن; the following phrase does not become a second vocative. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH(الخليل)]
- 2:286/q41/p36 Non-obligatory negation particles allow a noun before the verb, by analogy with interrogatives and conditionals; the clause after the noun supplies its predicate. — SIBAWAYH: Non-obligatory negation particles allow a noun before the verb, by analogy with interrogatives and conditionals; the clause after the noun supplies its predicate. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p37 Sībawayh prefers analyzing such ليس clauses with an omitted element after ليس and a new topic, as in إنه أمة الله ذاهبة. — SIBAWAYH: Sībawayh prefers analyzing such ليس clauses with an omitted element after ليس and a new topic, as in إنه أمة الله ذاهبة. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH+]
- 2:286/q41/p38 The Qur'anic clause إنا كل شيء خلقناه بقدر is an ordinary Arabic accusative-fronting construction like زيدا ضربته. — SIBAWAYH: The Qur'anic clause إنا كل شيء خلقناه بقدر is an ordinary Arabic accusative-fronting construction like زيدا ضربته. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p39 Some read ثمود accusative in وأما ثمود فهديناهم; Sībawayh says the reading does not conflict with the analysis because transmitted readings are sunna. — SIBAWAYH: Some read ثمود accusative in وأما ثمود فهديناهم; Sībawayh says the reading does not conflict with the analysis because transmitted readings are sunna. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p40 With ما أنا زيد لقيته, accusative زيدا is available only on the analysis that also makes زيدا لقيته accusative; the intervening topic otherwise blocks it. — SIBAWAYH: With ما أنا زيد لقيته, accusative زيدا is available only on the analysis that also makes زيدا لقيته accusative; the intervening topic otherwise blocks it. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p41 After إني, أنا, and ليتني, a fresh nominal or verbal clause can occupy the predicate position after the initial pronoun. — SIBAWAYH: After إني, أنا, and ليتني, a fresh nominal or verbal clause can occupy the predicate position after the initial pronoun. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p42 كان does not govern a later noun as an ordinary verb would; the following clause can serve as its predicate and block separate verbal government. — SIBAWAYH: كان does not govern a later noun as an ordinary verb would; the following clause can serve as its predicate and block separate verbal government. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p43 If كان has already taken its predicate, a following coordinated noun and clause can be accusative; if not, that clause is the predicate instead. — SIBAWAYH: If كان has already taken its predicate, a following coordinated noun and clause can be accusative; if not, that clause is the predicate instead. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p44 The object pronoun in حسبتني requires a predicate just as the name after كنت does; the following clause can supply that predicate. — SIBAWAYH: The object pronoun in حسبتني requires a predicate just as the name after كنت does; the following clause can supply that predicate. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p45 The lām in قد علمت لعبد الله تضربه signals an explanatory predicate rather than coordination; omission of wāw has the same effect. — SIBAWAYH: The lām in قد علمت لعبد الله تضربه signals an explanatory predicate rather than coordination; omission of wāw has the same effect. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p46 Al-Marrār al-Asadī's verse attests an accusative alternative in poetic usage. — SIBAWAYH: Al-Marrār al-Asadī's verse attests an accusative alternative in poetic usage. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p47 The accusative pattern uses a resumptive pronoun in the verb after the fronted noun. — SIBAWAYH: The accusative pattern uses a resumptive pronoun in the verb after the fronted noun. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p48 Hadba ibn al-Khashram's line is cited as poetic evidence for nominal fronting under negation. — SIBAWAYH: Hadba ibn al-Khashram's line is cited as poetic evidence for nominal fronting under negation. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p49 Zuhayr's line supplies another poetic example of a noun placed before the negative clause. — SIBAWAYH: Zuhayr's line supplies another poetic example of a noun placed before the negative clause. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p50 Jarir's line likewise supports the use of a fronted noun with negation. — SIBAWAYH: Jarir's line likewise supports the use of a fronted noun with negation. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p51 Nominative is also possible, and Sībawayh says it is stronger because the clause can begin after negation and be built on the topic. — SIBAWAYH: Nominative is also possible, and Sībawayh says it is stronger because the clause can begin after negation and be built on the topic. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p52 In the Hijazi dialect, ما works like ليس and therefore permits only the nominative construction. — SIBAWAYH: In the Hijazi dialect, ما works like ليس and therefore permits only the nominative construction. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p53 Some recited Muzahim al-Uqayli's line with أنا عارف nominative; Sībawayh allows an analysis on ليس or, more remotely, on the idiom كله لم أصنع. — SIBAWAYH: Some recited Muzahim al-Uqayli's line with أنا عارف nominative; Sībawayh allows an analysis on ليس or, more remotely, on the idiom كله لم أصنع. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p54 A reported view makes ليس govern like ما, but Sībawayh says this usage is rare and scarcely known. — SIBAWAYH: A reported view makes ليس govern like ما, but Sībawayh says this usage is rare and scarcely known. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]
- 2:286/q41/p55 Rules against stopping between syntactically joined elements govern voluntary recitation, not legal permissibility; necessity such as breath, teaching, or testing permits the stop, followed by resuming from earlier words. — NASHR: Rules against stopping between syntactically joined elements govern voluntary recitation, not legal permissibility; necessity such as breath, teaching, or testing permits the stop, followed by resuming from earlier words. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 2:286/q41/p56 Readers should seek the fullest coherent meaning rather than contrived stops produced by grammarians, reciters, or partisan interpreters; the cited examples create misleading syntax or theology. — NASHR: Readers should seek the fullest coherent meaning rather than contrived stops produced by grammarians, reciters, or partisan interpreters; the cited examples create misleading syntax or theology. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 2:286/q41/p57 Waqf may pair parallel clauses even when the first clause has no syntactic attachment to what follows, preserving paired wording such as gain/loss and reciprocal acts. — NASHR: Waqf may pair parallel clauses even when the first clause has no syntactic attachment to what follows, preserving paired wording such as gain/loss and reciprocal acts. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 2:286/q41/p58 All transmitters say Hamza stopped when breath ran out; against the explanation that long, precise recitation caused this, the author says he treated the Quran as one sura and therefore did not select fixed. — NASHR reporting حمزة: All transmitters say Hamza stopped when breath ran out; against the explanation that long, precise recitation caused this, the author says he treated the Quran as one sura and therefore did not select fixed stops. [1 notes: NASHR(حمزة)+]
- 2:286/q41/p59 The remaining readers are reported to attend to the quality of both stopping and resuming. — NASHR reporting أبو الفضل الخزاعي والرازي: The remaining readers are reported to attend to the quality of both stopping and resuming. [1 notes: NASHR(أبو الفضل الخزاعي والرازي)]
- 2:286/q41/p60 Nasir ibn Muhammad and followers among waqf authorities chose this paired-stop practice. — NASHR reporting نصير بن محمد: Nasir ibn Muhammad and followers among waqf authorities chose this paired-stop practice. [1 notes: NASHR(نصير بن محمد)]
- 2:286/q41/p61 Nafi considers the semantic quality of both stopping and resuming, following transmitted practice. — NASHR reporting نافع: Nafi considers the semantic quality of both stopping and resuming, following transmitted practice. [1 notes: NASHR(نافع)]
- 2:286/q41/p62 Abu Amr deliberately stops at verse endings and calls that preferable; al-Khuzai reports that he sought good beginnings, and al-Razi that he sought good stops. — NASHR reporting أبو عمرو: Abu Amr deliberately stops at verse endings and calls that preferable; al-Khuzai reports that he sought good beginnings, and al-Razi that he sought good stops. [1 notes: NASHR(أبو عمرو)]
- 2:286/q41/p63 Al-Razi reports that Asim sought good beginnings, while al-Khuzai says Asim and al-Kisai sought stops where the speech is complete. — NASHR reporting عاصم: Al-Razi reports that Asim sought good beginnings, while al-Khuzai says Asim and al-Kisai sought stops where the speech is complete. [1 notes: NASHR(عاصم)]
- 2:286/q41/p64 Warsh’s Madinan transmitters unanimously exclude يؤاخذ from added alif lengthening wherever it occurs, including this prayer; al-Dani says performance authorities agree, contrary to al-Shatibi’s inference from the Taysir’s omission. — NASHR: Warsh’s Madinan transmitters unanimously exclude يؤاخذ from added alif lengthening wherever it occurs, including this prayer; al-Dani says performance authorities agree, contrary to al-Shatibi’s inference from the Taysir’s omission. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 2:286/q41/p65 The author says the exception needs no separate entry in the Taysir: the form may derive from unhamzated واخذ or its substitution is obligatory, so the alif is not lengthened. — NASHR: The author says the exception needs no separate entry in the Taysir: the form may derive from unhamzated واخذ or its substitution is obligatory, so the alif is not lengthened. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 2:286/q41/p66 Al-Jaʿbari chose the reported option; al-Safrawi and al-Shatibi record three lengths and weaken the long one. The author says a widely received form cannot be rejected though another is preferable. — NASHR: Al-Jaʿbari chose the reported option; al-Safrawi and al-Shatibi record three lengths and weaken the long one. The author says a widely received form cannot be rejected though another is preferable. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 2:286/q41/p67 One of Warsh’s two systematic lengthening rules applies when a hamza follows a consonant sākin in the same word, with examples القرآن, الظمآن, مسئولا, and مذؤما. — NASHR: One of Warsh’s two systematic lengthening rules applies when a hamza follows a consonant sākin in the same word, with examples القرآن, الظمآن, مسئولا, and مذؤما. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 2:286/q41/p68 For a silent consonant between kasra and rāʾ, al-Azraq's lightening is conditional: he thickens after intervening ṣād, ṭāʾ, or qāf; khāʾ in ikhrāj does not block lightening. — NASHR: For a silent consonant between kasra and rāʾ, al-Azraq's lightening is conditional: he thickens after intervening ṣād, ṭāʾ, or qāf; khāʾ in ikhrāj does not block lightening. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 2:286/q41/p69 Ibn al-Jazari says a reader should seek the fullest meaning and soundest pause, not adopt strained stopping or starting to force an interpretation; he gives several such examples, including splitting the final prayer into. — ITQAN reporting ابن الجزري: Ibn al-Jazari says a reader should seek the fullest meaning and soundest pause, not adopt strained stopping or starting to force an interpretation; he gives several such examples, including splitting the final prayer into a vocative. [1 notes: ITQAN(ابن الجزري)]
- 2:286/q41/p70 One view permits pausing before a disconnected exception because it functions like a new nominal sentence whose predicate is understood. — ITQAN reporting الجواز مطلقا: One view permits pausing before a disconnected exception because it functions like a new nominal sentence whose predicate is understood. [1 notes: ITQAN(الجواز مطلقا)]
- 2:286/q41/p71 A second view forbids pausing before a disconnected exception because it depends on the preceding wording and meaning. — ITQAN reporting المنع مطلقا: A second view forbids pausing before a disconnected exception because it depends on the preceding wording and meaning. [1 notes: ITQAN(المنع مطلقا)]
- 2:286/q41/p72 Ibn al-Hajib permits pausing before a disconnected exception only when its predicate is stated, so the clause stands independently; without one it remains dependent. — ITQAN reporting ابن الحاجب: Ibn al-Hajib permits pausing before a disconnected exception only when its predicate is stated, so the clause stands independently; without one it remains dependent. [1 notes: ITQAN(ابن الحاجب)]
- 2:286/q41/p73 Ibn al-Hajib reports from the verifying scholars that pausing after a vocative is permissible because it is self-contained, even if it relates to what follows. — ITQAN reporting ابن الحاجب: Ibn al-Hajib reports from the verifying scholars that pausing after a vocative is permissible because it is self-contained, even if it relates to what follows. [1 notes: ITQAN(ابن الحاجب)]
- 2:286/q41/p74 Rāʾ assimilates into lām in the cited phrases, but not when rāʾ is open after a sākin, as in والحمير لتركبوها. — ITQAN: Rāʾ assimilates into lām in the cited phrases, but not when rāʾ is open after a sākin, as in والحمير لتركبوها. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 2:286/q41/p75 A conditional إن calls for an implied verb before a noun. — SIBAWAYH: A conditional إن calls for an implied verb before a noun. [1 notes: SIBAWAYH]

## 2:286/q42 What other rhetorical observations accompany this verse?  [* · rhetoric]
turns on: The alternatives differ over the wording, scope, or evidence cited in the notes.
- 2:286/q42/p1 Whoever is unconcerned about past sins will not be protected from sin later; without God's protection through the remaining days, one is lost in the afterlife. — TUSTARI: Whoever is unconcerned about past sins will not be protected from sin later; without God's protection through the remaining days, one is lost in the afterlife. [1 notes: TUSTARI]
- 2:286/q42/p2 ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz wrote the same explanation. — IBNABIHATIM reporting عمر بن عبد العزيز: ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz wrote the same explanation. [1 notes: IBNABIHATIM(عمر بن عبد العزيز)]
- 2:286/q42/p3 ʿAṭāʾ says a man unable to provide for family owes his wife only what he has. — IBNABIHATIM reporting عطاء: ʿAṭāʾ says a man unable to provide for family owes his wife only what he has. [1 notes: IBNABIHATIM(عطاء)]
- 2:286/q42/p4 Muqātil b. Ḥayyān gives an alternative gloss: keep us safe from that. — IBNABIHATIM reporting مقاتل بن حيان: Muqātil b. Ḥayyān gives an alternative gloss: keep us safe from that. [1 notes: IBNABIHATIM(مقاتل بن حيان)]
- 2:286/q42/p5 Ata glosses the phrase as ‘if we were ignorant or did it deliberately.’. — THALABI reporting عطاء: Ata glosses the phrase as ‘if we were ignorant or did it deliberately.’ [1 notes: THALABI(عطاء)]
- 2:286/q42/p6 Some theologians understand the phrase as only requiring what is lawful and within a person’s permitted scope. — THALABI reporting بعض أهل الكلام: Some theologians understand the phrase as only requiring what is lawful and within a person’s permitted scope. [1 notes: THALABI(بعض أهل الكلام)]
- 2:286/q42/p7 The clause is read either as God's promise of gracious treatment or as the Prophet and believers praising God for not overburdening anyone. — MAWARDI-FULL: The clause is read either as God's promise of gracious treatment or as the Prophet and believers praising God for not overburdening anyone. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p8 Ibn Zayd takes the burden to mean a sin for which there is neither repentance nor expiation. — MAWARDI-FULL reporting ابن زيد: Ibn Zayd takes the burden to mean a sin for which there is neither repentance nor expiation. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL(ابن زيد)]
- 2:286/q42/p9 Al-Jurjani presents metaphor as the most expansive, inventive, beautiful, and difficult-to-confine rhetorical figure. — JURJANI-ASRAR: Al-Jurjani presents metaphor as the most expansive, inventive, beautiful, and difficult-to-confine rhetorical figure. [1 notes: JURJANI-ASRAR]
- 2:286/q42/p10 Metaphor lets a single word yield many meanings with little wording, and lets one source produce many kinds of insight. — JURJANI-ASRAR: Metaphor lets a single word yield many meanings with little wording, and lets one source produce many kinds of insight. [1 notes: JURJANI-ASRAR]
- 2:286/q42/p11 Metaphor gives life and speech to inanimate things and makes mute bodies expressive. — JURJANI-ASRAR: Metaphor gives life and speech to inanimate things and makes mute bodies expressive. [1 notes: JURJANI-ASRAR]
- 2:286/q42/p12 It can embody subtle meanings hidden in the intellect so that the eyes seem to see them. — JURJANI-ASRAR: It can embody subtle meanings hidden in the intellect so that the eyes seem to see them. [1 notes: JURJANI-ASRAR]
- 2:286/q42/p13 Conversely, metaphor can make bodily qualities so refined that they become spiritual and accessible only to conjecture. — JURJANI-ASRAR: Conversely, metaphor can make bodily qualities so refined that they become spiritual and accessible only to conjecture. [1 notes: JURJANI-ASRAR]
- 2:286/q42/p14 Nuṣra means assistance. — JISHUMI: Nuṣra means assistance. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 2:286/q42/p15 The account of the believers’ faith in this prayer establishes the duty to follow the believers’ example. — JISHUMI: The account of the believers’ faith in this prayer establishes the duty to follow the believers’ example. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 2:286/q42/p16 The plea asks God not to punish the believers. — BAGHAWI-FULL: The plea asks God not to punish the believers. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p17 ʿAta interprets the plea as covering ignorance and deliberate action. — BAGHAWI-FULL reporting عطاء: ʿAta interprets the plea as covering ignorance and deliberate action. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL(عطاء)]
- 2:286/q42/p18 A person may pray for continued preservation of a blessing already received, both to sustain it and acknowledge it as a blessing. — KASHSHAF-FULL: A person may pray for continued preservation of a blessing already received, both to sustain it and acknowledge it as a blessing. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p19 Ibn Masʿud swore at the stoning site that this was the stoning of the person to whom al-Baqara was revealed. — KASHSHAF-FULL reporting عبد الله بن مسعود: Ibn Masʿud swore at the stoning site that this was the stoning of the person to whom al-Baqara was revealed. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL(عبد الله بن مسعود)]
- 2:286/q42/p20 Alternatively, it means duties so difficult they can scarcely be performed. — KASHSHAF-FULL reporting قيل: Alternatively, it means duties so difficult they can scarcely be performed. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL(قيل)]
- 2:286/q42/p21 The petitions are distinct approaches to prayer, though all seek entry into Paradise. — IBNATIYYA-FULL: The petitions are distinct approaches to prayer, though all seek entry into Paradise. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p22 A second explanation, reported from al-Rabīʿ, Mālik, and ʿAṭāʾ, asks God not to make the religious burden too heavy or strict. — TABRISI reporting الربيع ومالك وعطاء: A second explanation, reported from al-Rabīʿ, Mālik, and ʿAṭāʾ, asks God not to make the religious burden too heavy or strict. [1 notes: TABRISI(الربيع ومالك وعطاء)]
- 2:286/q42/p23 Ibn Abbas says that what accrues to a person is obedience and what accrues against them is disobedience. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD reporting ابن عباس: Ibn Abbas says that what accrues to a person is obedience and what accrues against them is disobedience. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن عباس)]
- 2:286/q42/p24 Ibn al-Anbari instead understands the request as asking not to be given a burdensome task, though it could be performed with strain; Arabs say they cannot bear looking at someone they actually can look. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD reporting ابن الأنباري: Ibn al-Anbari instead understands the request as asking not to be given a burdensome task, though it could be performed with strain; Arabs say they cannot bear looking at someone they actually can look at. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن الأنباري)]
- 2:286/q42/p25 The first of five views understands this as asking not to be given deeds that are difficult and arduous. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD reporting الضحاك، السدي، ابن زيد، الجمهور: The first of five views understands this as asking not to be given deeds that are difficult and arduous. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الضحاك، السدي، ابن زيد، الجمهور)]
- 2:286/q42/p26 Incorporating a usurped yard into a building or milling usurped grain does not extinguish the owner’s title. — RAZI-FULL: Incorporating a usurped yard into a building or milling usurped grain does not extinguish the owner’s title. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p27 Al-Razi says the debate over annulment was treated fully under the prohibition on nullifying charity through reproach and injury. — RAZI-FULL: Al-Razi says the debate over annulment was treated fully under the prohibition on nullifying charity through reproach and injury. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p28 The alternative cases, where one or both terms mean an unintentional act, are excluded within the opponent’s premises, since punishment would be wrong and cannot properly be requested. — RAZI-FULL reporting الخصم: The alternative cases, where one or both terms mean an unintentional act, are excluded within the opponent’s premises, since punishment would be wrong and cannot properly be requested. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(الخصم)]
- 2:286/q42/p29 The prayer may be hypothetical: these righteous believers only risked such lapses inadvertently, so they disclaim liability even if inadvertent acts could in principle be punished. — RAZI-FULL: The prayer may be hypothetical: these righteous believers only risked such lapses inadvertently, so they disclaim liability even if inadvertent acts could in principle be punished. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p30 Al-Razi says God removed such burdens and shackles from this community, citing the description at 7:157. — RAZI-FULL: Al-Razi says God removed such burdens and shackles from this community, citing the description at 7:157. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p31 A Prophetic report says the community was spared transformation, landslides, and drowning; al-Razi gives no grading. — RAZI-FULL reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: A Prophetic report says the community was spared transformation, landslides, and drowning; al-Razi gives no grading. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 2:286/q42/p32 A Prophetic report describes his mission as the tolerant, easy monotheistic way; al-Razi gives no grading. — RAZI-FULL reporting النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم: A Prophetic report describes his mission as the tolerant, easy monotheistic way; al-Razi gives no grading. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 2:286/q42/p33 Some theologians reduce these requests to acts of obedience; al-Razi grants this as the limit of their knowledge while retaining the deeper bodily and spiritual interpretation. — RAZI-FULL reporting كثير من المتكلمين: Some theologians reduce these requests to acts of obedience; al-Razi grants this as the limit of their knowledge while retaining the deeper bodily and spiritual interpretation. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(كثير من المتكلمين)]
- 2:286/q42/p34 Abu Hurayra recalls following Jaʿfar ibn Abi Talib while hungry; finding only a trace of clarified butter at home, Jaʿfar shared it with them. — QURTUBI-FULL reporting أبو هريرة: Abu Hurayra recalls following Jaʿfar ibn Abi Talib while hungry; finding only a trace of clarified butter at home, Jaʿfar shared it with them. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو هريرة)]
- 2:286/q42/p35 Ali ibn Abi Talib said he doubted that anyone who understands and has grasped Islam would sleep before reading the final two verses. — QURTUBI-FULL reporting علي بن أبي طالب: Ali ibn Abi Talib said he doubted that anyone who understands and has grasped Islam would sleep before reading the final two verses. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(علي بن أبي طالب)]
- 2:286/q42/p36 Al-Qurtubi calls this report sound and links these verses’ descent with the angel’s descent with al-Fatiha. — QURTUBI-FULL: Al-Qurtubi calls this report sound and links these verses’ descent with the angel’s descent with al-Fatiha. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p37 A sin may lead to punishment even without resolve. — BAYDAWI-FULL: A sin may lead to punishment even without resolve. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p38 A poem praises one who prevents wrongdoing and bears the heavy burden from others. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: A poem praises one who prevents wrongdoing and bears the heavy burden from others. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p39 Grammarians disagree on the verbal noun pattern for hollow verbs with yāʾ: some treat it like a regular verb, some allow two patterns for the noun but kasra for place and time, and others. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: Grammarians disagree on the verbal noun pattern for hollow verbs with yāʾ: some treat it like a regular verb, some allow two patterns for the noun but kasra for place and time, and others follow attested usage; Abu Hayyan calls the last safest. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p40 وإليك المصير acknowledges return to God’s recompense. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: وإليك المصير acknowledges return to God’s recompense. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p41 Ibn ʿAṭiyya says the verbal reading points to an inverted construction whose expected wording is إلا وسعته; Abū Ḥayyān notes analogous usage in verses and the idiom “I put the cap in my head.”. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting ابن عطية: Ibn ʿAṭiyya says the verbal reading points to an inverted construction whose expected wording is إلا وسعته; Abū Ḥayyān notes analogous usage in verses and the idiom “I put the cap in my head.” [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عطية)]
- 2:286/q42/p42 The question of imposing what cannot be borne belongs to theology; the apparent meaning of this verse is that such an imposition does not occur. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The question of imposing what cannot be borne belongs to theology; the apparent meaning of this verse is that such an imposition does not occur. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p43 The basic meaning of مؤاخذة is consequence or requital; here fāʿala may simply mean the basic verb أخذ. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: The basic meaning of مؤاخذة is consequence or requital; here fāʿala may simply mean the basic verb أخذ. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p44 Ubayy reads the verb with gemination and uses the plural form asara. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting أبي: Ubayy reads the verb with gemination and uses the plural form asara. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبي)]
- 2:286/q42/p45 These explanations are examples, not restrictions on the general meaning of the request. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL: These explanations are examples, not restrictions on the general meaning of the request. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p46 Ibn al-Anbari explains the plea as asking not to bear a load that is heavy to discharge even if one could endure it, like saying one cannot bear to look at someone one dislikes. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL reporting ابن الأنباري: Ibn al-Anbari explains the plea as asking not to bear a load that is heavy to discharge even if one could endure it, like saying one cannot bear to look at someone one dislikes. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن الأنباري)]
- 2:286/q42/p47 Al-Zamakhsharī says evil takes the intensive iktasaba because the desirous self exerts effort to reach it, while good is described by the unmarked kasaba because it follows the path. — SAMIN-DURR reporting الزمخشري: Al-Zamakhsharī says evil takes the intensive iktasaba because the desirous self exerts effort to reach it, while good is described by the unmarked kasaba because it follows the path. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR(الزمخشري)]
- 2:286/q42/p48 Others find no difference, noting that the Qurʾān uses both forms for evil: 74:38, 6:164, and 33:58. — SAMIN-DURR: Others find no difference, noting that the Qurʾān uses both forms for evil: 74:38, 6:164, and 33:58. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:286/q42/p49 One view says even a slight good is credited graciously by God, while punishment follows only deliberate striving; the author says this depends on distinguishing the verb forms and finds that distinction strongest. — SAMIN-DURR: One view says even a slight good is credited graciously by God, while punishment follows only deliberate striving; the author says this depends on distinguishing the verb forms and finds that distinction strongest. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR+]
- 2:286/q42/p50 In a report in al-Darimi, the Prophet names Ayat al-Kursi as the greatest verse and says he would choose the end of al-Baqara for his community because it contains every good of this world. — IBNKATHIR-FULL reporting رجل: In a report in al-Darimi, the Prophet names Ayat al-Kursi as the greatest verse and says he would choose the end of al-Baqara for his community because it contains every good of this world and the next. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(رجل)]
- 2:286/q42/p51 After Abu Bakr al-Hudhali mentioned the report to Hasan, Hasan agreed and cited this Qur’anic plea as its basis. — IBNKATHIR-FULL reporting الحسن: After Abu Bakr al-Hudhali mentioned the report to Hasan, Hasan agreed and cited this Qur’anic plea as its basis. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(الحسن)]
- 2:286/q42/p52 The verb akhadha has several senses, including usurpation, as in the report about taking even a handspan of land. — BURHAN: The verb akhadha has several senses, including usurpation, as in the report about taking even a handspan of land. [1 notes: BURHAN]
- 2:286/q42/p53 Akhadha and its derivatives can mean punish or seize in retribution, as in the cited accounts of God taking unjust peoples and the prayer not to be called to account. — BURHAN: Akhadha and its derivatives can mean punish or seize in retribution, as in the cited accounts of God taking unjust peoples and the prayer not to be called to account. [1 notes: BURHAN]
- 2:286/q42/p54 The construction akhadha yafʿalu can mean beginning to do something, like jaʿala yafʿalu and karuba yafʿalu. — BURHAN: The construction akhadha yafʿalu can mean beginning to do something, like jaʿala yafʿalu and karuba yafʿalu. [1 notes: BURHAN]
- 2:286/q42/p55 A mere unfulfilled intention to sin is not counted against a person; different wording elsewhere may reflect the needs of its particular context. — BIQAI-FULL: A mere unfulfilled intention to sin is not counted against a person; different wording elsewhere may reflect the needs of its particular context. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p56 Al-Ḥarālī takes God’s placing this plea on the servants’ tongues as a sign that He accepts it. — BIQAI-FULL reporting الحرالي: Al-Ḥarālī takes God’s placing this plea on the servants’ tongues as a sign that He accepts it. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي)]
- 2:286/q42/p57 The Prophet tells ʿImran ibn Husayn, who had hemorrhoids, to pray standing, then sitting if unable, then on one side if unable to sit. — DURR-FULL reporting رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم: The Prophet tells ʿImran ibn Husayn, who had hemorrhoids, to pray standing, then sitting if unable, then on one side if unable to sit. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 2:286/q42/p58 Among the material granted to Muhammad but not to any earlier prophet are al-Fatiha, Ayat al-Kursi, and the conclusion of al-Baqara. — ITQAN: Among the material granted to Muhammad but not to any earlier prophet are al-Fatiha, Ayat al-Kursi, and the conclusion of al-Baqara. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 2:286/q42/p59 For parallelism, join a clause to its matching counterpart even when it could stand complete and has no grammatical link to what follows. — ITQAN reporting ابن الجزري: For parallelism, join a clause to its matching counterpart even when it could stand complete and has no grammatical link to what follows. [1 notes: ITQAN(ابن الجزري)]
- 2:286/q42/p60 Ja'far lists this Qur'anic clause among expressions used proverbially (irsāl al-mathal). — ITQAN reporting جعفر بن شمس الخلافة: Ja'far lists this Qur'anic clause among expressions used proverbially (irsāl al-mathal). [1 notes: ITQAN(جعفر بن شمس الخلافة)]
- 2:286/q42/p61 This asks relief from punishments that cannot be borne and that follow from prior burdens; describing punishment as loading is figurative, based on its consequences. — ALUSI-FULL: This asks relief from punishments that cannot be borne and that follow from prior burdens; describing punishment as loading is figurative, based on its consequences. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p62 On this reading, both occurrences of “what” may denote good: benefit actually attained and good that was not attained; even blind, fruitless striving wastes effort and may lead to worse harm. — ELMALILI: On this reading, both occurrences of “what” may denote good: benefit actually attained and good that was not attained; even blind, fruitless striving wastes effort and may lead to worse harm. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 2:286/q42/p63 Most exegetes favor the first construal and attach the others to it; Elmalılı calls that first reading the main explanation because it is clear and contains the secrets of divine obligation. — ELMALILI: Most exegetes favor the first construal and attach the others to it; Elmalılı calls that first reading the main explanation because it is clear and contains the secrets of divine obligation. [1 notes: ELMALILI+]
- 2:286/q42/p64 Kasaba and iktasaba are synonymous in Arabic here; the latter is not literally a verb of compliance, and the forms vary to avoid repetition, as Dhu al-Rumma illustrates. — IBNASHUR-FULL: Kasaba and iktasaba are synonymous in Arabic here; the latter is not literally a verb of compliance, and the forms vary to avoid repetition, as Dhu al-Rumma illustrates. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p65 Al-Nabigha's paired forms hamalat and ihtamalat likewise exemplify stylistic variation rather than a fixed good-versus-evil distinction. — IBNASHUR-FULL: Al-Nabigha's paired forms hamalat and ihtamalat likewise exemplify stylistic variation rather than a fixed good-versus-evil distinction. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p66 Carrying is a metaphor for imposing a severe duty that weighs on the soul, preparing the image of a burden. — IBNASHUR-FULL: Carrying is a metaphor for imposing a severe duty that weighs on the soul, preparing the image of a burden. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p67 Muhammad's description as one who removes their burdens supports this reading of the prayer. — IBNASHUR-FULL: Muhammad's description as one who removes their burdens supports this reading of the prayer. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:286/q42/p68 The setting he describes is the Anṣār’s full support for Islam and the emigrants’ sacrifice of family, children, wealth, and homeland to follow the Prophet, making divine praise apt. — TABATABAI: The setting he describes is the Anṣār’s full support for Islam and the emigrants’ sacrifice of family, children, wealth, and homeland to follow the Prophet, making divine praise apt. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 2:286/q42/p69 Their prayer shows that after assent to religion they turn to establishing and spreading it: calling to truth, striving, enjoining right, and uniting peoples to end division, a central purpose of religion. — TABATABAI: Their prayer shows that after assent to religion they turn to establishing and spreading it: calling to truth, striving, enjoining right, and uniting peoples to end division, a central purpose of religion. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 2:286/q42/p70 Al-Baqillani argues that challenge was not limited to the Prophet's contemporaries, that proof reaches people according to their eloquence and is assessed across eloquent speakers collectively, and that later generations are even less capable. — BINTSHATI-IJAZ: Al-Baqillani argues that challenge was not limited to the Prophet's contemporaries, that proof reaches people according to their eloquence and is assessed across eloquent speakers collectively, and that later generations are even less capable than the first. [1 notes: BINTSHATI-IJAZ]
- 2:286/q42/p71 The author notes that al-Baqillani first condemns limiting the challenge to early Arabs, then says in the next paragraph that the challenge applies equally to them and everyone else. — BINTSHATI-IJAZ: The author notes that al-Baqillani first condemns limiting the challenge to early Arabs, then says in the next paragraph that the challenge applies equally to them and everyone else. [1 notes: BINTSHATI-IJAZ]
- 2:286/q42/p72 The author presents the Māturīdī school as drawing support from this verse while opposing the other schools’ accounts of human action. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR reporting Mâtürîdî mezhebi: The author presents the Māturīdī school as drawing support from this verse while opposing the other schools’ accounts of human action. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Mâtürîdî mezhebi)+]
- 2:286/q42/p73 Al-Ashʿarī holds that the human will and power accompany God’s in the act’s occurrence but have no effect. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR reporting İmam Eş‘arî: Al-Ashʿarī holds that the human will and power accompany God’s in the act’s occurrence but have no effect. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(İmam Eş‘arî)]
- 2:286/q42/p74 Salvation in the afterlife has degrees; guarding against misguidance is a demanding, high rank, so each action and chosen word is placed where it fits. — SAMARRAI-ASRAR: Salvation in the afterlife has degrees; guarding against misguidance is a demanding, high rank, so each action and chosen word is placed where it fits. [1 notes: SAMARRAI-ASRAR]
- 2:286/q42/p75 Tadmin lets a verb take another verb’s sense and preposition: “سمع” with “لـ” means respond (“استجاب”), not merely hear; not every hearing entails response. — SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT: Tadmin lets a verb take another verb’s sense and preposition: “سمع” with “لـ” means respond (“استجاب”), not merely hear; not every hearing entails response. [1 notes: SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT]
- 2:286/q42/p76 This asks God not to impose commands or prohibitions too difficult to uphold. — STUDYQURAN reporting R: This asks God not to impose commands or prohibitions too difficult to uphold. [1 notes: STUDYQURAN(R)]
- 2:286/q42/p77 Tirmidhi grades the report hasan and notes another route from Ibn ʿAbbās, as well as a related report from Abū Hurayra. — TIRMIDHI reporting أبو عيسى: Tirmidhi grades the report hasan and notes another route from Ibn ʿAbbās, as well as a related report from Abū Hurayra. [1 notes: TIRMIDHI(أبو عيسى)]
